# OCTOBER TERM 1999, CHUNK 1 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| October 12, 1999 | *Prunty v. Brooks*, No. 99-5316 | Application concerning in forma pauperis status and prospective filing administration |
| October 18, 1999 | *Flippo v. West Virginia*, No. 98-8770 | Summary reversal and remand |

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Application: Dempsey v. Martin, No. 99-5283, October 12, 1999

Acting Justice or Court: The Court. The motion was filed directly with the Court, rather than through a Circuit Justice, so no circuit allotment, referral, or renewal route applies.

Public action: The motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis is denied under Rule 39.8. Dempsey may pay the Rule 38 docketing fee and submit a Rule 33.1 petition by November 2, 1999. For any later petition for certiorari or extraordinary writ in a noncriminal matter, Dempsey must obtain leave to file by submitting a concise certification identifying a nonfrivolous ground. A submission satisfying that screen remains eligible for ordinary in forma pauperis consideration. Criminal-sanction and habeas filings remain outside the prospective condition. `OT_1999CHUNK1.md`

Publicly stated basis: Dempsey accumulated 19 frivolous filings after an earlier Rule 39.8 denial, including repeated abuses of both the certiorari and extraordinary-writ processes. A litigant-specific screen protects the Court’s docket while preserving an avenue for a future nonfrivolous filing regardless of ability to pay. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528007/usrep528007.pdf))

Disclosed vote: Denial of the present in forma pauperis motion, 9-0. Prospective filing screen, 8-1. Supporting the prospective screen: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

Effect on judgment below or existing order: No merits judgment is under review, and the substantive claims in the present petition are not adjudicated. The petition will not proceed on the subsidized docket unless Dempsey satisfies the current filing instructions.

Next procedural stage: Dempsey may pay the required fee and submit a compliant petition by November 2, 1999. Otherwise, the present filing ends without merits review.

Separate writing: Stevens concurred in denying the present frivolous motion but dissented from the standing prefiling screen. He would continue to dispose of later submissions individually rather than impose a prospective filing condition.

### Application: Prunty v. Brooks, No. 99-5316, October 12, 1999

Acting Justice or Court: The Court. The motion was filed directly with the Court, rather than through a Circuit Justice, so no circuit allotment, referral, or renewal route applies.

Public action: The motion for leave to proceed in forma pauperis is denied under Rule 39.8. Prunty may pay the Rule 38 docketing fee and submit a Rule 33.1 petition by November 2, 1999. For any later noncriminal petition for certiorari, Prunty must obtain leave to file by submitting a concise certification identifying a nonfrivolous ground. A submission satisfying that screen remains eligible for ordinary in forma pauperis consideration.

Publicly stated basis: Prunty filed ten frivolous certiorari petitions, including the present petition, despite an earlier Rule 39.8 denial. The prospective condition is confined to the demonstrated category of abuse and does not make payment of a fee the sole route for a future qualifying petition. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528009/usrep528009.pdf))

Disclosed vote: Denial of the present in forma pauperis motion, 9-0. Prospective filing screen, 8-1. Supporting the prospective screen: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens.

Effect on judgment below or existing order: No merits judgment is under review, and the substantive claims in the present petition are not adjudicated. The petition will not proceed on the subsidized docket unless Prunty satisfies the current filing instructions.

Next procedural stage: Prunty may pay the required fee and submit a compliant petition by November 2, 1999. Otherwise, the present filing ends without merits review. The prospective condition does not govern extraordinary writs, criminal matters, habeas matters, or a nonfrivolous certiorari petition satisfying the screen.

Separate writing: Stevens concurred in denying the present frivolous motion but dissented from the standing prefiling screen. He would continue to address later petitions individually.

### Summary Disposition: Flippo v. West Virginia, No. 98-8770, October 18, 1999

Acting Justice or Court: The Court.

Public action: The petition for a writ of certiorari is granted, the operative judgment of the Circuit Court of West Virginia for Fayette County is reversed, and the case is remanded.

Publicly stated basis: The trial court authorized an approximately 16-hour evidentiary search of a secured homicide scene, including the opening of a closed briefcase, on the categorical ground that officers could examine anything found at the crime scene. That ground conflicts directly with *Mincey v. Arizona*. Emergency entry, a prompt search for victims or a killer, and immediate protective activity do not authorize a prolonged evidentiary search after the emergency has ended. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528011/usrep528011.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

Disclosed vote: 9-0.

Effect on judgment below or existing order: The suppression ruling and resulting judgment resting on a general homicide-scene exception are reversed.

Next procedural stage: On remand, the state courts may consider any properly preserved contention concerning consent, another recognized warrant exception, or harmless error.

Controlling proposition: The Fourth Amendment recognizes no general crime-scene or murder-scene exception. Once emergency and protective purposes have ended, an extended evidentiary search and the opening of a closed container require a warrant or an independently recognized exception.

Authority limit: Officers may enter to provide immediate aid, search promptly for victims or a dangerous person, secure the premises, preserve evidence against imminent loss, and seek a warrant.

Doctrinal Effect: *Mincey v. Arizona*: reaffirmed; the seriousness of a homicide does not itself create warrantless evidentiary-search authority.

### Summary Disposition: Texas v. Lesage, No. 98-1111, November 29, 1999

Acting Justice or Court: The Court, per curiam.

Public action: The petition for a writ of certiorari is granted. The Fifth Circuit’s judgment is reversed insofar as it permitted retrospective § 1983 relief based on the 1996-1997 admissions denial despite conclusive same-decision proof, and the case is remanded.

Publicly stated basis: The undisputed proof established that Lesage would have been rejected under a lawful race-neutral process. Under *Mt. Healthy City Board of Education v. Doyle*, that showing defeats liability tied to the discrete adverse decision. A separately live claim challenging an ongoing race-conscious process presents a different injury, the inability to compete on equal terms, but the record does not establish the continued existence or abandonment of such a claim. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528018/usrep528018.pdf))

Disclosed vote: Compensatory relief for the rejection, reversed 9-0. Nominal relief for the alleged use of race in reaching that discrete decision, rejected 8-1. Supporting rejection of nominal relief: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stone. Preservation of independently live prospective and remaining statutory claims for remand, 9-0.

Effect on judgment below or existing order: The Fifth Circuit may not permit retrospective § 1983 liability, whether compensatory or nominal, for the discrete rejection once the State conclusively establishes that the same decision would have occurred lawfully. The status of the §§ 1981 and 2000d claims, and any independently live prospective claim, remains for the lower courts.

Next procedural stage: The Fifth Circuit shall remand for entry of judgment on the retrospective § 1983 claim and for resolution of any properly preserved statutory or prospective claim that remains live.

Separate writing: Stone concurred in reversing compensatory relief but dissented from rejection of nominal damages. He would permit nominal relief if Lesage proves that an impermissible racial classification actually governed the decisional process, because same-decision proof limits outcome-based compensation but does not erase the completed process violation.

Controlling proposition: Conclusive proof that the government would have made the same discrete decision under a lawful process defeats retrospective § 1983 liability for that decision, including nominal damages.

Authority limit: The decision neither validates the admissions policy nor determines that race was used unlawfully. It does not govern a plaintiff who establishes ongoing unequal competition and seeks properly supported prospective relief.

Doctrinal Effect: *Mt. Healthy City Board of Education v. Doyle*: applied; the same-decision rule governs a discrete equal-protection challenge as well as a retaliation claim.

Doctrinal Effect: *Carey v. Piphus*: distinguished; nominal damages do not follow when the governing same-decision rule defeats liability for the challenged discrete governmental decision.

### Certification: Fiore v. White, No. 98-942, November 30, 1999

Acting Justice or Court: The Court, in an opinion by Breyer.

Public action: The Court certifies the following question to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania: Whether the interpretation of 35 Pa. Stat. § 6018.401(a) stated in *Commonwealth v. Scarpone* was the correct interpretation of Pennsylvania law when Fiore’s conviction became final.

Publicly stated basis: The federal due-process question depends on whether Pennsylvania law always required proof that a permit holder deviated from the permit in a manner falling within the charged statutory prohibition, or whether *Scarpone* later changed the governing law. The State’s highest court must authoritatively fix that antecedent historical premise before this Court determines whether Fiore was convicted without proof of conduct the statute criminalized. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/23/))

Disclosed vote: 9-0.

Effect on judgment below or existing order: No final Supreme Court judgment is entered. The Third Circuit’s judgment remains under review, and the federal due-process issue is reserved.

Next procedural stage: The Clerk shall transmit the certified question, the briefs, and the relevant record to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania. This Court retains jurisdiction pending the response.

### Los Angeles Police Department v. United Reporting Publishing Corp.

Argued October 13, 1999 | Decided December 7, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
facial First Amendment judgment and permanent injunction affirmed, 146 F.3d 1133 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528032/usrep528032.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; commercial speech; public records

Questions Presented:

1. Whether California’s use-based restriction on access to government-held arrestee addresses is facially invalid under the First Amendment.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor in full; Scalia as to the facial-adjudication and no-general-access holdings; Souter in full; Thomas as to those holdings; Ginsburg and Breyer in full | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; facial-adjudication and no-general-access portions for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence | Scalia | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Agrees that the facial injunction cannot stand and leaves the as-applied question open, but does not join the Court’s reliance on *Lanphere* |
| Dissent | Stevens | Kennedy | Would affirm | Applies *Lanphere* to the statutory use classification and regards the existing record as sufficient for relief |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: Court author.

Announced ground: California has no general constitutional duty to disclose executive arrestee-address records, but an actual denial based solely on intended truthful and lawful commercial solicitation remains subject to *Lanphere*. The undeveloped as-applied record does not support the permanent facial injunction entered below.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Facial invalidation of the use-based access provision

Holding and operative rule: The First Amendment creates no general right to inspect executive criminal-justice records, and the permanent facial injunction cannot stand on the record and posture presented. An actual categorical denial of otherwise available records based solely on intended truthful and lawful commercial solicitation remains governed by *Lanphere*’s substantial-interest, material-advancement, and reasonable-fit requirements. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: California could close the relevant executive records through a neutral nondisclosure rule without violating a general First Amendment access right. It instead created an access regime that makes intended use of the information material to eligibility. Simulated *Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado* therefore forecloses the proposition that an actual use-based denial is categorically outside First Amendment review. The judgment under review, however, is a permanent facial injunction. United Reporting did not submit the statutory declaration, and the lower courts did not adjudicate a developed as-applied record distinguishing privacy interests, demonstrated solicitation harms, available safeguards, and the operation of the particular denial. Facial overbreadth remains an exceptional form of adjudication and does not permit invalidation of the entire provision on this posture. The injunction must be vacated, while any properly preserved as-applied claim remains open under *Lanphere*. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528032/usrep528032.pdf)) `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The facial judgment fails because the statute regulates access to government-held information and creates no overbreadth concern sufficient to support third-party facial adjudication. He would leave reconsideration or application of *Lanphere* to a case requiring resolution of a properly developed as-applied claim.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Kennedy: California grants and withholds the same publicly releasable information according to the requester’s intended speech use. Because *Lanphere* already subjects that distinction to commercial-speech review, they would hold the present denial insufficiently justified and affirm the injunction.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lanphere & Urbaniak v. Colorado*: followed; an actual denial based solely on intended truthful and lawful commercial solicitation remains subject to commercial-speech review even though no general constitutional access right exists.

*New York v. Ferber*: applied; facial overbreadth adjudication remains exceptional and requires a posture supporting relief beyond the claimant’s own application.

*Houchins v. KQED, Inc.*: followed; the desire to speak with executive-branch information does not itself compel government disclosure.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, the permanent facial injunction is vacated, and the case is remanded for consideration of any properly presented and preserved as-applied or alternative claim under *Lanphere*.

Effect: California may employ neutral nondisclosure, confidentiality, redaction, delayed-release, fee, and qualification rules. It may regulate false, deceptive, coercive, or harassing solicitation directly. An actual denial based solely on intended truthful and lawful commercial solicitation is not categorically valid and must satisfy the governing commercial-speech standard.

### Drye v. United States

Argued November 8, 1999 | Decided December 7, 1999  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below:  
federal tax lien held to reach Drye’s state-created inheritance interest, 152 F.3d 892 (8th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528049/usrep528049.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Federal taxation; federal tax liens

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a federal tax lien reaches a taxpayer’s state-created right to accept an inheritance or redirect it through a disclaimer.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Inheritance-control interest under § 6321

Holding and operative rule: A taxpayer’s state-law power to accept an inheritance or redirect it to another through a disclaimer is “property” or a “right to property” reached by a preexisting federal tax lien under 26 U.S.C. § 6321.

Reasoning: State law defines the legal interests the taxpayer possesses, but federal law determines whether those interests constitute property or rights to property for purposes of the federal lien. At his mother’s death, Drye acquired a valuable and legally protected power to take the estate for himself or direct it to the next recipient. That control had pecuniary value and determined who would receive the property. Arkansas’s relation-back rule could govern state inheritance consequences, but it could not erase the control Drye actually acquired or bind the federal classification of that control. Once the state-created interest satisfies § 6321, a state exemption or disclaimer label cannot prevent attachment of the federal lien. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528049/usrep528049.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Bess*: followed; state law defines the taxpayer’s interests, while federal law determines whether those interests fall within the federal tax-lien statute.

*United States v. National Bank of Commerce*: applied; § 6321 reaches legally protected, transferable, and economically valuable control.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The federal tax liens attach to the inheritance interest Drye controlled notwithstanding his later disclaimer. Any remaining accounting, foreclosure, levy, or distribution administration proceeds under the judgment below. The decision does not govern a disclaimer completed before the taxpayer acquires legally cognizable control.

### Kimel v. Florida Board of Regents; United States v. Florida Board of Regents

Argued October 13, 1999 | Decided January 11, 2000  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgments below:  
ADEA claims dismissed or directed to be dismissed on sovereign-immunity grounds, 139 F.3d 1426 (11th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528062/usrep528062.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federalism; state sovereign immunity; ADEA

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the ADEA unmistakably expresses Congress’s intent to authorize private suits against nonconsenting state employers.
2. Whether the ADEA’s extension to state employers is valid legislation under § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment sufficient to support private retrospective monetary relief against the States.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone in full; Stevens as to the clear-statement holding; Scalia as to the clear-statement and § 5 holdings; Kennedy as to the § 5 and remedial-channel holdings; Souter as to the clear-statement and remedial-channel holdings; Thomas as to the § 5 holding; Ginsburg as to the clear-statement and remedial-channel holdings; Breyer as to the clear-statement holding | Supports vacatur and remand | Issue-specific joins as stated |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stevens | Souter and Ginsburg as to the § 5 analysis; Breyer in full | Supports vacatur and remand but dissents from the Court’s § 5 holding | Would sustain § 5 abrogation and, alternatively, permit the Article I monetary action without a state-channel prerequisite |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | Scalia as to the § 5 and remedial-immunity portions; Kennedy as to the clear-statement portion | Would affirm | Rejects the clear statement for Thomas and Kennedy; agrees that § 5 abrogation is invalid for Thomas and Scalia; rejects the *Alden* remedial channel and fallback for Thomas and Scalia |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s opinion, which holds that the ADEA clearly binds state employers, rejects § 5 abrogation for retrospective state-treasury liability, and applies the simulated *Alden* remedial-channel rule to the surviving Article I duty.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Clear statement of abrogation

Holding and operative rule: The ADEA unmistakably identifies States as covered employers and authorizes private monetary enforcement against them. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: The ADEA incorporates the Fair Labor Standards Act’s enforcement provisions, authorizes employees to maintain actions against an employer, and defines covered public agencies to include state governments and their agencies. Read together, those provisions clearly identify States as defendants and make the same monetary enforcement machinery available against them. The clear-statement inquiry asks what Congress enacted, not whether Congress placed every operative phrase in a single section or expressly named the Eleventh Amendment. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528062/usrep528062.pdf))

##### Question 2: Section 5 validity and the surviving Article I remedy

Holding and operative rule: The ADEA’s state-employer extension is not congruent and proportional § 5 legislation for retrospective state-treasury liability because age classifications ordinarily receive rational-basis review and the Act reaches substantially more conduct than the Equal Protection Clause forbids. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas) The ADEA nevertheless remains valid Commerce Clause regulation binding state employers. Under simulated *Alden v. Maine*, a nonconsenting State may channel a retrospective monetary claim into an adequate equivalent state remedial system, but if no adequate substitute exists, the clearly authorized federal action remains available. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg)

Reasoning: Congress may use § 5 to prevent and remedy constitutional violations, but it may not convert every statutory violation into a constitutional one. Because age classifications are generally constitutional when rationally related to a legitimate governmental purpose, the ADEA prohibits a broad range of state employment decisions that the Fourteenth Amendment permits. Its ordinary private damages remedy therefore cannot rest on § 5 as a categorical abrogation of immunity. That conclusion does not erase the substantive federal obligation. The ADEA remains valid Commerce Clause regulation, clearly binds state employers, and clearly creates a private monetary remedy. Simulated *Alden* permits the State to select an adequate state forum and remedial mechanism for a retrospective treasury claim, provided that route offers neutral adjudication, reasonable access and timeliness, substantially full compensation, and an enforceable payment obligation. If no adequate substitute exists, the federal fallback applies. No age-discrimination merits issue is decided. `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Breyer in full and by Souter and Ginsburg as to § 5: The ADEA is valid § 5 enforcement legislation because Congress could prohibit age discrimination more broadly than the judiciary’s direct equal-protection rule. Even apart from § 5, Stevens and Breyer would permit the clearly authorized federal Article I action without first allowing the State to substitute its own forum.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Kennedy as to the clear-statement issue and by Scalia as to the § 5 and remedial-immunity issues: Thomas would find the ADEA’s incorporated language insufficiently clear, while Thomas and Scalia agree that § 5 does not support the action and would reject the simulated *Alden* channel-and-fallback rule. They would affirm the categorical dismissal.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*City of Boerne v. Flores*: applied; the ADEA’s broad regulation of rational age classifications is not congruent and proportional § 5 legislation for retrospective state-treasury liability.

*Alden v. Maine*: followed; failure of the § 5 abrogation route does not extinguish a valid and clearly enacted Article I monetary obligation, which remains subject to adequate state channeling and the federal fallback.

*Seminole Tribe of Florida v. Florida*: clarified; its source and clarity requirements do not authorize extinction of the substantive Article I duty or displace the later remedial rule established in *Alden*.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is vacated, and the consolidated cases are remanded for determination whether the States provide adequate equivalent remedial forums for the ADEA claims and, if not, for application of the clearly authorized federal fallback.

Effect: The categorical immunity dismissal cannot stand. A State providing an adequate equivalent system may require its use for retrospective monetary relief. A State providing no adequate substitute remains subject to the federal action, while properly framed prospective officer relief remains separately available. The decision makes no finding that any state employer violated the ADEA.

### Illinois v. Wardlow

Argued November 2, 1999 | Decided January 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Illinois; judgment below:  
suppression sustained, 183 Ill. 2d 306, 701 N.E.2d 484 (1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528119/usrep528119.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; investigatory stops

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the supported record supplied reasonable suspicion for the initial stop after Wardlow fled upon seeing police in an area described as high crime.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Would find reasonable suspicion from unprovoked flight in the supported high-crime setting |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of *Terry* to the complete pre-stop record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Particularized suspicion from location and flight

Holding and operative rule: Considering the complete supported record, Wardlow’s flight upon seeing police in an area associated with narcotics trafficking did not supply the objective and particularized suspicion required for a *Terry* stop.

Reasoning: *Terry* requires the whole encounter to be assessed, and neither flight nor the character of the location is irrelevant. A demonstrated connection between a place and ongoing criminal activity may provide context, while evasive conduct may add weight. Those considerations do not receive predetermined or conclusive force. The location evidence here described the area generally rather than connecting Wardlow to a particular transaction, report, person, object, or observed offense. His flight was relevant but remained compatible with motives other than participation in criminal activity. Taken together, the circumstances did not provide the minimal objective and particularized basis necessary to seize Wardlow. The Court does not adopt a categorical requirement that some additional fixed factor always be present. It holds only that the supported circumstances in this record were insufficient. Because the initial stop was unlawful, the Court does not separately reach the justification or scope of the frisk. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528119/usrep528119.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Unprovoked headlong flight is strongly evasive conduct, and the area’s demonstrated connection to narcotics trafficking supplies relevant context. Under *Terry*’s practical totality inquiry, the officers were entitled to stop Wardlow briefly to resolve the ambiguity.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Terry v. Ohio*: applied; generalized location evidence and equivocal flight did not establish reasonable suspicion on this record, although both remain relevant considerations.

*Ornelas v. United States*: followed; the ultimate reasonable-suspicion determination receives independent appellate review while supported historical facts remain undisturbed.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Illinois sustaining suppression is affirmed.

Effect: The firearm remains suppressed, and the state judgment based on the unlawful initial stop remains in force. Officers may continue to consider flight and location as part of the totality, but neither receives automatic weight or replaces the requirement of objective, particularized suspicion.

### Friends of the Earth, Inc. v. Laidlaw Environmental Services (TOC), Inc.

Argued October 12, 1999 | Decided January 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
civil-penalty judgment vacated as moot, 149 F.3d 303 (4th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528167/usrep528167.pdf))  
Area of Law: Article III; environmental law; mootness

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the environmental organizations had standing to seek civil penalties for violations ongoing at filing and whether later compliance and facility closure mooted the case.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Treats the entered civil-penalty judgment as independently surviving postjudgment compliance |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Reserves Article II questions concerning private enforcement of public penalties |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Rejects injury, deterrent redressability, and the Court’s mootness analysis |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s standing, deterrent-redressability, and voluntary-cessation analysis.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Standing, civil-penalty redressability, and mootness

Holding and operative rule: Concrete curtailment of recreational and aesthetic use establishes injury in fact; civil penalties may redress violations ongoing at filing through deterrence; and a defendant’s voluntary cessation moots the controversy only when recurrence cannot reasonably be expected.

Reasoning: Members of the plaintiff organizations curtailed their recreational and aesthetic use of the affected waterway because of reasonable concerns connected to Laidlaw’s permit violations. That is a concrete use injury, not a generalized demand for statutory compliance. Civil penalties payable to the Treasury can redress that injury when violations are ongoing at filing because penalties remove the economic advantage of delay and deter recurrence. *Steel Co.* involved completed compliance before suit and a wholly past informational injury; it does not govern a suit filed while the challenged violations remained ongoing. Later compliance and closure bear on the continuing controversy, but voluntary cessation places a demanding burden on the party asserting mootness. The record does not establish that recurrence became impossible or that every effective form of relief disappeared. The Fourth Circuit therefore erred by vacating the penalty judgment categorically as moot. Penalty amount, facility closure, fees, and remaining statutory issues require ordinary appellate and record-based consideration. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528167/usrep528167.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: A valid civil-penalty judgment cannot be retroactively nullified by the losing party’s voluntary postjudgment conduct, even if later events eliminate the need for prospective relief. He would preserve the judgment on that additional ground.

Kennedy: The case does not adequately present whether private pursuit of public civil penalties raises an Article II problem concerning executive enforcement authority. He reserves that issue for a case in which it is distinctly presented and briefed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The affidavits do not establish a sufficiently concrete injury, and a penalty paid to the Treasury does not redress private recreational or aesthetic harm. They would also hold that later compliance and closure eliminated any continuing controversy. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528167/usrep528167.pdf))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lujan v. Defenders of Wildlife*: applied; concrete curtailment of a member’s actual recreational or aesthetic use satisfies injury in fact.

*Steel Co. v. Citizens for a Better Environment*: distinguished; Treasury penalties may supply deterrent redress when violations were ongoing at filing and the claimant’s concrete injury has a continuing or reasonably recurring connection to those violations.

Voluntary-cessation doctrine: reaffirmed; later compliance moots a case only when the defendant establishes that the challenged conduct cannot reasonably be expected to recur.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, the District Court’s civil-penalty judgment is restored as operative pending ordinary appellate review, and the case is remanded for consideration of the penalty amount, facility closure, mootness, fees, and remaining statutory questions.

Effect: The organizations may continue to defend and seek review of the civil-penalty judgment without proving that Laidlaw’s violations produced completed ecological damage. Penalties cannot support standing for wholly past violations lacking a continuing or reasonably recurring remedial connection, and facility closure remains relevant without being automatically dispositive.

---

**Sources:**

- [U.S. Reports: Dempsey v. Martin, District Attorney for Suffolk County, 528 U.S. 7 (1999).](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528007/usrep528007.pdf)
- [Fiore v. White | 528 U.S. 23 (1999) | Justia U.S. Supreme Court Center](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/528/23/)
