## Decisions and Dispositions

### Reno v. Condon

Argued November 10, 1999 | Decided January 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: permanent injunction against enforcement of the Driver's Privacy Protection Act affirmed, 155 F.3d 453 (4th Cir. 1998).   
Area of Law: Federalism; Commerce Clause; anti-commandeering

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress may regulate the disclosure and interstate dissemination of personal information held by state motor-vehicle departments without exceeding the Commerce Clause or commandeering state government.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Congress may regulate state motor-vehicle information as an article of interstate commerce, and the Act directly regulates States as owners and suppliers of data rather than commanding them to legislate or administer federal law against others.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Federal regulation of state-held motor-vehicle information

Holding and operative rule: Congress may regulate the sale, disclosure, resale, and redisclosure of personal information in state motor-vehicle records as commerce. A federal law directly regulating States in their capacity as database owners and information suppliers does not commandeer state government when it requires neither state legislation nor state enforcement against private parties.

Reasoning: State motor-vehicle departments collect, maintain, sell, and disclose personal information that moves through an interstate market, while private recipients resell and redisclose the same data. That information is a thing in interstate commerce within the categories recognized in *United States v. Lopez*, decided April 26, 1995. The Driver's Privacy Protection Act regulates the dissemination transaction itself and reaches both the States as initial suppliers and private actors as later suppliers. It does not require a State to enact a code, investigate private parties, or execute a federal program in the sense prohibited by *Printz v. United States*, decided June 27, 1997. Administrative steps necessary for a regulated entity to comply with a direct federal rule do not convert regulation into commandeering. The facial injunction therefore rests on an erroneous extension of anti-commandeering into immunity from otherwise valid federal regulation of a State's own commercial activity.  `OT_1999CHUNK2.md` `HOLDINGS.md`

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Lopez*: applied; personal information supplied and disseminated through an interstate market is a thing in commerce subject to federal regulation.

*Printz v. United States*: clarified; anti-commandeering bars compelled state administration of federal law, not direct regulation of a State's own activity as a data supplier.

*South Carolina v. Baker*: followed; generally applicable federal regulation may reach state activity without requiring state legislation or enforcement against third parties.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, the permanent injunction shall be dissolved, and judgment shall be entered for the United States on the facial federalism challenge.  
Effect: State motor-vehicle departments and private recipients remain subject to the Act's disclosure restrictions. The decision does not authorize Congress to command state legislation, conscript state officers to enforce federal law against others, or regulate noneconomic state activity without an enumerated-power foundation.

### Summary Disposition: Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Slater, No. 99-295, January 12, 2000

Acting Justice or Court: The Court.  
Public action: The petition for a writ of certiorari is granted, the judgment of the United States Court of Appeals for the Tenth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded.  
Publicly stated basis: Respondents bear the heavy burden of establishing mootness. Colorado's postfiling certification did not conclusively end Adarand's threatened injury because federal acceptance, the certification's legal validity, its exposure to challenge, and its continuing force remained uncertain. The Tenth Circuit improperly shifted that burden to Adarand.   
Disclosed vote: 9-0.  
Effect on judgment below or existing order: The mootness dismissal and vacatur of the District Court's judgment are reversed; the merits remain open.  
Next procedural stage: The Tenth Circuit shall resume the appeal and resolve the preserved merits and remedial questions.

Controlling proposition: A postfiling certification does not moot a contracting challenge while its acceptance and continuing validity remain uncertain and renewed injury remains reasonably possible.

Authority limit: The Court decides only mootness and expresses no view on the contracting program's constitutional validity.

Doctrinal Effect: Voluntary-cessation doctrine: applied; uncertain postfiling relief does not satisfy the burden required to extinguish a live controversy.

### Weeks v. Angelone

Argued December 6, 1999 | Decided January 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief denied and petition dismissed, 176 F.3d 249 (4th Cir. 1999).   
Area of Law: Capital sentencing; federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a capital jury's unresolved question about the continuing availability of a life sentence after finding an aggravating circumstance establishes constitutional error supporting federal habeas relief.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O'Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Express jury confusion over life-or-death discretion

Holding and operative rule: When a capital jury asks whether proof of an aggravating circumstance makes death mandatory, the trial court must respond with sufficient clarity that life remains an authorized sentence and that mitigating evidence may justify it. Referring the jury back to language that does not unambiguously resolve its demonstrated confusion violates the constitutional requirement of an individualized capital-selection decision.

Reasoning: A general presumption that jurors understand and follow instructions cannot do the work ordinarily assigned to it once the jury identifies the precise point it does not understand. The question here distinguished eligibility from selection and asked whether the aggravating finding left the jury any lawful choice. The court repeated a paragraph whose structure had produced that uncertainty rather than directly confirming that the jury could impose life after finding an aggravator. A capital sentencer may not treat eligibility as a command to select death or fail to give effect to mitigation. The state court's approval of the nonanswer was an unreasonable application of that clearly established principle. Because the question went to the jury's ultimate sentencing authority and the jury returned death, the complete record leaves at least grave doubt under *Brecht v. Abrahamson*, 507 U.S. 619 (1993), and *O'Neal v. McAninch*, decided February 21, 1995, that the error lacked a substantial and injurious effect. Federal habeas relief is therefore required. 

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O'Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The referenced paragraph stated that the jury may impose death after finding an aggravator and shall impose life if death is not justified by all the evidence. The state court reasonably applied the governing precedents, and the jury's continued deliberation and verdict do not justify federal habeas relief.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Buchanan v. Angelone*: distinguished; the facial sufficiency of a general capital instruction does not resolve a later question showing that the jury remains uncertain whether life is legally available.

*Eddings v. Oklahoma*: applied; a capital jury must remain able to give mitigating evidence effect in the selection between life and death.

*Brecht v. Abrahamson*: applied; the capital-instruction error requires relief when it had a substantial and injurious effect or leaves the reviewing court in grave doubt.

*O'Neal v. McAninch*: applied; grave doubt concerning the effect of an unresolved life-or-death instruction error requires federal habeas relief.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to grant a conditional writ unless Virginia conducts a constitutionally adequate new penalty proceeding within a reasonable period or imposes a lawful noncapital sentence.  
Effect: Weeks's conviction remains intact. The ruling requires no particular wording and does not invalidate a reference to existing instructions when that reference clearly answers the jury's actual question.

### Gutierrez v. Ada

Argued December 6, 1999 | Decided January 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: runoff order affirmed, 179 F.3d 672 (9th Cir. 1999).   
Area of Law: Elections; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Guam Organic Act counts all general-election ballots or only votes cast for gubernatorial slates when determining whether a slate received a majority.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Majority denominator under the Guam Organic Act

Holding and operative rule: The statutory majority is calculated from votes cast for gubernatorial slates, not from every ballot submitted in the simultaneous general election. A slate receiving more than half of the votes cast for Governor and Lieutenant Governor has won without a runoff.

Reasoning: Section 1422 repeatedly speaks about the election of the Governor and Lieutenant Governor, and the phrase governing a majority appears within that office-specific sequence. In the same provision, Congress used general-election language when it meant the broader event and used votes to describe expressions of choice for the gubernatorial slate. Treating blank, overmarked, or otherwise nonresponsive ballots as votes against every slate would replace the enacted office-specific denominator with total ballot turnout and make gubernatorial election needlessly difficult. The certified slate received a majority of the votes cast for that office, so the Ninth Circuit's runoff order cannot stand. 

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Guam Organic Act § 1422: clarified; votes cast in the gubernatorial election, rather than all ballots submitted in the general election, supply the majority denominator.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to set aside the runoff order and recognize the certified gubernatorial slate.  
Effect: The certified slate remains elected on the stated returns. The decision does not govern materially different statutory language or disputes concerning fraud, invalid votes, or the accuracy of the count.

### Smith v. Robbins

Argued October 5, 1999 | Decided January 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: conditional habeas relief on the appellate-counsel claim affirmed and remaining exhausted trial-error claims remanded, 152 F.3d 1062 (9th Cir. 1998).   
Area of Law: Criminal appeals; right to counsel; federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. Whether California may employ a procedure different from *Anders* for appointed counsel who finds no nonfrivolous appellate issue.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Adequate advocacy under an alternative to Anders

Holding and operative rule: A State may use a procedure different from the precise *Anders* format, but it may not permit appointed appellate counsel to withhold every issue that might arguably support the appeal and shift the issue-spotting function to the court or the indigent appellant. California's *Wende* procedure therefore fails to provide the meaningful adversarial representation required on a first appeal as of right.

Reasoning: *Douglas v. California*, 372 U.S. 353 (1963), and *Evitts v. Lucey*, 469 U.S. 387 (1985), require more than the formal presence of a lawyer; they require the assistance of an advocate in the first appeal the State provides. *Anders v. California*, 386 U.S. 738 (1967), *McCoy v. Court of Appeals of Wisconsin*, 486 U.S. 429 (1988), and *Penson v. Ohio*, 488 U.S. 75 (1988), do not constitutionalize one filing label or withdrawal mechanism, but they insist that counsel's professional review supply the appellate court with a focused, adversarial basis for deciding that an appeal is frivolous. Under *Wende*, counsel may summarize the record while identifying no possible appellate issue, leaving the court to search from the beginning and the defendant to proceed pro se if able. Independent judicial review is an important safeguard, not a substitute for counsel's advocate function. Because the procedure itself permits that substitution and the courts below identified issues that counsel should have brought to adversarial attention, the Ninth Circuit's judgment is affirmed. 

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by O'Connor, Scalia, and Kennedy: *Anders v. California*, 386 U.S. 738 (1967), provides one constitutionally acceptable method, not the exclusive form of appellate screening. *Wende* retains counsel, requires a trained review and record summary, permits a pro se submission, and directs independent judicial examination; any failure to identify a genuinely nonfrivolous issue should be tested as an ordinary ineffective-assistance claim.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Anders v. California*: clarified; its exact mechanics are not exclusive, but a constitutionally equivalent procedure must preserve counsel's active advocate role and focused identification of potentially supportive issues.

*Penson v. Ohio*: followed; judicial screening cannot replace representation during the appellate decisional process.

*Douglas v. California* and *Evitts v. Lucey*: applied; meaningful counsel is required on the first appeal as of right.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed, including its remand for further proceedings on Robbins's remaining exhausted habeas claims.  
Effect: California may adopt an alternative to *Anders* that provides genuinely equivalent advocacy, but the *Wende* procedure may not govern Robbins's appeal. The Court expresses no view on the merits of his remaining trial-error claims.

### United States v. Martinez-Salazar

Argued November 29, 1999 | Decided January 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: conviction reversed because an allotted peremptory challenge was used to cure an erroneous for-cause ruling, 146 F.3d 653 (9th Cir. 1998).   
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; jury selection

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a defendant is denied a constitutional or Rule-based right when he uses an allotted peremptory challenge to remove a juror who should have been excused for cause and an impartial jury is ultimately seated.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Curative use of an allotted peremptory challenge

Holding and operative rule: A defendant who preserves the for-cause objection, uses an allotted peremptory challenge to remove the juror, receives the full number of challenges prescribed by Rule 24, and is tried by an impartial jury suffers no constitutional or Rule 24 deprivation merely because the remaining challenges are exhausted.

Reasoning: Peremptory challenges are an important statutory aid to selecting an impartial jury, but they are not themselves guaranteed by the Constitution. Martinez-Salazar received and exercised the complete allotment provided by Rule 24. His decision to use one challenge to remove a juror who should have been excused for cause was difficult, but it was an exercise of the challenge, not its denial. The Court does not approve the trial court's for-cause error and does not impose a duty to cure such an error by peremptory strike. The decisive facts are that no biased juror sat and no prescribed challenge was withheld. The Ninth Circuit therefore erred in treating the curative use as an automatic Fifth Amendment violation. 

#### Concurrences

Souter: The Court does not decide whether a defendant who uses a challenge curatively and identifies another juror he otherwise would have struck is entitled to an additional challenge or what remedy would follow from a substantial impairment.

Scalia, joined by Kennedy: The judgment follows because Martinez-Salazar received the full allotment and voluntarily used one challenge on the objectionable juror. They would not address whether a defendant who leaves a challenged juror seated may later obtain reversal.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Ross v. Oklahoma*: followed and clarified; an impartial seated jury and receipt of the full prescribed allotment defeat a due-process claim based solely on curative use of a peremptory challenge.

Federal Rule of Criminal Procedure 24: clarified; the Rule guarantees the stated number of challenges, not a guarantee that none will be used to correct a for-cause error.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and Martinez-Salazar's conviction remains undisturbed as against this jury-selection claim.  
Effect: The decision does not govern a case involving a biased seated juror, denial of the prescribed allotment, deliberate manipulation of for-cause rulings, or an independent *Batson* or *J.E.B.* violation.

### Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board

Argued April 26, 1999; reargued October 6, 1999 | Decided January 24, 2000  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the District of Columbia; judgment below: preclearance of the 1992 school-board redistricting plan granted, 7 F. Supp. 2d 29 (D.D.C. 1998).   
Area of Law: Voting Rights Act; voting rights; Article III

Questions Presented:

1. Whether anticipated redistricting after the 2000 census moots the dispute over preclearance of the 1992 plan.
2. Whether a nonretrogressive plan violates § 5's effects prong because it preserves diluted Black voting strength or because a more favorable plan was available.
3. Whether § 5's purpose prong is limited to intent to cause retrogression or also reaches a discriminatory racial purpose to deny or abridge voting rights when the change is nonretrogressive.
4. Whether the Board proved the plan free of prohibited purpose or established that independently sufficient lawful reasons would have produced the identical plan.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens as to mootness, effects, discriminatory purpose, record application, and the judgment; O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy as to mootness, effects, and the same-decision rule; Souter as to mootness, effects, discriminatory purpose, record application, and the judgment; Thomas as to mootness, effects, and the same-decision rule; Ginsburg and Breyer as to mootness, effects, discriminatory purpose, record application, and the judgment | Supports reversal | Mootness, effects, discriminatory-purpose, same-decision, record-application, and judgment propositions as stated |
| Concurrence | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Discriminatory-purpose holding, record application, and judgment |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | O'Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Mootness, effects, and same-decision propositions |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: The case remains live because the plan can affect the next § 5 baseline; nonretrogression defeats only the effects theory; the purpose clause independently reaches intentional racial denial or abridgment; and although an identical-plan same-decision showing is available, the Board did not carry it.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Continuing baseline consequence

Holding and operative rule: A preclearance controversy is not moot when the challenged plan retains a concrete legal consequence by supplying or materially affecting the baseline for a later § 5 comparison. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: The prospect that a new plan will govern the next election does not erase the legal status of the 1992 plan. Section 5 compares a proposed voting change against the lawful operative baseline, and the preclearance judgment determines whether the 1992 plan may occupy that position. That continuing consequence is concrete and affects the legal analysis of the next covered change. The Court therefore has a live controversy without holding that every superseded voting plan remains reviewable indefinitely. 

##### Question 2: Nonretrogression and the effects prong

Holding and operative rule: A nonretrogressive plan does not violate § 5's effects prong merely because it preserves dilution, fails to create a majority-Black district, or is less favorable than an available alternative. Prohibited effect requires worsening from the lawful operative baseline. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: *Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board*, decided May 12, 1997, keeps the effects inquiry distinct from § 2. The parties' nonretrogression premise resolves the § 5 effects question even though a separate § 2 claim, a purpose objection, or evidence drawn from rejected alternatives may remain. Treating the availability of a more minority-favorable plan as prohibited effect would replace the statutory baseline comparison with a maximization requirement the earlier decision rejected.

##### Question 3: Discriminatory but nonretrogressive purpose

Holding and operative rule: Section 5's separate purpose clause is not limited to intent to cause retrogression. A covered jurisdiction may not obtain preclearance for a voting change selected with a racial purpose to deny or abridge voting rights merely because the change is nonretrogressive.

Reasoning: The statute states purpose and effect as independent preclearance conditions. Giving the effects clause a retrogression benchmark does not convert nonretrogression into a safe harbor for intentional discrimination. Otherwise a covered jurisdiction could deliberately preserve or impose racial voting inequality whenever the existing baseline was no better. *Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board*, decided May 12, 1997, already required consideration of dilutive operation, rejected alternatives, historical context, decision sequence, procedural departures, official statements, and pretext as purpose evidence. Here the Board's prolonged resistance, rejection of feasible alternatives, departure from school-districting considerations, sudden adoption of the ill-fitting Police Jury plan, and unsupported neutral explanations establish that prohibited purpose materially influenced the choice. The Board did not carry its statutory burden to establish the absence of that purpose. 

##### Question 4: Identical-plan same-decision showing

Holding and operative rule: When prohibited racial purpose materially influenced adoption, a covered jurisdiction may obtain preclearance only by proving that independently sufficient lawful reasons would have produced the identical voting change absent the prohibited consideration. (joined by Stone, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas) The Board did not carry that burden on this record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: The same-decision principle prevents a prohibited consideration that did not determine the governmental choice from automatically invalidating an otherwise independently produced plan, but § 5's existing allocation of persuasion remains unchanged. The covered jurisdiction must identify lawful reasons sufficient in their own right and prove that those reasons would have generated the identical lines. General assertions of administrative convenience, reliance on another body's map, or preservation of precincts do not suffice when the record shows departures from the jurisdiction's ordinary objectives and substantial evidence of pretext. *Texas v. Lesage*, decided November 29, 1999, confirms that same-decision proof must be conclusive as to the challenged governmental outcome; here the Board's proof is not. Preclearance must therefore be denied whether the identical-plan route is unavailable, as four Justices conclude, or available under the Court's controlling rule.

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Section 5 requires the jurisdiction to show that the change does not have a prohibited purpose. Once such purpose materially influenced adoption, an identical-result showing does not erase it, although the Board fails even under the Court's more permissive rule.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by O'Connor, Kennedy, and Thomas: The cases remain live, and the plan is nonretrogressive, but the purpose prong should be limited to intent to cause retrogression. They join the same-decision rule only as the governing limit if the broader purpose holding controls and would otherwise affirm the preclearance judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Reno v. Bossier Parish School Board; Price v. Bossier Parish School Board*, decided May 12, 1997: clarified; its retrogression-based effects rule and purpose-evidence rule remain, and its reserved purpose question is resolved to reach intentional racial denial or abridgment even without retrogression.

*Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp.*: extended; its same-decision principle operates within § 5 while leaving the statutory burden of persuasion on the covered jurisdiction.

*Texas v. Lesage*, decided November 29, 1999: distinguished; its same-decision rule concerns retrospective liability for a discrete outcome, while § 5 requires the covered jurisdiction to prove entitlement to preclearance of the identical voting change.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States District Court for the District of Columbia is reversed, and judgment shall be entered denying preclearance to the 1992 plan.  
Effect: The 1992 plan may not serve as a lawfully precleared baseline for a later covered change. The decision neither resolves a separate § 2 claim, draws a replacement map, prescribes a racial percentage, invalidates completed elections, nor requires maximization of Black voting strength.

### Nixon v. Shrink Missouri Government PAC

Argued October 5, 1999 | Decided January 24, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: Missouri's candidate-contribution limits enjoined, 161 F.3d 519 (8th Cir. 1998).   
Area of Law: First Amendment; campaign finance

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Missouri's candidate-contribution limits violate the First Amendment because the State lacked evidence beyond the corruption interests recognized in *Buckley* or because the limits were too low.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's application of *Russell v. Burris* to the Missouri record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Anticorruption predicate and practical fit

Holding and operative rule: A candidate-contribution limit is constitutional when government establishes an objectively grounded risk of actual or apparent quid-pro-quo corruption or circumvention and closely fits the ceiling to that risk without materially disabling meaningful association or effective campaign advocacy. Missouri satisfied that standard on this record.

Reasoning: *Russell v. Burris*, decided April 28, 1999, places the burden on the State but does not require completed bribery, a changed official vote, or one prescribed form of evidence. Missouri identified a very large contribution, episodes of state corruption, and evidence of a public concern with candidate indebtedness sufficient to ground the asserted interest. The limits regulate the amount of direct financial support rather than the contributor's underlying view, and the record does not show that candidates were unable to mount effective campaigns or that contributors lacked a meaningful avenue of association. The First Amendment inquiry is practical, not a comparison with the nominal federal amount sustained in *Buckley v. Valeo*, 424 U.S. 1 (1976). No universal dollar floor or automatic inflation formula displaces the record-specific fit analysis. The injunction must therefore be vacated. 

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The constitutional distinction between contributions and expenditures should remain open to reconsideration, but the Missouri limits are valid under the governing contribution doctrine and the present record.

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg: Contribution limits require a practical accommodation between political participation and the integrity of representative government. The Court's record-specific application creates neither a formula nor a license for ceilings that materially suppress effective advocacy.

#### Dissents

Kennedy: The existing contribution framework has encouraged circumvention while restricting direct political association. Missouri's evidence and chosen amounts do not justify continuing that regime on this record.

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Direct contributions are core political speech and association and should receive strict scrutiny. Missouri has not shown that these low ceilings are narrowly tailored to a proved quid-pro-quo danger.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Russell v. Burris*: applied; Missouri carried the current burden to establish an objectively grounded anticorruption risk and a close practical fit.

*Buckley v. Valeo*: clarified; state contribution limits need not match the federal dollar amount, but their operation must leave meaningful association and effective advocacy available.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is reversed, the injunction shall be vacated, and the case is remanded for entry of judgment for the state officials.  
Effect: Missouri may enforce the challenged contribution limits on this record. A future challenge supported by materially different proof of practical suppression, discriminatory structure, or an unsupported anticorruption premise remains available.

### Paul P. v. Verniero

Argued November 15, 1999 | Decided February 7, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: summary judgment for the State on the facial privacy claim affirmed and application-specific safeguard issues remanded, 170 F.3d 396 (3d Cir. 1999).   
Area of Law: Due process; informational privacy; sex-offender notification

Questions Presented:

1. Whether nonpublic home-address and identifying information falls within the constitutional protection for informational privacy.
2. If so, what balancing standard and dissemination safeguards govern compelled disclosure of that information through a sex-offender notification system.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O'Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in part, vacate in part, and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's extension of limited informational-privacy protection and its requirement of safeguards fitted to concrete public-safety need.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Privacy interest in compiled residential information

Holding and operative rule: Compiled nonpublic home-address and identifying information carries a limited Fourteenth Amendment privacy interest. The public character of an underlying conviction does not make the State's current compilation and residential localization of the registrant categorically unprotected.

Reasoning: The Constitution does not create a general right to conceal a conviction or every fact available from public sources. The information at issue, however, does more than repeat a judgment of conviction. It links a named person to a current residence, employment, identifying characteristics, and an official risk classification in a form designed for targeted dissemination. Under *Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority*, decided May 5, 1997, governmental access and disclosure of identified private information must remain genuinely and proportionately connected to the function that justifies it. The sensitivity is less than that of medical information, but the current residential compilation creates a real, nontrivial interest against disclosure to persons lacking a protective need. 

##### Question 2: Targeted notification and dissemination safeguards

Holding and operative rule: Public safety may justify higher-tier disclosure to persons with a concrete safety need after the individualized procedures required by *W.P. v. Verniero*, decided March 16, 1998. The State must reasonably fit the recipients, information, use, scope, confidentiality, and redistribution controls to the notification's protective function.

Reasoning: Notification is not unconstitutional merely because it includes protected information. The State has a substantial protective interest in warning persons who are genuinely likely to encounter a registrant found to present the relevant risk. That interest supports disclosure only as far as the need extends. The record must therefore permit review of who receives the notice, why the address is necessary for that recipient, what use is authorized, and what meaningful safeguards restrain unnecessary redistribution. Perfect prevention of every unauthorized disclosure is not required, but warnings without effective recipient limits, confidentiality rules, or enforceable distribution controls do not satisfy the genuine-and-proportionate connection required by *Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority*, decided May 5, 1997. The Third Circuit correctly rejected a categorical ban and recognized the address interest, but its affirmance of summary judgment is vacated insofar as it treated the dissemination system as adequate without applying that standard to the developed safeguards. 

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The Constitution supplies no freestanding right to prevent government from notifying the public about a convicted offender's current address and assessed danger. The State's individualized classification and public-safety purpose are sufficient, and any misuse by recipients should be addressed by statute rather than substantive due process.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Doe v. Southeastern Pennsylvania Transportation Authority*: extended; limited informational-privacy protection reaches compiled nonpublic residential and identifying data, and disclosure must remain genuinely and proportionately connected to the governmental function.

*W.P. v. Verniero*: followed; higher-tier notification remains conditioned on individualized risk procedures and a notification scope supported by the State's proof.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed insofar as it rejects a categorical bar and recognizes the permissibility of targeted notification, vacated insofar as it approves dissemination without the required safeguard inquiry, and remanded for proceedings consistent with this opinion.  
Effect: New Jersey may continue confidential registration, law-enforcement access, and individualized notice to persons with a concrete safety need. It must maintain meaningful controls over recipient eligibility, use, scope, confidentiality, and redistribution; public conviction information remains public, and no general secrecy right is created.

### Weisgram v. Marley Co.

Argued January 18, 2000 | Decided February 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below: inadmissible expert testimony excluded and judgment as a matter of law directed for Marley, 169 F.3d 514 (8th Cir. 1999).   
Area of Law: Civil procedure; evidence; judgment as a matter of law

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a court of appeals may direct judgment as a matter of law after excluding evidence and finding the remaining admissible record legally insufficient, rather than automatically ordering a new trial.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O'Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court's application of Rule 50 and the appellate remedial authority to the completed record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Appellate judgment after exclusion of proof

Holding and operative rule: Rule 50 and 28 U.S.C. § 2106 permit a court of appeals to direct judgment as a matter of law after excluding erroneously admitted evidence and finding the remaining admissible record legally insufficient. A new trial is not automatic when the party had a fair opportunity to prove the case.

Reasoning: Judgment as a matter of law tests whether a legally sufficient evidentiary basis supports the verdict. Evidence that should not have been admitted cannot supply that basis merely because the trial court initially allowed it. Appellate authority includes directing the judgment the record requires, while Rule 50 preserves discretion to order a new trial when fairness or a preserved procedural ground warrants one. Weisgram had notice that the expert proof was challenged, the governing reliability requirements were established, and the plaintiffs had a full opportunity to present their case. After the expert testimony was excluded, the remaining evidence did not establish defect or causation, and no specific ground for a new trial was preserved. The Eighth Circuit therefore acted within its authority in directing judgment for Marley. 

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Daubert v. Merrell Dow Pharmaceuticals, Inc.*: applied; inadmissible expert proof cannot support the legal sufficiency of a verdict.

*Neely v. Martin K. Eby Construction Co.*: followed; appellate courts possess authority to direct judgment while retaining discretion to remand for a new trial in an appropriate posture.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 50: clarified; sufficiency is assessed on the properly admitted record, and exclusion on appeal does not create an automatic retrial right.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Judgment for Marley remains in force. A new trial may still be appropriate in a different case involving an unexpected legal change, a blocked proffer, a preserved new-trial ground, or another procedural unfairness.
