## Decisions and Dispositions

### Hunt-Wesson, Inc. v. Franchise Tax Board of California

Argued January 12, 2000 | Decided February 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Appeal of California, First Appellate District; judgment below:  
assessment sustained, No. A079969 (Cal. Ct. App. Dec. 11, 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528458/usrep528458.pdf))  
Area of Law: State taxation; Due Process; Commerce Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Whether California may reduce a nondomiciliary corporation’s interest deduction dollar-for-dollar by reference to constitutionally exempt income from nonunitary subsidiaries.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Interest-deduction offset tied to nonunitary income

Holding and operative rule: A State may not disallow interest expense by reference to constitutionally exempt nonunitary income unless the State establishes a fair attribution between the expense and that income. It may disallow expenses actually attributable to exempt income through a supported allocation method.

Reasoning: California could not tax the nonunitary income directly because the income lacked the required constitutional relationship to the State. The challenged provision reached the same value indirectly by reducing the deduction for expenses of the taxable unitary business dollar-for-dollar according to the amount of exempt nonunitary income received. That correspondence did not reflect where the borrowed funds were used or how the relevant income was generated. A deduction limitation may properly allocate expense between taxable and exempt activity, but California’s rule assumed that all borrowing first supported nonunitary investment and supplied no factual or formula-based attribution capable of sustaining that assumption. Because the rule exposed additional unitary income to tax solely by reference to constitutionally exempt value, the assessment violated the Due Process and Commerce Clauses. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528458/usrep528458.pdf)) `OT_1999CHUNK3.md`

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Complete Auto Transit, Inc. v. Brady*: applied; a state tax must remain fairly apportioned to activity within the State and may not reach constitutionally exempt value indirectly.

*Container Corp. of America v. Franchise Tax Board*: followed; the attribution of income and expense must rationally reflect how the enterprise generates the income being taxed.

*National Life Insurance Co. v. United States*: followed; a deduction limitation measured directly by exempt income may constitute an impermissible tax on that income.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeal of California is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to invalidate the assessment and recalculate liability using a supported method that fairly attributes interest expense between taxable unitary activity and exempt nonunitary activity.  
Effect: California may not use the amount of nonunitary income as an automatic proxy for disallowing interest expense. It remains free to employ a tracing, ratio-based, or other reasonable allocation method that attributes expenses actually connected to exempt income.

### Baral v. United States

Argued January 18, 2000 | Decided February 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below:  
refund claim rejected, 172 F.3d 918 (D.C. Cir. 1999). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528431/usrep528431.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal taxation; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether wage withholding and an estimated-tax payment are paid for refund-limit purposes when remitted to the Treasury or only when the taxpayer files the return reporting the liability.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Statutory payment date for withholding and estimated tax

Holding and operative rule: For the refund lookback rule, wage withholding and estimated-tax remittances are deemed paid on the statutory due date specified by 26 U.S.C. § 6513, not when the taxpayer later files a delinquent return or when the Service assesses the liability.

Reasoning: Sections 6513(b)(1) and (2) expressly assign deemed-payment dates to withholding and estimated-tax remittances. For Baral’s 1988 tax year, both categories were deemed paid on April 15, 1989. His refund claim was filed on June 1, 1993, and the applicable three-year-plus-extension lookback period reached only to February 1, 1990. The remittances therefore fell outside the statutory period. Treating them as deposits until the delinquent return was filed would disregard the specific payment-date provisions and would give late filers a longer refund period than taxpayers who filed on time. Neither later assessment nor the filing of the return displaced the dates Congress assigned. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528431/usrep528431.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

26 U.S.C. §§ 6511 and 6513: clarified; the specific deemed-payment dates assigned to withholding and estimated-tax remittances govern the refund lookback calculation.

*Rosenman v. United States*: distinguished; general deposit principles do not override a statute that expressly identifies when a covered remittance is paid.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Baral’s refund and credit claim remains barred because no portion of the overpayment was deemed paid within the applicable lookback period. The ruling does not address a timely administrative claim or a remittance outside § 6513.

### Rotella v. Wood

Argued November 3, 1999 | Decided February 23, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
civil RICO action dismissed as untimely, 147 F.3d 438 (5th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528549/usrep528549.pdf))  
Area of Law: Civil RICO; limitations

Questions Presented:

1. Whether civil RICO’s four-year limitations period begins only when the plaintiff discovers both the injury and a pattern of racketeering.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Pattern discovery and civil RICO accrual

Holding and operative rule: The civil RICO limitations period does not await discovery of the complete racketeering pattern, and later predicate acts do not revive a stale claim for an earlier injury. An independently caused later injury and diligent fraudulent-concealment tolling remain governed by their separate rules.

Reasoning: Civil RICO supplies a four-year limitations period, and *Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp.* already rejects use of a later predicate act to restart the period for an earlier completed injury. Requiring discovery of the complete pattern would create a still longer delay by allowing a plaintiff who knew of a complete and actionable injury to postpone suit until the broader racketeering structure was uncovered. That result would undermine repose and allow evidence concerning events increasingly remote from trial. Rotella conceded that his injury and civil RICO claim were complete more than four years before filing. He therefore loses whether the unresolved general trigger is injury occurrence or injury discovery. Fraudulent concealment remains available only through proof of concealment and reasonable diligence, and the Court does not decide when a claim accrues if an injury occurs before the alleged pattern is legally complete. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528549/usrep528549.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Klehr v. A.O. Smith Corp.*: applied and clarified; later predicates do not revive damages for a stale injury, and discovery of the complete pattern is not an additional accrual prerequisite.

*Agency Holding Corp. v. Malley-Duff & Associates, Inc.*: followed; the uniform four-year civil RICO limitations period remains in force.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.  
Effect: Rotella’s claim for the earlier completed injury remains untimely. A genuinely new injury caused by a later act may accrue separately, and concealment may support tolling only upon the required showing of concealment and reasonable diligence.

### Rice v. Cayetano

Argued October 6, 1999 | Decided February 23, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
ancestry restriction upheld, 146 F.3d 1075 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528495/usrep528495.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fifteenth Amendment; voting rights

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Hawaii may limit voting for OHA trustees to persons defined by Hawaiian ancestry.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | Souter | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg as to Part II | Would affirm | Part II joined by Ginsburg |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Ancestry-based franchise in OHA elections

Holding and operative rule: A State may not condition the franchise in an election for public officials on an ancestry rule operating as a racial classification when the election is not an internal election of a constitutionally sufficient political community. Hawaii’s restriction on voting for OHA trustees violates the Fifteenth Amendment. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: The Fifteenth Amendment reaches an ancestry qualification when ancestry functions as a proxy for race. Hawaii’s statutory definition expressly traced eligibility to descent from the peoples inhabiting the islands in 1778 and excluded otherwise qualified state citizens lacking that descent. The election selected trustees of a state agency in a statewide public electoral process, not officers of a federally recognized tribe or another distinct sovereign conducting its internal affairs. The State therefore could not allocate the public franchise according to the race it associated with the trust’s beneficiaries. Nor did the special-purpose district cases excuse the restriction, because departure from one-person, one-vote principles under the Fourteenth Amendment does not authorize racial exclusion under the Fifteenth. The judgment is confined to this public state election and does not decide tribal membership or internal tribal governance. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528495/usrep528495.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer, joined by Souter: The ancestry definitions were not a workable political-community classification because they swept beyond any tribal membership rule and did not correspond coherently to the groups receiving OHA benefits. They concurred in invalidating the restriction without adopting every aspect of the Court’s ancestry analysis.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg as to Part II: Hawaii’s relationship with its indigenous population and its administration of trust responsibilities supplied a political rather than invidiously racial basis for limiting participation in the trustee election. He would affirm while preserving federal authority over Native Hawaiian affairs.

Ginsburg: Congress and Hawaii could recognize Native Hawaiians as an indigenous political community and structure a limited electoral mechanism connected to their trust interests. She would reject the Fifteenth Amendment challenge.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Guinn v. United States*: followed; an ancestry rule that functions as a racial proxy may not be used to restrict the franchise.

*Morton v. Mancari*: declined to extend to a statewide election for officials of a state agency when no qualifying tribal political community conducts the election.

*Salyer Land Co. v. Tulare Lake Basin Water Storage District* and *Ball v. James*: distinguished; exceptions associated with special-purpose districts do not excuse a Fifteenth Amendment racial restriction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the ancestry restriction governing elections for OHA trustees shall not be enforced against otherwise qualified voters.  
Effect: Rice and other otherwise qualified Hawaii voters may participate in OHA trustee elections without satisfying the statutory ancestry definition. The decision does not govern membership rules or internal elections of a federally recognized tribe.

### Roe v. Flores-Ortega

Argued November 1, 1999 | Decided February 23, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
federal habeas relief granted under the circuit’s consent rule, 160 F.3d 534 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528470/usrep528470.pdf))  
Area of Law: Sixth Amendment; ineffective assistance; criminal appeals

Questions Presented:

1. What *Strickland* standard governs counsel’s failure to file a notice of appeal when the defendant did not clearly instruct counsel either to appeal or not to appeal.

#### Judgment

Vacated and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer in full; Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg as to Part II-B | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Breyer; Part II-B for Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports vacatur and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins Part II-B; dissents from Parts II-A and III |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins Souter’s opinion and Part II-B |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Failure to consult or file a notice of appeal

Holding and operative rule: *Strickland* governs. Counsel must follow an express instruction to file an appeal and must respect an express and competent decision not to appeal. When the defendant gives no clear instruction, counsel must reasonably consult if a rational defendant would want an appeal or the particular defendant has demonstrated an interest in appealing. Prejudice requires a reasonable probability that, but for deficient performance, the defendant would have taken a timely appeal, without a separate requirement to prove that the lost appeal would probably have succeeded. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The decision whether to appeal belongs to the defendant, but the Constitution does not impose an automatic filing or consultation rule in every case. Counsel’s duty depends on all relevant circumstances, including whether the conviction followed trial or plea, whether nonfrivolous grounds were apparent, whether the defendant received the expected sentence, and whether the defendant expressed interest in review. Consultation means advising the client about the advantages and disadvantages of appeal and making a reasonable effort to determine the client’s wishes. When deficient performance causes forfeiture of the entire first appeal, prejudice concerns the lost appellate proceeding rather than the probable success of particular claims. The Ninth Circuit presumed deficiency and prejudice without completing either inquiry, and the record does not establish what counsel and Flores-Ortega discussed. Remand is therefore required. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528470/usrep528470.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: The granted question concerned an appeal following a guilty plea. After a trial, counsel will almost always have a duty to consult, but the Court’s circumstances-based standard is sufficient here.

Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg: Counsel should almost always consult after conviction unless the defendant has plainly and competently waived review or extraordinary circumstances make advice unnecessary. They would find counsel’s performance deficient on this record while applying the Court’s lost-appeal prejudice standard.

Ginsburg: She joined Souter’s clearer consultation rule and would hold that counsel could not end the representation without ensuring that Flores-Ortega understood his appellate options.

#### Dissents

No separate dissent filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Strickland v. Washington*: clarified; failure to initiate an appeal is assessed through a circumstances-based duty to consult and a lost-appeal prejudice inquiry.

*Rodriquez v. United States*: followed; a defendant need not establish the probable merits of an appellate claim to obtain relief for counsel’s forfeiture of the appeal.

*Smith v. Robbins*, decided January 19, 2000: clarified; the requirement of active advocacy on a first appeal does not create an automatic duty to file an appeal without instruction, and the antecedent consultation question remains governed by *Strickland*.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is vacated, and the case is remanded for findings concerning counsel’s duty to consult, the consultation or instructions actually given, deficient performance, and whether Flores-Ortega would have taken a timely appeal.  
Effect: Flores-Ortega does not receive automatic reinstatement of his appeal on the present record. An express instruction to appeal remains controlling, and a defendant proving deficient consultation and a reasonable probability that he would have appealed is entitled to relief without first proving the appeal’s likely merits.

### Village of Willowbrook v. Olech

Argued January 10, 2000 | Decided February 23, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal reversed and class-of-one claim allowed to proceed, 160 F.3d 386 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528562/usrep528562.pdf))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Equal Protection Clause permits a class-of-one claim when a plaintiff alleges intentional differential treatment from materially similarly situated persons without a rational basis, and whether subjective ill will must be pleaded as a separate element.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Per curiam | Per curiam | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Breyer | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the per curiam opinion in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the per curiam opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Class-of-one equal protection

Holding and operative rule: A plaintiff states a class-of-one equal-protection claim by plausibly alleging intentional differential treatment from materially similarly situated persons without a rational basis for the distinction. Subjective spite may be evidence of arbitrariness or pretext, but it is not an independent element.

Reasoning: The Equal Protection Clause protects an individual against intentional and arbitrary governmental discrimination even when the plaintiff does not identify a larger class sharing the injury. Olech alleged that the Village intentionally demanded a 33-foot easement from her while requiring only 15 feet from materially similarly situated owners, that the larger demand was irrational and arbitrary, and that the Village ultimately accepted the ordinary 15-foot easement. Those allegations state a traditional equal-protection claim apart from any allegation of personal animus. The holding does not convert negligence, an ordinary administrative mistake, a materially different factual situation, or an objectively rational exercise of discretion into a constitutional violation. The plaintiff must still prove intentional differential treatment, genuine similarity, and absence of a rational basis. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep528/usrep528562/usrep528562.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: The complaint also alleged vindictive and illegitimate action, which reduced concern that ordinary zoning or administrative errors would become constitutional cases. He agreed that the pleaded facts were sufficient without making subjective ill will a separate formal element.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Sioux City Bridge Co. v. Dakota County*: extended; intentional and irrational differential administration may violate equal protection even when the plaintiff constitutes a class of one.

Rational-basis equal protection: clarified; animus may support proof of arbitrariness or pretext but is not indispensable when intentional differential treatment lacks any rational basis.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, and the case shall proceed in the District Court on the class-of-one claim.  
Effect: Olech may attempt to prove intentional irrational treatment in comparison with materially similarly situated property owners. The decision does not constitutionalize negligence, routine administrative error, discretionary disagreement, or treatment supported by a material factual difference.

### Shalala v. Illinois Council on Long Term Care, Inc.

Argued November 8, 1999 | Decided February 29, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
district-court dismissal reversed and federal-question jurisdiction sustained for the ripe claims, 143 F.3d 1072 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529001/usrep529001.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Medicare; federal jurisdiction

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Medicare’s statutory review channel bars a pre-enforcement federal-question action when regulated nursing homes may have no safe and practical route to judicial review.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Meaningful review under Medicare’s statutory channel

Holding and operative rule: A specialized Medicare review channel does not displace § 1331 jurisdiction when, as applied, it supplies no safe and practical route to judicial review and requires exposure to catastrophic sanctions merely to create a reviewable vehicle. Routine delay, expense, or ordinary exhaustion remains insufficient.

Reasoning: The channeling inquiry asks not only whether an administrative sequence exists on paper, but whether that sequence preserves meaningful judicial review of the claims presented. The nursing homes challenged the legality of generally applicable regulations before enforcement. On the record before the Court, obtaining review could require accepting findings carrying immediate operational and reputational consequences, risking termination from Medicare, incurring severe daily sanctions, or threatening continuity of resident care. A route that can be activated only through institutional self-destruction does not provide meaningful review within the rule established by *Thunder Basin*. This is not a general hardship exception. A provider must ordinarily use a realistic administrative path, and delay or compliance cost alone does not open § 1331. The Seventh Circuit separately held the vagueness claim unripe, and Medicaid matters outside the judgment remain unresolved. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529001/usrep529001.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by O’Connor, Souter, and Ginsburg: Section 405(h), as incorporated into Medicare, channels claims arising under the program through agency presentment and § 405(g) review. The Secretary’s interpretation made an administrative hearing available after a noncompliance determination without requiring termination, so the asserted hardship reflected postponed rather than foreclosed review.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich*: clarified; meaningful review requires a practically usable route and is absent when regulated parties must incur catastrophic and irreparable consequences merely to create a vehicle.

*Bowen v. Michigan Academy of Family Physicians*: followed; a channeling provision is not construed to eliminate judicial review of a substantial regulatory challenge.

*Heckler v. Ringer*: distinguished; ordinary presentment and delayed review through a usable benefits channel differ from a route requiring exposure to institution-ending sanctions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed, and the case shall proceed in the District Court on the ripe claims properly preserved within that judgment.  
Effect: The Council may pursue those claims under § 1331 without first exposing a member facility to termination or catastrophic sanctions solely to manufacture review. The decision does not excuse ordinary exhaustion where a realistic administrative route exists, revive the unripe vagueness claim, or adjudicate the merits of any challenged Medicare regulation.

### United States v. Johnson

Argued December 8, 1999 | Decided March 1, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below:  
automatic credit against supervised release recognized, 154 F.3d 569 (6th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529053/usrep529053.pdf))  
Area of Law: Criminal law; supervised release

Questions Presented:

1. Whether excess prison time served after convictions were later vacated automatically reduces supervised release under 18 U.S.C. § 3624(e).

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Excess imprisonment and supervised release

Holding and operative rule: Supervised release begins when the person is actually released from imprisonment. Excess custody does not automatically shorten the term, although the district court may consider that custody when exercising its modification and early-termination authority under 18 U.S.C. § 3583(e).

Reasoning: Section 3624(e) states that supervised release begins on the day the person is released from imprisonment and does not run during a period of imprisonment. Release therefore means actual freedom from confinement, not a constructive date calculated later after convictions or sentences are invalidated. Imprisonment and supervised release serve different statutory functions: incarceration imposes confinement, while supervision assists transition and rehabilitation in the community. Automatically subtracting excess prison time would shorten the distinct period of community supervision Congress prescribed. The statute nevertheless permits the sentencing court to address the equitable consequences of excess incarceration by modifying conditions under § 3583(e)(2) or terminating supervision after the statutory minimum under § 3583(e)(1). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529053/usrep529053.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

18 U.S.C. § 3624(e): clarified; supervised release begins upon actual release from imprisonment and is not reduced automatically by excess custody.

18 U.S.C. § 3583(e): clarified; modification and early termination, rather than automatic credit, provide the statutory means for considering excess incarceration.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the actual-release rule and the district court’s authority under § 3583(e).  
Effect: Johnson’s supervised-release term is not automatically reduced by the excess prison time. The district court may consider that custody in deciding whether lawful modification or early termination is warranted.

### United States v. Locke; International Association of Independent Tanker Owners v. Locke

Argued December 7, 1999 | Decided March 6, 2000  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
Washington tanker-regulation scheme largely sustained, 148 F.3d 1053 (9th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529089/usrep529089.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal preemption; maritime law

Questions Presented:

1. Which Washington requirements governing tanker design, equipment, operation, personnel, training, and reporting are preempted by federal maritime statutes and Coast Guard regulations.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Federal displacement of Washington tanker rules

Holding and operative rule: Title II of the Ports and Waterways Safety Act occupies the subjects of general vessel design, equipment, operation, manning, and personnel qualification. Title I leaves room for genuinely local waterway measures subject to conflict review, while the Oil Pollution Act’s saving clauses preserve state liability and compensation rules rather than independent systemic vessel standards.

Reasoning: Congress directed national uniformity for the tanker subjects enumerated in Title II. Washington’s crew-training and drill rule, English-language qualification, general navigation-watch requirement, and worldwide casualty-reporting obligation regulated those occupied subjects or conflicted with federal rules intended to provide the sole reporting obligation. The equipment rule previously invalidated by the Ninth Circuit remains preempted. Other provisions require separate analysis because Title I preserves state measures addressing distinctive local-waterway conditions, such as channel depth, narrowness, visibility, and traffic, when they do not conflict with a federal determination. The Oil Pollution Act’s saving provisions protect state authority over liability, compensation, and additional cleanup responsibilities but do not reopen fields Congress assigned to national vessel regulation. Agency preference or a generalized interest in uniformity is insufficient by itself; displacement must rest on enacted federal law, a valid regulation, or an affirmative federal determination. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529089/usrep529089.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Ray v. Atlantic Richfield Co.*: reaffirmed; Title II leaves no room for state regulation of the enumerated national tanker-design, equipment, operation, manning, and personnel subjects.

*Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*: followed; preemption must rest on an operative federal source or conflict and may not arise from agency preference or regulatory absence alone.

Oil Pollution Act saving clauses: clarified; they preserve state liability, compensation, and cleanup authority but do not authorize state duplication of occupied national vessel standards.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed and the cases are remanded with instructions to enjoin WAC §§ 317-21-130, 317-21-230, and 317-21-250, the general-watch component of § 317-21-200, and to leave § 317-21-265 invalid while reassessing the remaining provisions individually under the distinction between occupied national vessel standards and permissible local-waterway measures.  
Effect: Washington may not enforce the identified training, language, general-watch, equipment, and worldwide-reporting rules. Local measures addressing demonstrated characteristics of Washington waters remain potentially valid when they neither enter an occupied field nor conflict with a binding federal determination.

### Portuondo v. Agard

Argued November 1, 1999 | Decided March 6, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
conditional federal habeas relief granted, 117 F.3d 696 (2d Cir. 1997), rehearing denied, 159 F.3d 98 (2d Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529061/usrep529061.pdf))  
Area of Law: Due process; right to testify; federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a prosecutor violates due process and the right to testify by arguing that a defendant tailored his testimony after hearing the other trial witnesses.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stone, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality | Scalia | O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stevens | Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | Stone, Souter | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins Ginsburg’s dissent in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the dissent’s conclusion that protected trial presence alone may not be converted into affirmative evidence of dishonesty and that the error warrants the conditional habeas relief entered below.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by Scalia. No opinion of the Court.

##### Question 1: Generic tailoring argument based on trial presence

Holding and operative rule: Controls under *Marks*: The prosecutor’s summation did not cross the constitutional threshold of fundamental unfairness on this record, so the grant of federal habeas relief cannot stand. No majority adopts the plurality’s broader categorical approval of generic tailoring arguments or resolves every direct Fifth or Sixth Amendment theory. (joined by Stevens and Breyer)

Reasoning: Four Justices would hold that a defendant who testifies may have credibility challenged like any other witness and that the prosecutor may refer generally to the opportunity to tailor testimony after hearing earlier witnesses. Stevens and Breyer regarded the generic accusation as improper because it rested on the exercise of trial-presence rights rather than concrete evidence of fabrication, but concluded that the summation did not make this trial fundamentally unfair. Stone, Souter, and Ginsburg would hold that the argument unconstitutionally penalized protected presence and testimony and would sustain habeas relief. The Stevens concurrence supplies the narrowest ground supporting reversal: the particular comments do not justify setting aside this conviction under the constitutional fundamental-fairness threshold. The judgment therefore does not establish that generic tailoring accusations are categorically permissible. ([Library of Congress Tiles](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529061/usrep529061.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Controls under *Marks*: Stevens, joined by Breyer, concluded that the prosecutor should not have invited an inference of dishonesty merely from Agard’s exercise of his right to attend trial. The comments nevertheless did not cross the high constitutional threshold separating serious trial error from fundamental unfairness requiring the conviction to be set aside.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by Stone and Souter: A prosecutor may expose contradictions, changed accounts, collusion, or other evidence of fabrication, but may not use protected presence alone as the affirmative premise for an adverse credibility inference. They would affirm because the Second Circuit’s completed harmless-error review supported conditional habeas relief.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rose v. Lundy*: applied; the case-specific summation did not cross the constitutional threshold of fundamental unfairness required to set aside the state conviction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for denial of habeas relief on the tailoring-comment claim and for proceedings on any remaining preserved claims.  
Effect: Agard’s conviction is not set aside on this ground. The judgment does not establish categorical approval of generic tailoring arguments, prevent trial courts or States from prohibiting them, or restrict argument based on concrete evidence of inconsistency, collusion, changed accounts, or actual fabrication.
