# OCTOBER TERM 1999, CHUNK 4 OF 8

## Docket and Chronology

| Date | Case or matter | Event |
|---|---|---|
| March 13, 2000 | *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States* | Merits decision |
| March 21, 2000 | *FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.* | Merits decision |
| March 22, 2000 | *Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth* | Merits decision |
| March 22, 2000 | *Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.* | Merits decision |
| March 28, 2000 | *Florida v. J. L.* | Merits decision |
| March 28, 2000 | *Garner v. Jones* | Merits decision |
| March 29, 2000 | *City of Erie v. Pap’s A.M.* | Merits decision |
| April 3, 2000 | *Strout v. Albanese* | Merits decision |
| April 10, 2000 | *Carter v. City of Philadelphia* | Merits decision |
| April 17, 2000 | *Bond v. United States* | Merits decision |

## Decisions and Dispositions

### Fraternal Order of Police v. United States

Argued December 13, 1999 | Decided March 13, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment for the United States affirmed on panel rehearing, 173 F.3d 898 (D.C. Cir. 1999). ([Department of Justice](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/fraternal-order-police-v-united-states-opposition))  
Area of Law: Second Amendment; equal protection; federalism; Commerce Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Whether 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(9), by prohibiting firearm possession by a person whose qualifying misdemeanor domestic-violence conviction remains legally operative, impermissibly burdens the individual right to keep and bear arms recognized in the simulation, or instead constitutes a permissible danger-linked firearm disability, including as to government-issued duty firearms.
2. Whether Congress violates equal protection by withholding § 925(a)(1)’s official-use exemption from persons convicted of misdemeanor domestic violence while leaving that exemption available under federal law to persons subject to other § 922(g) disabilities, including felons.
3. Whether application of § 922(g)(9) to state and local law-enforcement officers violates the Tenth Amendment or anti-commandeering principle.
4. Whether § 922(g)(9) is within Congress’s Commerce Clause authority when the Government must prove in each possession case that the firearm was possessed in or affecting commerce.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Thomas as to Questions 1 through 3 | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Questions 1 through 3 for Thomas |
| Concurrence | Stone | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Individual-right ground and danger-linked boundary |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins Questions 1 through 3 and rejects the Commerce Clause holding |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full and authors a concurrence.  
Announced ground: The Court need not announce a general scrutiny tier to sustain a firearm disability resting on a constitutionally adequate adjudication of violent domestic abuse. Stone’s concurrence, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, states that the individual right associated with common arms for lawful self-defense permits this narrowly danger-linked disability while the qualifying conviction remains operative.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Danger-linked firearm disability after a domestic-violence conviction

Holding and operative rule: A constitutionally adequate conviction for domestic violence involving actual or attempted physical force, or the threatened use of a deadly weapon, supplies an adjudicated danger predicate sufficient to prohibit firearm possession while the conviction remains legally operative. No new present-danger hearing, fixed expiration date, or general restoration entitlement is constitutionally required. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Section 922(g)(9) regulates present possession rather than adding punishment to the completed misdemeanor. The predicate conviction establishes through criminal process that the person used or attempted force, or threatened deadly force, against a person in a domestic relationship. Congress could treat that adjudication as materially relevant to the danger created by later firearm access, including access to a government-issued duty weapon. The statutory treatment of expungement, set-aside, pardon, and effective restoration confirms that the disability follows the conviction’s continuing legal force. The Court does not resolve the treatment of nonviolent misdemeanors, ordinary drug possession, mental-health classifications, civil protective orders, arrests, or every felony category. ([Department of Justice](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/fraternal-order-police-v-united-states-opposition)) `OT_1999CHUNK4.md`

##### Question 2: Equal protection and the official-use exemption

Holding and operative rule: Congress does not violate equal protection by withholding the official-use exemption from persons convicted of misdemeanor domestic violence while leaving that exemption available under federal law to some persons subject to other firearm disabilities. The classification has a rational relationship to closing a demonstrated enforcement gap. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Congress could distinguish the domestic-violence misdemeanor category because conduct that would ordinarily be charged as a felony was frequently treated as a misdemeanor in the domestic setting, allowing offenders to escape employment and firearm screens built around felony status. Congress could also conclude that government employers already excluded violent felons from armed positions more consistently than they excluded domestic-violence misdemeanants. Rational-basis review does not require Congress to eliminate every related danger simultaneously or extend the new disability to every arguably comparable category. The classification directly addresses the category Congress identified without depending on occupational animus or another suspect criterion. ([Department of Justice](https://www.justice.gov/osg/brief/fraternal-order-police-v-united-states-opposition))

##### Question 3: Direct regulation and anti-commandeering

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(g)(9) directly regulates firearm possession by individuals and does not commandeer state government merely because some regulated persons are state or local law-enforcement officers. It requires no State to enact legislation, administer a federal program, or enforce federal law against others. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The federal prohibition is enforced by federal authorities through federal criminal process. A State remains responsible for selecting its employees and defining their duties, but it may not authorize an employee to engage in possession that valid federal law prohibits. The incidental consequence that a covered employee may no longer satisfy an armed position’s requirements does not transform direct regulation into an order that state officials administer federal law. *Reno v. Condon*, decided January 12, 2000, confirms that anti-commandeering preserves institutional responsibility rather than creating immunity from otherwise valid direct federal regulation. The judgment does not authorize Congress to require state police to investigate, disarm, prosecute, or supervise covered persons on the Federal Government’s behalf.

##### Question 4: Commerce Clause nexus

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(g)(9) is valid as a fitted possession restriction within the federal regulation of the interstate firearms market when the Government genuinely proves the offense-specific connection to commerce required by the statute. A nominal jurisdictional phrase or remote historical movement that performs no present constitutional work is insufficient.

Reasoning: *United States v. Hale*, decided June 5, 1996, permits Congress to include possession when possession and transfer are functionally connected within a valid interstate product-market regime and exclusion would materially impair that regime. Section 922(g)(9) also contains a case-specific commerce element. That element must identify a genuine relationship between the charged possession and interstate commerce; it may not serve as a verbal substitute for the attenuated aggregate-effects theory rejected in *United States v. Lopez*, 514 U.S. 549 (1995). The organizational challenge does not present a particular prosecution in which the Government relied solely on an object’s remote interstate history. The statute is therefore sustained subject to the requirement that its commerce element do actual work in each application.

#### Concurrences

Stone, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The Second Amendment protects an individual interest in common arms for lawful home self-defense, but that interest does not prevent a firearm disability tied to a constitutionally adequate conviction for violent domestic abuse. The concurrence announces no general scrutiny tier and reserves materially different predicates, restoration claims, public carriage, and licensing.

#### Dissents

Thomas concurred in the Court’s resolution of the firearms-right, equal-protection, and anti-commandeering questions but dissented from the Commerce Clause holding. He would hold that intrastate possession by a state or local employee cannot be reached merely because the firearm moved across state lines at some earlier time, and he regarded the broader federal market relationship as insufficiently connected to the particular possession.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Hale*: clarified; its fitted-market rule reaches a possession disability imposed on an adjudicated dangerous class when the offense-specific commerce connection is genuinely proved.

*Reno v. Condon*: applied; direct federal regulation of state-associated activity is distinct from compelled state administration of federal law.

*Printz v. United States*: distinguished; § 922(g)(9) imposes no federal enforcement or administrative duty on state officers.

*United States v. Lopez*: applied; a jurisdictional element must establish a genuine case-specific commerce connection and may not operate as a nominal incantation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Section 922(g)(9) remains enforceable against persons with legally operative qualifying domestic-violence misdemeanor convictions, including state and local officers carrying duty firearms. The decision creates no general rule for nonviolent predicates and does not determine what commerce proof will suffice in a materially different individual prosecution.

### FDA v. Brown & Williamson Tobacco Corp.

Argued December 1, 1999 | Decided March 21, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
FDA jurisdiction over tobacco products as customarily marketed rejected and the implementing rule invalidated, 153 F.3d 155 (4th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529120/usrep529120.pdf))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act authorizes the FDA to regulate tobacco products as drug-delivery devices.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.  
Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s whole-statute conclusion that the FDCA and Congress’s tobacco-specific enactments foreclose the asserted regulatory authority.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: FDA authority over tobacco products as customarily marketed

Holding and operative rule: The FDCA does not authorize the FDA to regulate tobacco products as customarily marketed when that construction conflicts with the Act’s remedial structure and with the distinct tobacco regime Congress repeatedly enacted and preserved.

Reasoning: Tobacco products can fall within the FDCA’s literal definitions only if the statute’s operative consequences are also applied. On the FDA’s findings, the Act would ordinarily require removal of products that could not be shown safe and effective for their intended use, yet Congress repeatedly legislated against a background in which tobacco remained lawfully marketed and adopted tobacco-specific restrictions without assigning the FDA the comprehensive authority claimed here. An agency may not derive a power whose legally required consequences contradict the administrative structure Congress enacted. Ordinary deference therefore does not apply because the whole statutory scheme clearly forecloses the asserted jurisdiction. The seriousness of tobacco-related harm cannot substitute for delegated authority. Congress remains free to confer that authority expressly, and the decision does not address a tobacco product marketed with a distinct therapeutic claim. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529120/usrep529120.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg: Nicotine is a drug, cigarettes and smokeless tobacco are delivery devices, and the FDCA’s broad language authorizes the FDA to regulate them. The tobacco-specific statutes, in their view, supplemented rather than displaced the agency’s authority and did not justify withholding ordinary administrative deference.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: applied; deference is unavailable when the statute as a whole and related enactments clearly foreclose the agency’s asserted jurisdiction.

*MCI Telecommunications Corp. v. American Telephone & Telegraph Co.*: followed; delegated administrative authority does not permit replacement of Congress’s enacted regulatory structure with an incompatible regime.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The challenged FDA tobacco rule remains invalid, and tobacco products as customarily marketed remain outside the asserted FDCA drug-and-device jurisdiction. Congress may confer tobacco authority expressly, and FDA authority over a product making a therapeutic claim remains open.

### Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth

Argued November 9, 1999 | Decided March 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
mandatory student-fee program held unconstitutional and broad relief affirmed, 151 F.3d 717 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529217/usrep529217.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; speech and association

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a public university violates the First Amendment by requiring students to pay an activity fee used to support student organizations engaged in political, ideological, and religious expression.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Souter | Stevens, Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that a generally applicable fee may fund a viewpoint-neutral private-speech program, while a student referendum may not replace viewpoint neutrality.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Mandatory fees supporting a private student-speech program

Holding and operative rule: A public university may use a generally applicable student fee to support a broad program of private extracurricular speech without individual ideological opt-outs when allocation rules and administration are viewpoint neutral. A majority vote or referendum may not allocate funds according to popularity or viewpoint.

Reasoning: The fee supports a program intended to facilitate a wide range of private student expression rather than to transmit one university message. Because access to the program is distributed among independent student organizations, attribution remains private and the constitutional safeguard is viewpoint neutrality, not an objecting student’s right to remove a proportional share of funding from disfavored speakers. The referendum mechanism presents a distinct problem because majority voting can reward popular views and burden unpopular ones. The lower courts therefore erred by imposing broad compelled-subsidy liability, but the referendum mechanism requires focused consideration on remand. The decision does not approve university ideological speech, discriminatory administration, or public financing of worship or devotional instruction as such. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529217/usrep529217.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by Stevens and Breyer: They agreed that the broad judgment could not stand and that viewpoint neutrality governs the program. They would avoid treating the university program as a conventional public forum and would rest more directly on the educational character of the fee-funded extracurricular program.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: extended; viewpoint-neutral support for diverse private student expression may be financed through a generally applicable student fee.

*Abood v. Detroit Board of Education*: distinguished; a university speech program providing neutral access to independent speakers is not equivalent to compelled support for one bargaining representative’s ideological advocacy.

*Keller v. State Bar of California*: distinguished; the university’s program is not confined to speech attributable to a mandatory professional association.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for consideration of the student-referendum mechanism under the viewpoint-neutrality requirement.

Effect: The University may continue collecting and distributing the activity fee through objective and viewpoint-neutral procedures without providing ideological opt-outs. It may not use popularity voting as a substitute for neutrality, and the decision does not resolve any distinct claim involving direct financing of worship or devotional instruction.

### Wal-Mart Stores, Inc. v. Samara Brothers, Inc.

Argued January 19, 2000 | Decided March 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
product-design trade-dress judgment affirmed, 165 F.3d 120 (2d Cir. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529205/usrep529205.pdf))  
Area of Law: Intellectual property; trademark

Questions Presented:

1. Whether product design can be inherently distinctive trade dress protectable under § 43(a) of the Lanham Act without proof of secondary meaning.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s requirement of secondary meaning for product design.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Distinctiveness of product design

Holding and operative rule: Product design is protectable under § 43(a) only upon proof that its primary significance to consumers is source identification. The claimant must separately establish nonfunctionality, likely confusion, and the remaining statutory elements.

Reasoning: Consumers ordinarily understand product design as making the product more useful or appealing, not as identifying its producer. Treating design as inherently distinctive would risk granting a perpetual trademark-like monopoly over useful or aesthetically desirable product features without evidence that consumers associate the feature with one source. Requiring secondary meaning fits the source-identification purpose of trademark law and leaves design creators to rely on copyright, patent, or acquired trade-dress protection where the requirements of those bodies of law are met. Samara obtained its product-design judgment without the required secondary-meaning finding, so the judgment and dependent relief cannot stand. Product packaging may present a different question, and secondary meaning does not eliminate the separate requirements of nonfunctionality and likely confusion. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529205/usrep529205.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Two Pesos, Inc. v. Taco Cabana, Inc.*: clarified; its recognition of inherently distinctive trade dress does not dispense with secondary meaning for product design.

*Qualitex Co. v. Jacobson Products Co.*: followed; source significance and functionality remain distinct statutory inquiries.

*Inwood Laboratories, Inc. v. Ives Laboratories, Inc.*: applied; secondary meaning asks whether the feature’s primary significance identifies source rather than the product itself.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings requiring proof of secondary meaning and the remaining elements of product-design trade dress.

Effect: The product-design judgment and relief dependent on it are set aside. Independent copyright and state-law claims remain undisturbed except to the extent the lower courts must separate overlapping remedies.

### Florida v. J. L.

Argued February 29, 2000 | Decided March 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Florida; judgment below:  
suppression of the firearm sustained, 727 So. 2d 204 (Fla. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529266/usrep529266.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; investigatory stops

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an anonymous tip that a described person at a stated location possesses a firearm, without predictive information or corroboration of illegality, supplies reasonable suspicion for a stop and frisk.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | Stone | Supports affirmance | Traceability, emergency, and source-reliability boundaries |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full and joins Kennedy’s concurrence.  
Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s distinction between corroborating identity and corroborating concealed illegality, together with the concurrence’s reservation of traceable-source and genuine-emergency cases.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Reliability of an anonymous firearm accusation

Holding and operative rule: An anonymous accusation supports a *Terry* stop only when police possess corroborated circumstances indicating the tipster’s reliability or a reliable basis for the asserted illegality. Corroboration of readily observable identity and location alone is insufficient, and there is no categorical firearm exception.

Reasoning: The tip accurately described a young person’s appearance and location, but that information showed only that the caller could identify the person. It supplied no predictive information, explanation of how the caller knew about the firearm, traceable source, observed threatening conduct, or other basis for crediting the assertion of concealed criminality. A rule allowing a stop whenever an anonymous caller pairs a description with an accusation would permit any person to trigger coercive police action against another without accountability. The presence of a firearm can heighten danger once reliable suspicion exists, but it cannot supply reliability missing from the accusation itself. *Illinois v. Wardlow*, decided January 12, 2000, likewise requires objective and particularized suspicion rather than generalized danger. Traceable calls, predictive information, observed danger, and genuine emergencies remain distinct. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529266/usrep529266.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Kennedy, joined by Stone: A tip may carry substantially greater reliability when the caller can be identified, uses a recorded emergency system, exposes himself to legal consequences for false reporting, or supplies information demonstrating firsthand knowledge. A genuine emergency involving an imminent threat may also justify prompt protective action on a different record.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Terry v. Ohio*: followed; a stop requires objective, particularized suspicion grounded in reliable information.

*Alabama v. White*: distinguished; the tip in *White* predicted future conduct that police corroborated, while the caller here supplied only observable identity and location.

*Illinois v. Wardlow*, decided January 12, 2000: applied; generalized danger cannot replace an individualized basis for suspecting the person stopped.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Florida is affirmed.

Effect: The firearm remains suppressed, and the resulting state disposition remains in force. Officers may act on anonymous information when traceability, prediction, firsthand basis, observed danger, emergency circumstances, or comparable evidence supplies the necessary reliability.

### Garner v. Jones

Argued January 11, 2000 | Decided March 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment for the parole officials reversed and retroactive application of the amended reconsideration rule held unconstitutional, 164 F.3d 589 (11th Cir. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-137.ZS.html))  
Area of Law: Ex Post Facto Clause; parole procedure

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Georgia’s retroactive amendment extending the interval between parole reconsideration hearings for life-sentenced prisoners creates an unconstitutional risk of increased punishment.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in part in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s practical-risk inquiry and its direction that Jones receive an adequate opportunity to develop evidence concerning the rule’s operation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Practical risk from extended parole reconsideration intervals

Holding and operative rule: A retroactive parole-scheduling change violates the Ex Post Facto Clause when its design or practical administration creates a significant, nonspeculative risk of prolonged confinement. Neither a longer interval nor the existence of nominal administrative discretion is conclusive by itself.

Reasoning: The relevant inquiry concerns the risk that the amended rule will increase actual punishment, not whether the rule is labeled procedural or discretionary. Georgia changed the ordinary reconsideration interval from three years to as long as eight, but the Board asserts authority to advance review when changed circumstances or new information make earlier consideration appropriate. The legal significance of that mechanism depends on how it operates. A genuine, accessible advancement process may prevent the longer maximum from creating a significant risk; an illusory or systematically unavailable mechanism may not. The Eleventh Circuit treated the extended interval as effectively dispositive without a developed operational record, while the District Court denied discovery that could bear directly on practical implementation. Remand is required for appropriate discovery and a record-based determination. The Court neither invalidates the eight-year maximum facially nor conclusively validates the Board’s advancement policy. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/99-137.ZS.html)) `OT_1999CHUNK4.md`

#### Concurrences

Scalia concurred in part in the judgment. He would concentrate on the legal operation of the enacted rule and was unwilling to make informal administrative policies an independent source of enforceable law, but agreed that the Eleventh Circuit’s categorical analysis could not stand.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens and Ginsburg: Extending the guaranteed reconsideration interval from three years to eight creates a substantial risk of additional incarceration, particularly when the State controls the evidence concerning discretionary advancement. They would affirm the judgment requiring relief, while permitting the State to demonstrate through an adequate record that the practical risk was eliminated.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*California Department of Corrections v. Morales*: clarified; retroactive parole changes are tested by significant practical risk rather than a fixed formula or procedural label.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adequate discovery and a determination whether the amended interval and advancement mechanism create a significant risk of prolonged confinement for Jones.

Effect: Georgia’s eight-year maximum is not facially invalidated, and Jones has not yet established an ex post facto violation. He may develop evidence concerning the Board’s actual practices, access to advancement, and the comparative risk of longer incarceration.

### City of Erie v. Pap’s A.M.

Argued November 10, 1999 | Decided March 29, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania; judgment below:  
public-nudity ordinance invalidated as applied to the adult-entertainment establishment, 553 Pa. 348, 719 A.2d 273 (1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529277/usrep529277.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; expressive conduct; public nudity

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Erie’s public-nudity ordinance may constitutionally require dancers in an adult-entertainment establishment to wear minimal clothing.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Mootness and continuing controversy |
| Plurality | O’Connor | Kennedy, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Merits application of incidental-burden review |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | General public-nudity regulation |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the continuing-controversy holding and opposes the merits judgment |
| Dissent | Stevens | Stone, Ginsburg | Would affirm | Merits |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Court’s continuing-controversy holding and joins Stevens’s dissent on the merits.  
Announced ground: The case remains live, but Erie may not receive incidental-burden treatment when its justification and operation principally target the communicative adult-entertainment setting and the evidentiary record does not establish a sufficiently fitted regulation of independently nonexpressive harms.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by O’Connor. No opinion of the Court on the merits.

##### Threshold: Continuing controversy

Holding and operative rule: Pap’s cessation of operations does not moot the case when the corporation remains legally extant, the state judgment bars enforcement of the ordinance against it, and resumption remains legally possible. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer)

Reasoning: The state judgment continues to determine Erie’s authority to enforce its ordinance against Pap’s, and Pap’s remained a legal entity capable of resuming the challenged activity. The record did not establish permanent dissolution, an enforceable abandonment of the business, or another event making renewed exposure impossible. The controversy therefore remains live despite the cessation of current performances.

##### Question 1: Minimal-clothing requirement for nude dancing

Holding and operative rule: Controls under *Marks*: A generally applicable public-nudity ordinance directed at crime and other nonexpressive secondary effects may be applied to nude dancing when it satisfies the incidental-burden requirements of *United States v. O’Brien*, 391 U.S. 367 (1968), and leaves the erotic message available through minimally clothed performance.

Reasoning: O’Connor, Kennedy, and Breyer concluded that the ordinance regulates the public state of nudity rather than the erotic viewpoint of the dance and that Erie could rely on crime and related secondary effects associated with adult-entertainment establishments. Requiring pasties and a G-string leaves the dance and its erotic message substantially available while addressing the identified nonexpressive harm. Scalia and Thomas supplied the remaining judgment votes on the broader ground that a general public-nudity law regulates conduct and need not be treated as a speech restriction. The O’Connor plurality’s *O’Brien* rationale is the logical subset of the judgment-supporting grounds and therefore controls under *Marks*. The judgment does not authorize a law whose actual operation targets disfavored ideas or a regulation unsupported by any independent nonexpressive objective. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529277/usrep529277.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Necessary fifth vote: Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurred in the judgment on the ground that a generally applicable prohibition of public nudity regulates conduct rather than expression and should be sustained without applying the plurality’s expressive-conduct analysis.

Souter concurred in the continuing-controversy holding but dissented from the merits judgment. He would require a stronger evidentiary basis connecting the ordinance to the asserted secondary effects before sustaining its application to protected expressive activity.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Stone and Ginsburg: Erie defended the ordinance through harms attributed specifically to the adult-entertainment setting, making the communicative context central to the law’s justification and operation. On the record presented, they would hold that the ordinance failed the scrutiny applicable to that content-linked burden and would affirm the state judgment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Barnes v. Glen Theatre, Inc.*: applied and clarified; a general public-nudity law may reach nude dancing when justified by independently nonexpressive concerns.

*United States v. O’Brien*: applied; the controlling merits rationale requires a content-neutral objective and an incidental burden fitted to that objective.

*Ward v. Rock Against Racism*: followed; content neutrality turns on the law’s operative justification rather than hostility to a particular message.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Pennsylvania is reversed, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the controlling incidental-burden rule.

Effect: Erie may enforce the minimal-clothing requirement against Pap’s under the record and ordinance before the Court. The decision does not authorize suppression of the erotic viewpoint or foreclose challenges to discriminatory enforcement, pretextual regulation, or a materially different evidentiary record.

### Strout v. Albanese

Argued January 24, 2000 | Decided April 3, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment sustaining Maine’s exclusion of sectarian schools from its tuition-assistance program affirmed, 178 F.3d 57 (1st Cir. 1999). ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/178/178.F3d.57.html))  
Area of Law: Free Exercise Clause; Establishment Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Whether excluding otherwise eligible sectarian schools and their students from a generally available tuition-assistance program solely because the schools are religious violates the Free Exercise Clause.
2. Whether the Establishment Clause requires, permits, or forbids that exclusion when assistance reaches schools through independent parental choice.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between neutral civic access and public payment for integrated devotional instruction.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Free Exercise challenge to the sectarian-school exclusion

Holding and operative rule: The Free Exercise Clause does not require Maine to use public tuition payments for devotional instruction, evangelism, religious formation, or an inseparably sectarian curriculum. The State may not, however, exclude religious speakers from a genuinely neutral forum or deny payment for a genuinely severable secular service solely because the provider is religious.

Reasoning: Maine does not prohibit attendance at a religious school or prevent parents from directing their children’s religious education. It defines the publicly financed educational service for which the State will pay and excludes integrated sectarian instruction from that expenditure. Under *Jackson v. Benson*, decided March 16, 1999, the constitutional distinction concerns the designated use of public value, not religious identity alone. Religious organizations remain eligible for neutral civic services and genuinely secular benefits, but the Free Exercise Clause does not convert a decision to fund private education into an obligation to pay a religious institution’s charge for integrated devotional formation. The exclusion therefore does not impose a penalty on private religious exercise within the meaning of the governing law.

##### Question 2: Establishment Clause and parental designation

Holding and operative rule: Public payment for integrated sectarian schooling remains direct public financing of devotional activity even when parents select the school. Parental designation does not convert restricted public value into unrestricted private expenditure.

Reasoning: Maine creates the tuition entitlement, limits it to approved educational providers, and pays the selected school to discharge the State-supported tuition obligation. The parent cannot withdraw the amount for unrestricted use. As *Jackson v. Benson*, decided March 16, 1999, holds, the intervening choice determines the recipient but does not change ownership or the designated use of the funds. *Southworth*, decided March 22, 2000, concerns a broad private-speech program in which viewpoint-neutral allocations facilitate independent student expression; it did not authorize public payment for integrated religious instruction. Maine may therefore maintain the exclusion as applied here. The decision does not authorize hostility toward religion or exclusion from neutral forums and leaves genuinely severable secular services outside the holding. ([Law Resource](https://law.resource.org/pub/us/case/reporter/F3/178/178.F3d.57.html)) `OT_1999CHUNK4.md`

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: A neutral tuition program directed by independent parental choice does not attribute a school’s religious teaching to the State. Once Maine pays tuition for private education, they would hold that categorical exclusion of otherwise eligible schools because their teaching is religious violates free-exercise and equal-treatment principles.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Jackson v. Benson*, decided March 16, 1999: followed; restricted public monetary value remains public financing when parental choice directs it to integrated devotional instruction.

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: followed; equal access to a genuine private-speech forum remains distinct from payment of a provider’s charge for devotional production or instruction.

*Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth*, decided March 22, 2000: distinguished; a viewpoint-neutral private student-speech program does not control direct tuition payment for integrated sectarian schooling.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Maine may continue excluding integrated sectarian schooling from the tuition-assistance program. The judgment does not permit exclusion of religious organizations from neutral civic forums or denial of genuinely severable secular services solely because the provider is religious.

### Carter v. City of Philadelphia

Argued February 14, 2000 | Decided April 10, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal of the District Attorney’s Office claims reversed and the municipal-liability theory remanded for further proceedings, 181 F.3d 339 (3d Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/181/339/586860/))  
Area of Law: Section 1983; municipal liability

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a district attorney acts as a state official or a municipal policymaker when establishing training, supervision, and administrative policies for prosecutors.
2. What showing of deliberate indifference and causal connection is necessary to impose municipal liability for constitutional violations resulting from those policies.

#### Judgment

Affirmed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Breyer | Supports affirmance and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm and remand.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s function-specific classification of the district attorney’s administrative policymaking and its pleading-stage application of deliberate indifference and causation.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: District attorney as municipal administrative policymaker

Holding and operative rule: Under Pennsylvania law and for the functions presented, the Philadelphia District Attorney acts as a municipal policymaker when establishing office-wide training, supervision, record-management, and administrative practices. Advocacy in an individual prosecution may receive a different official-capacity classification and remains subject to its own immunity rules.

Reasoning: Official status under § 1983 is determined function by function. The challenged conduct concerns management of a locally organized office, training and supervision of employees, maintenance of records, and institutional safeguards governing recurring prosecutorial and investigative duties. Philadelphia funds and administers the office in the respects material to those functions, and no identified state official exercises controlling responsibility over the particular policies alleged. Classification of those administrative responsibilities as municipal does not transform every act of courtroom advocacy into municipal policy or eliminate absolute immunity where it otherwise applies. It establishes only that an office-wide administrative policy made by the final local decisionmaker can be attributed to the municipality. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/181/339/586860/))

##### Question 2: Deliberate indifference and causal connection

Holding and operative rule: A municipal training or supervision claim may proceed when the complaint plausibly alleges an underlying constitutional injury, a known pattern or an obvious and recurring constitutional risk, deliberate failure by the responsible policymaker to adopt necessary safeguards, and a direct causal connection between that failure and the injury. Attribution alone and respondeat superior are insufficient.

Reasoning: Carter alleged a pervasive pattern of police and prosecutorial misconduct, institutional failure to train or supervise concerning truthfulness and disclosure, and a resulting wrongful conviction followed by ten years of imprisonment. At the pleading stage and before discovery, he was not required to identify internal directives unavailable to him with evidentiary precision. The allegations identify the recurring situations employees confront, the constitutional consequences of mishandling them, and a history said to make continued inaction deliberately indifferent. Those allegations permit discovery, but they establish no liability. Carter must prove an underlying violation, final policymaking authority, deliberate municipal fault, and that the policy or omission was the moving force behind the injury. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/181/339/586860/)) `OT_1999CHUNK4.md`

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: They would classify the challenged prosecutorial administration as an exercise of authority derived from the Commonwealth or, alternatively, hold the complaint too generalized to identify deliberate municipal fault and direct causation. They warned that allowing the claim to proceed on broad pattern allegations risked converting municipal liability into respondeat superior.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Monell v. Department of Social Services*: applied; a final local policymaker’s office-wide administrative policy may be attributable to the municipality, but employment relationship alone remains insufficient.

*City of Canton v. Harris*: applied and clarified; deliberate indifference may be pleaded through a known pattern or an obvious recurring risk tied to a particular training or supervision failure.

*Board of County Commissioners of Bryan County v. Brown*, decided April 28, 1997: distinguished; its applicant-specific single-hire formulation does not govern a claim based on recurring training and supervision after institutional notice.

*Plakas v. Drinski*, decided January 9, 1995: followed; municipal liability still requires an underlying constitutional injury attributable to municipal policy.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for discovery and proof of the underlying constitutional injury, policymaking authority, deliberate indifference, causation, immunity, and relief.

Effect: Carter’s claims against the municipal office may proceed, but no misconduct or municipal liability is established. The decision creates no respondeat superior liability and does not withdraw absolute immunity from qualifying advocacy within the judicial phase.

### Bond v. United States

Argued February 29, 2000 | Decided April 17, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
denial of suppression and conviction affirmed, 167 F.3d 225 (5th Cir. 1999). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529334/usrep529334.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; searches and seizures

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an officer conducts a Fourth Amendment search by physically manipulating the exterior of a passenger’s soft luggage placed in an overhead bus rack.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.  
Opposing: Scalia, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Scalia | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Deliberately squeezing and manipulating closed soft luggage to discover concealed contents is a search because exposure to ordinary handling does not surrender privacy against exploratory touch.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Investigative manipulation of soft luggage

Holding and operative rule: Deliberately squeezing and manipulating closed soft luggage to discover concealed contents is a Fourth Amendment search. A passenger’s exposure of luggage to routine handling, lifting, repositioning, bumps, or accidental contact does not constitute consent to exploratory tactile examination.

Reasoning: A traveler placing luggage in an overhead rack expects that other passengers and transportation personnel may move or touch it as necessary. That practical exposure does not eliminate the expectation that no one will feel the bag in a calculated manner to determine what lies inside. Agent Cantu’s conduct was not an incidental touch or ordinary repositioning; he squeezed and manipulated the soft bag for the investigative purpose of discerning concealed material. Visual observation from a lawful vantage reveals what has been exposed to view, but exploratory touch can obtain information that the owner kept concealed within a protected effect. The manipulation therefore crossed the constitutional line before Bond later opened the bag. The decision does not govern visual inspection, a canine sniff, routine safety handling, lifting, repositioning, accidental contact, or ordinary jostling by other passengers. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529334/usrep529334.pdf)) `OT_1999CHUNK4.md`

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Scalia: A passenger placing a soft bag in a communal overhead rack expects physical contact from other passengers and transportation personnel. They would hold that the agent’s exterior touch did not exceed the range of contact to which the bag was exposed and therefore did not constitute a search.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Katz v. United States*: applied; a reasonable expectation of privacy may survive exposure to ordinary handling when officers employ materially different investigative manipulation to reveal concealed information.

*United States v. Place*: followed; personal luggage is an effect protected by the Fourth Amendment.

*California v. Ciraolo* and *Florida v. Riley*: distinguished; lawful visual observation from a public vantage does not resolve purposeful tactile exploration of a closed container.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for suppression of the evidence unless the Government establishes a genuinely independent, preserved, and untainted basis for the later search.

Effect: Bond’s conviction may not remain in force on the present search rationale. The lower courts must determine the lawful consequence of suppression and any properly preserved independent-source or other untainted ground.

---
