## Decisions and Dispositions

### Williams v. Taylor (Terry Williams)

Argued October 4, 1999 | Decided April 18, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief denied under an all-reasonable-jurists formulation of 28 U.S.C. § 2254(d)(1), 163 F.3d 860 (4th Cir. 1998). ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529362/usrep529362.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; ineffective assistance of counsel; capital sentencing

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Virginia Supreme Court’s rejection of Williams’s ineffective-assistance claim was contrary to or an unreasonable application of clearly established federal law under § 2254(d)(1).

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy as to Parts I, III, and IV; Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer; Parts I, III, and IV for Stone, O’Connor, and Kennedy |
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Kennedy, Thomas in full; Scalia except the footnote | Supports reversal and remand | Part II defining the “contrary to” and “unreasonable application” clauses |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Kennedy | Supports reversal and remand | Remaining interpretive and application observations |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the governing § 2254(d)(1) interpretation but would deny sentencing relief |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand for sentence-limited conditional habeas relief.

Public role: joins Stevens’s Opinion of the Court as to Parts I, III, and IV and O’Connor’s Opinion of the Court as to Part II.

Announced ground: Stone joins the objective-unreasonableness interpretation of § 2254(d)(1) and the conclusion that the Virginia court assessed prejudice through a materially truncated mitigation record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Meaning of § 2254(d)(1)

Holding and operative rule: A state decision is contrary to clearly established Supreme Court law when it applies a rule opposite to the governing rule or reaches a different result on materially indistinguishable facts. A state court unreasonably applies clearly established law when it identifies the correct rule but applies it in an objectively unreasonable manner; an incorrect application alone does not authorize habeas relief. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas)

Reasoning: Section 2254(d)(1) assigns independent work to the “contrary to” and “unreasonable application” clauses. The Fourth Circuit’s all-reasonable-jurists formulation improperly made relief turn on a subjective survey of possible judicial views. The statutory inquiry is objective. A federal court may not grant the writ merely because it would decide the constitutional question differently, but neither must it defer to a state application falling outside the range permitted by this Court’s governing holdings. Clearly established law consists of the Court’s holdings in force when the state adjudication occurred, rather than dicta or later-developed doctrine. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529362/usrep529362.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

##### Question 1B: Application to the omitted mitigation

Holding and operative rule: The Virginia Supreme Court unreasonably applied *Strickland v. Washington* by evaluating prejudice without giving proper effect to the substantial mitigating evidence counsel failed to investigate and present. Williams is entitled to sentence-limited conditional relief.

Reasoning: Counsel began meaningful sentencing preparation only shortly before the hearing and failed to uncover extensive evidence of severe childhood abuse and neglect, cognitive limitations, head injuries, favorable prison conduct, and Williams’s ability to function safely in a structured environment. The omitted evidence materially altered the sentencing profile presented to the jury. The state court also treated *Lockhart v. Fretwell* as modifying the ordinary *Strickland* prejudice inquiry even though the claim concerned the loss of mitigating evidence Williams had a substantive right to present. Considering the totality of available mitigation against the aggravating evidence, the rejection of prejudice was not merely debatable or incorrect; it was objectively indefensible under *Strickland*. ([tile.loc.gov](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529362/usrep529362.pdf?utm_source=chatgpt.com)) `OT_1999CHUNK5.md`

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy: The AEDPA standard remains materially more deferential than de novo review, but the word “unreasonable” imposes an objective judicial limit and does not permit deference to an application outside the range allowed by governing Supreme Court law.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: They agreed with the Court’s general construction of § 2254(d)(1) but would hold that the Virginia Supreme Court’s prejudice determination remained within the objectively reasonable range and would deny habeas relief.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Strickland v. Washington*: applied; prejudice requires consideration of the totality of available mitigation, including substantial evidence unreasonably left undiscovered or unpresented.

*Lockhart v. Fretwell*: distinguished; its fundamental-fairness discussion does not displace ordinary *Strickland* prejudice when counsel’s deficient performance deprives the defendant of mitigation the law permits him to present.

28 U.S.C. § 2254(d)(1): clarified; an unreasonable application is objectively unreasonable, not merely incorrect, and does not require agreement among all reasonable jurists.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to direct issuance of a conditional writ unless Virginia conducts a constitutionally adequate new sentencing proceeding within a reasonable period or imposes a lawful noncapital sentence.

Effect: Williams’s capital conviction remains intact. His death sentence may not remain in force without a new constitutionally adequate penalty proceeding.

### Williams v. Taylor (Michael Wayne Williams)

Argued February 28, 2000 | Decided April 18, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal evidentiary-hearing relief denied under 28 U.S.C. § 2254(e)(2), 189 F.3d 421 (4th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-420.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; evidentiary hearings

Questions Presented:

1. When has a state prisoner “failed to develop” the factual basis of a claim in state court within § 2254(e)(2), and does the provision bar a hearing despite diligent efforts to obtain the facts?

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, vacated in part, and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stone.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Juror-bias and nondisclosure claims | Vacated and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Determine diligence under the correct fault-based standard and permit an evidentiary hearing if the statutory opening clause does not apply |
| Separate Brady claim | Affirmed, 8-1 | Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Stone | The hearing bar remains because the factual basis was not diligently pursued and the further statutory conditions were not met |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the statutory rule and juror-bias and nondisclosure remand; Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the Brady affirmance | Supports affirmance in part, vacatur in part, and remand | Complete opinion for eight Justices; statutory interpretation and remand portions for Stone |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Stone | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the fault-based interpretation and remand but would require the District Court to determine diligence on the separate nondisclosure theory as well |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to vacate and remand the hearing denial on all claims for a district-court determination of diligent state-court effort; he opposes affirmance of the separate Brady-hearing component.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in part and files a concurrence in part and dissent in part.

Announced ground: A prisoner has not failed to develop a factual basis when reasonable and timely efforts were made but the state process prevented development. Stone would leave the disputed diligence applications to the District Court rather than conclusively bar one claim on the existing appellate record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Meaning of “failed to develop”

Holding and operative rule: Section 2254(e)(2)’s opening clause applies only when a lack of diligence, or greater fault, is attributable to the prisoner or counsel. The absence of a completed state-court record by itself does not establish a failure to develop. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The statutory language directs attention to the prisoner’s efforts, not simply to whether the desired evidence appears in the state record. Diligence ordinarily requires a reasonable attempt, in light of the information available, to investigate and present the claim through the state procedures provided. When a prisoner timely requests factual development and the state process refuses or prevents it, the opening clause is not triggered and the stringent exceptions following it do not govern. Strategic inaction, abandonment of known leads, and unexplained neglect remain failures attributable to the prisoner. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-420.pdf))

##### Question 1B: Juror-bias and nondisclosure claims

Holding and operative rule: The existing record does not establish a prisoner-attributable failure to develop the juror-bias and related nondisclosure claims. The denial of factual development on those claims is vacated and remanded. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Williams sought factual development after learning information that had not been produced through the state proceedings, and the record did not show that a reasonable earlier inquiry would have uncovered the relevant juror relationship and nondisclosure. The lower court treated the absence of state findings as sufficient to activate § 2254(e)(2), without first determining whether Williams had made reasonable and timely efforts appropriate to the facts then available. The District Court must apply the correct diligence inquiry and, if the opening clause is not satisfied, determine under the ordinary hearing rules what development is necessary to address bias, nondisclosure, and materiality. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-420.pdf))

##### Question 1C: Separate Brady claim

Holding and operative rule: Section 2254(e)(2) bars a hearing on the separate Brady theory because available investigative leads were not diligently pursued in state court and Williams does not satisfy the statute’s further conditions for excusing that failure.

Reasoning: The record concerning this theory identified information available during the state proceedings that could have been pursued through ordinary investigation. The failure to follow those leads was attributable to the defense rather than to a state refusal to permit development. Because Williams conceded that the claim could not satisfy the additional requirements imposed once § 2254(e)(2)’s opening clause applies, the federal court lacked authority to conduct an evidentiary hearing on that theory. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-420.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stone concurred in the Court’s fault-based construction and the remand of the juror-bias and nondisclosure claims but dissented from conclusively affirming the Brady-hearing bar. He would have directed the District Court to determine diligence through focused factual development before applying § 2254(e)(2).

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

28 U.S.C. § 2254(e)(2): clarified; “failed to develop” requires lack of diligence or greater fault attributable to the prisoner or counsel.

Federal habeas evidentiary-hearing doctrine: clarified; state-process failure is distinct from prisoner neglect, and only the latter activates § 2254(e)(2)’s heightened statutory conditions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed as to the separate Brady-hearing claim, vacated as to the juror-bias and nondisclosure claims, and remanded for application of the diligence standard and any authorized evidentiary proceedings.

Effect: Williams may seek factual development of the juror-bias and related nondisclosure claims if the District Court finds the required diligent state-court effort. The separate Brady theory remains barred from an evidentiary hearing.

### Powell v. Ridge

Argued February 28, 2000 | Decided April 24, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the Title VI disparate-impact complaint reversed and the private-enforcement theories remanded for further proceedings, 189 F.3d 387 (3d Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/387/526567/))  
Area of Law: Civil rights; Title VI; 42 U.S.C. § 1983

Questions Presented:

1. Whether regulations issued under § 602 of Title VI that prohibit unjustified disparate impacts create federal rights privately enforceable directly or through 42 U.S.C. § 1983.
2. Whether plaintiffs may obtain relief for a funding policy’s disparate racial impact without proving the intentional discrimination required by § 601 itself.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.  
Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between regulatory validity, agency enforcement, and the existence of a privately enforceable statutory right.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Private enforcement of § 602 regulations

Holding and operative rule: Title VI’s disparate-impact regulations do not themselves create a private federal right enforceable either through an implied action or through § 1983. A regulation may validly govern funding recipients without supplying a private right absent rights-creating statutory text.

Reasoning: Section 602 authorizes federal agencies to issue regulations effectuating § 601 and provides administrative mechanisms that include compliance proceedings and potential funding consequences. That delegation permits agencies to prohibit unjustified disparate effects as a condition of federal assistance. It does not state that private persons may sue to enforce every regulatory obligation. Under the current § 1983 rule, a claimant must identify a particular statutory provision intended to benefit the claimant, imposing a binding obligation, and defining a concrete judicially enforceable interest. Section 1983 supplies a remedy for an existing federal right; it does not permit an agency to create the substantive right that Congress omitted. The Third Circuit therefore erred by inferring private enforceability from the validity and beneficial purpose of the regulations. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/387/526567/)) `STANDARDS_AND_TESTS.md`

##### Question 2: Disparate impact without discriminatory purpose

Holding and operative rule: The private right recognized under § 601 reaches intentional discrimination. A plaintiff may not obtain private judicial relief under Title VI solely by proving that a funding policy has a racially disparate impact.

Reasoning: Section 601 prohibits exclusion, denial of benefits, or discrimination on the ground of race, color, or national origin. The private action attached to that prohibition enforces the statutory right Congress enacted. Section 602’s broader regulatory authority and administrative enforcement structure do not alter the elements of the private § 601 claim. A properly pleaded allegation that officials intentionally selected or maintained a funding system because of its racial consequences remains available, as does agency enforcement of valid disparate-impact regulations. The Court decides only that disparate impact, without intentional discrimination and without independent rights-creating statutory text, does not support the private action allowed to proceed below.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Title VI has long been understood to permit private injunctive enforcement, and the § 602 regulations validly define discriminatory practices prohibited in federally assisted programs. They would allow the schoolchildren to seek prospective relief under the regulations and would treat § 1983 as an additional available vehicle.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Guardians Association v. Civil Service Commission*: clarified; recognition of administrative disparate-impact regulation does not itself establish a private right to enforce § 602 regulations.

*Alexander v. Choate*: clarified; the validity of disparate-impact regulations and the availability of agency enforcement do not imply private enforceability.

*Blessing v. Freestone*: applied; § 1983 enforces concrete statutory rights and does not manufacture a right supplied only by regulation.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the claims resting solely on private enforcement of the disparate-impact regulations while permitting any properly preserved intentional-discrimination claim under § 601 to proceed.

Effect: The plaintiffs may not obtain private relief merely by proving disparate impact. The Department of Education may continue enforcing valid § 602 regulations through the administrative mechanisms Congress supplied.

### Nelson v. Adams USA, Inc.

Argued March 27, 2000 | Decided April 25, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below: postjudgment amendment adding Nelson as a party and imposing personal fee liability affirmed, 175 F.3d 1343 (Fed. Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-460.pdf))  
Area of Law: Due process; civil procedure

Questions Presented:

1. Whether due process and the Federal Rules permit a court to add a corporate officer as a party after judgment and simultaneously impose personal liability without an opportunity to respond.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that party status, notice, service, and a meaningful opportunity to contest personal liability must precede judgment.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Simultaneous joinder and personal judgment

Holding and operative rule: A court may not add a person as a party after judgment and simultaneously impose personal liability. The newly added party must receive service of the amended pleading and a meaningful opportunity to answer, assert defenses, obtain appropriate discovery, and contest liability before adjudication.

Reasoning: Rule 15 governs both permission to amend and the procedure following amendment. When an amendment adds an adverse party, the new party’s response period begins after service of the amended pleading. Nelson was not a party when Adams sought to alter the judgment, no amended pleading was served on him, and personal liability was imposed at the same moment joinder was permitted. Knowledge that an amendment had been requested did not substitute for party status or a lawful opportunity to defend. Appellate review after entry of judgment could not cure the absence of a pre-judgment hearing on the merits of Nelson’s individual liability. The procedure violated both the Federal Rules and due process. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-460.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Federal Rules of Civil Procedure 12 and 15: applied; a newly added defendant’s opportunity to respond begins upon service of the pleading naming that defendant.

*Mullane v. Central Hanover Bank & Trust Co.*: followed; notice and a meaningful opportunity to be heard must precede an adjudication imposing personal liability.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is reversed, Nelson’s personal judgment is vacated, and the case is remanded for any amended claim to proceed through service, responsive pleading, defenses, appropriate discovery, and adjudication.

Effect: Nelson is not adjudged immune from alter-ego, successor-liability, or another properly pleaded theory. Respondents may pursue such a theory only through procedures affording him a fair opportunity to contest it.

### Slack v. McDaniel

Argued October 4, 1999; reargued March 29, 2000 | Decided April 26, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: certificate of probable cause denied following procedural dismissal of the renewed federal habeas petition, unreported. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529473/usrep529473.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal habeas corpus; appellate procedure

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Slack’s renewed petition was second or successive.
2. Which certificate regime governed his post-AEDPA appeal.
3. What showing is required when habeas relief was denied on procedural grounds.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Ginsburg in full; Stevens, Souter, Breyer as to Parts I, III, and IV; Scalia and Thomas as to Parts I and II | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, O’Connor, and Ginsburg; Parts I, III, and IV for Stevens, Souter, and Breyer; Parts I and II for Scalia and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Stevens | Souter, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Disagrees with the post-AEDPA certificate-regime holding but joins the remaining opinion and judgment |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the temporal certificate holding but opposes the treatment of the renewed petition and resulting reversal |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s first-petition rule and its two-component certificate inquiry following a procedural dismissal.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Renewed petition after exhaustion

Holding and operative rule: A petition refiled after a mixed petition was dismissed without prejudice solely to permit exhaustion is a continuation of the prisoner’s first federal habeas effort and is not second or successive.

Reasoning: *Rose v. Lundy* requires dismissal of a mixed petition but contemplates a return to federal court after exhaustion. Treating that return as a second or successive petition would convert compliance with the exhaustion requirement into forfeiture of federal review. Slack’s first petition was dismissed without adjudication of any claim and without a condition restricting the claims he could present after exhaustion. The renewed petition therefore receives first-petition treatment. States may enforce proper procedural bars, and federal courts may use ordinary rules against deliberate withholding, disobedience of dismissal conditions, duplication, and abuse. Those safeguards do not justify altering the established meaning of second or successive. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529473/usrep529473.pdf))

##### Question 2: Certificate regime governing the appeal

Holding and operative rule: An appeal initiated after AEDPA’s effective date is governed by the certificate-of-appealability provisions of § 2253(c), even when the underlying district-court habeas proceeding began earlier. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Ginsburg)

Reasoning: Section 2254 governs entitlement to district-court habeas relief, while § 2253 governs the distinct appellate step. An appellate proceeding begins when the prisoner seeks authorization to appeal. Because Slack initiated appellate review after AEDPA became effective, the Ninth Circuit should have treated his notice of appeal as an application for a certificate of appealability rather than applying the former certificate-of-probable-cause regime. This temporal rule does not retroactively alter the substantive law governing claims filed in the District Court before AEDPA. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529473/usrep529473.pdf))

##### Question 3: Certificate after a procedural dismissal

Holding and operative rule: When a district court dismisses a habeas petition on procedural grounds without reaching the constitutional claim, a certificate should issue only if reasonable jurists could debate both whether the petition states a valid constitutional claim and whether the procedural ruling was correct.

Reasoning: Section 2253(c) requires a substantial showing of the denial of a constitutional right, while permitting appellate correction of an erroneous procedural ruling that prevents adjudication of such a right. The inquiry therefore has two threshold components. A court may address first whichever component is more apparent. If the procedural bar is plainly valid, no certificate issues regardless of the asserted merits; if the procedural ruling is reasonably debatable, the applicant must also show a debatable constitutional claim. Slack established that reasonable jurists could dispute the second-or-successive and abuse-of-the-writ rulings, but the remaining certificate inquiry must be completed on remand. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529473/usrep529473.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Souter and Breyer: They would apply the former certificate-of-probable-cause regime to an appeal arising from a pre-AEDPA district-court proceeding. They joined the two-component threshold standard, the first-petition rule, and the judgment.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: They joined the conclusion that § 2253(c) governed the appeal but would treat the renewed filing, insofar as it added previously omitted claims, under the rules governing successive or abusive petitions and would not reverse the procedural disposition on the Court’s theory.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Rose v. Lundy*: clarified; dismissal of a mixed petition for exhaustion ordinarily preserves treatment of a properly renewed petition as the first federal petition.

*Stewart v. Martinez-Villareal*: followed; second or successive is a habeas term of art and does not mechanically include every later filing.

*Barefoot v. Estelle*: applied; reasonable debatability supplies the certificate threshold, with separate constitutional and procedural components after a procedural dismissal.

*Lindh v. Murphy*: clarified; district-court merits provisions and appellate certificate provisions attach to their respective procedural stages.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for treatment of Slack’s notice of appeal as a certificate-of-appealability application under the two-component standard.

Effect: Slack’s renewed petition may not be dismissed as second or successive merely because his earlier mixed petition was dismissed for exhaustion. Timeliness, procedural default, deliberate withholding, compliance with dismissal conditions, and other independently valid barriers remain available.

### Carmell v. Texas

Argued November 30, 1999 | Decided May 1, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Court of Appeals of Texas, Second District; judgment below: convictions sustained under the amended corroboration rule, 963 S.W.2d 833 (Tex. App. 1998). ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529513/usrep529513.pdf))  
Area of Law: Ex Post Facto Clause; criminal evidence

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Texas violated the Ex Post Facto Clause by retroactively reducing the evidence legally sufficient to convict.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Ginsburg | O’Connor, Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse the affected counts and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the Ex Post Facto Clause preserves the legal quantum of evidence required for conviction when the offense was committed.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Retroactive reduction of legally required proof

Holding and operative rule: A State may not retrospectively make conviction possible on a lesser quantum of evidence than the law required when the offense occurred. A statutory change eliminating a corroboration or timely-outcry condition that previously governed legal sufficiency falls within the Ex Post Facto Clause.

Reasoning: The former Texas rule did more than regulate the manner in which admissible evidence was introduced. For the affected offenses, it made the victim’s testimony legally insufficient unless corroborated or accompanied by a timely outcry. The amendment permitted conviction on that testimony alone. Applying the amendment to pre-enactment conduct therefore required less evidence to sustain conviction than the law demanded at the time of the offenses, fitting the fourth category identified in *Calder v. Bull*. Changes governing witness competency or ordinary courtroom procedure remain distinguishable because they do not alter the minimum amount or degree of proof legally necessary for conviction. Counts 7 through 10 were sustained under the reduced requirement and must be reconsidered under the former rule. ([Library of Congress Tile](https://tile.loc.gov/storage-services/service/ll/usrep/usrep529/usrep529513/usrep529513.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Ginsburg, joined by O’Connor and Kennedy: The amendment altered an evidentiary rule governing when testimony could support conviction rather than an element, burden of proof, or quantum of evidence within the Ex Post Facto Clause. They would affirm under the treatment of competency and admissibility changes in *Hopt* and *Thompson*.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Calder v. Bull*: reaffirmed; the Ex Post Facto Clause prohibits retrospective laws permitting conviction on less evidence than previously required.

*Hopt v. Territory of Utah*: distinguished; a witness-competency change differs from a rule defining the minimum proof legally sufficient to convict.

*Thompson v. Missouri*: distinguished; expanded admissibility does not control a statute reducing the quantum of evidence necessary for conviction.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals of Texas is reversed as to counts 7 through 10, and the case is remanded for application of the corroboration or timely-outcry requirement in force when the offenses occurred.

Effect: The separable convictions not dependent on the retroactive amendment remain undisturbed. The Texas courts shall determine the lawful consequence for the affected counts under the former sufficiency rule.

### Christensen v. Harris County

Argued February 23, 2000 | Decided May 1, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: Harris County’s compelled-use compensatory-time policy sustained, 158 F.3d 241 (5th Cir. 1998). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-576.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fair Labor Standards Act; administrative deference

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the FLSA prohibits compelled use of accrued compensatory time absent an employee agreement.
2. What weight the Department of Labor’s contrary opinion letter receives.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas.  
Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter in full; Scalia except Part III | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, and Souter; statutory merits portions for Scalia |
| Concurrence | Souter | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Reserves the Secretary’s authority to issue a valid regulation limiting compelled use |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Rejects the Court’s classification of the opinion letter but finds the agency interpretation unreasonable |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg, Breyer | Would reverse | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Ginsburg | Would reverse | Agency-deference ground |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s statutory holding and its treatment of the informal opinion letter as persuasive rather than controlling.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Compelled use of compensatory time

Holding and operative rule: Section 207(o) creates no employee veto over a public employer’s direction to use accrued compensatory time when no statute, valid regulation, or binding agreement prohibits the policy.

Reasoning: The FLSA identifies how compensatory time may be earned, preserved, used at an employee’s request, and converted to cash, but it does not prohibit an employer from scheduling its use. The employees’ agreement governed the substitution of compensatory time for cash overtime; it did not create an implied restriction on every later scheduling decision. Harris County’s policy reduced accumulated liability while providing the paid leave represented by the accrued time. The ruling does not authorize the County to deny an employee’s protected request to use time within a reasonable period absent undue disruption, confiscate accrued value, exceed statutory accrual limits, or violate a controlling agreement. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-576.pdf))

##### Question 2: Weight of the opinion letter

Holding and operative rule: An interpretation contained in an informal opinion letter lacking the force of law does not receive *Chevron* deference. It receives weight only to the extent its reasoning, consistency, and expertise make it persuasive. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, and Thomas)

Reasoning: The Department’s view was not adopted through notice-and-comment rulemaking, formal adjudication, or another procedure carrying delegated legal force. It therefore could not control merely because it represented the agency’s litigating or administrative position. Nor could deference to an agency’s interpretation of its own regulation alter the result, because the regulation did not contain genuine ambiguity requiring the meaning asserted in the letter. Informal guidance may still assist a court according to the care, consistency, and persuasive force of its analysis. Here the letter identified no statutory or regulatory prohibition capable of supporting its employee-agreement requirement. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-576.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter: The decision holds only that current law does not prohibit the County’s policy. It does not foreclose the Secretary of Labor from adopting a valid regulation restricting compelled use within delegated authority.

Scalia: The authoritative position of the Secretary may qualify for controlling deference even when expressed outside notice-and-comment rulemaking, but the interpretation advanced here is not a reasonable reading of the statute. He therefore joined the statutory merits analysis and judgment.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg and Breyer: The statutory exception permitting compensatory time depends on an antecedent agreement, and the employer may not use the arrangement in a manner inconsistent with the employee’s agreed control over accrued leave.

Breyer, joined by Ginsburg: The Department’s construction was reasonable, reflected the responsible agency’s authoritative position, and should have governed the disputed policy.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: clarified; informal guidance lacking the force of law does not receive Chevron deference.

*Skidmore v. Swift & Co.*: followed; informal agency interpretations receive persuasion-based weight according to their reasoning and consistency.

*Auer v. Robbins*: distinguished; deference to an agency’s interpretation of its own regulation does not apply when the regulation is unambiguous or the asserted meaning is inconsistent with its text.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Harris County may continue the challenged compelled-use policy subject to the FLSA’s express protections and any binding agreement or valid regulation. The employees retain the statutory value of accrued compensatory time and all independently protected use and payment rights.

### Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections

Argued March 6, 2000 | Decided May 8, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit; judgment below: Equal Protection judgment and injunction affirmed, with deletion of separate free-association wording, 174 F.3d 305 (3d Cir. 1999) (en banc). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/174/305/519099/))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection; elections; political association

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Pennsylvania violates the Equal Protection Clause by permitting major parties to cross-nominate candidates for specified local offices while prohibiting minor parties from making the same cross-nominations, notwithstanding *Timmons*’ approval of a generally applicable anti-fusion rule.
2. What scrutiny governs an election law that simultaneously burdens voting and associational rights and facially discriminates between major and minor political parties.
3. Whether the State must justify the unequal treatment itself, rather than merely identify interests sufficient to justify a neutral prohibition on fusion.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between a neutral anti-fusion rule and facially unequal access to a fusion mechanism the State has chosen to permit.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Unequal access to fusion

Holding and operative rule: A State that permits cross-nomination may not make that electoral opportunity available to major parties while categorically denying it to minor parties without a constitutionally sufficient justification for the distinction.

Reasoning: Pennsylvania’s provisions do not merely regulate whether fusion will exist. For five local offices they authorize major parties to share nominees while excluding minor parties from the same mechanism. That allocation burdens the minor party’s ability to select a willing standard-bearer, communicate its endorsement through the ballot, and combine electoral support with another party. The Constitution creates no freestanding entitlement to fusion, and Pennsylvania remains free to prohibit it evenhandedly. Once the State permits fusion, however, it must explain why the asserted harm arises from minor-party cross-nomination but not from the same activity by major parties. The record supplies no such distinction. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/174/305/519099/)) `HOLDINGS.md`

##### Question 2: Governing election-law review

Holding and operative rule: *Anderson-Burdick* governs the practical voting and associational burden, while equal protection requires focused review of the facial party-status classification. The demanded fit increases with the burden and discrimination shown; minor parties are not designated a suspect class.

Reasoning: Election regulations are assessed through the character and magnitude of their practical burdens, the legitimacy and weight of the State’s interests, and the extent to which those interests require the challenged rule. Severe burdens require narrow tailoring to a compelling interest, while modest and evenhanded burdens may be sustained by important regulatory interests. Pennsylvania’s rule is not evenhanded. The party-status classification increases the burden on minor parties and requires the State to connect its asserted interests to the unequal allocation actually enacted. No separate automatic scrutiny label displaces that burden-sensitive inquiry, and the Court does not create a new suspect classification.

##### Question 3: Justification of the classification

Holding and operative rule: The State must justify the unequal treatment itself. Interests capable of supporting a neutral fusion ban do not sustain selective access unless they explain why minor-party fusion presents a materially different regulatory danger.

Reasoning: Ballot integrity, prevention of candidate manipulation, electoral stability, and encouragement of independent candidacies can be legitimate interests. They do not explain Pennsylvania’s asymmetry. Major-party fusion can produce the same shared candidacies, combined labels, and reduction in the number of competing candidates. Indeed, allowing major-party cross-nomination while excluding minor parties may strengthen established parties and make independent competition more difficult. Because the proffered interests would support an across-the-board rule, but do not justify the major-party exception, the classification violates equal protection. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/174/305/519099/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: Pennsylvania could rationally distinguish between candidates selected through major-party primaries and candidates nominated later through minor-party procedures, including to protect primary voters’ expectations and orderly nomination sequencing. They would treat the burden as modest and uphold the classification under deferential election-administration review.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Timmons v. Twin Cities Area New Party*: clarified; approval of a reasonable and nondiscriminatory anti-fusion rule does not sustain facially selective access to fusion.

*Anderson v. Celebrezze* and *Burdick v. Takushi*: applied; the Court evaluates both the practical electoral burden and the fit of the interests asserted for the actual classification.

*Williams v. Rhodes*: followed; a State may not give established parties an unjustified electoral advantage through substantially unequal ballot rules.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Third Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Pennsylvania must afford minor parties the same cross-nomination opportunity it affords major parties for the specified local offices. It may replace the current arrangement with a neutral anti-fusion rule applicable to all parties.

### Gillespie v. City of Indianapolis

Argued March 20, 2000 | Decided May 9, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of Gillespie’s constitutional challenge to 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(9) affirmed, 185 F.3d 693 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/185/693/609166/))  
Area of Law: Federalism; Commerce Clause; anti-commandeering

Questions Presented:

1. Whether applying 18 U.S.C. § 922(g)(9) to state and local employees who would otherwise carry firearms in performing their official duties violates the Tenth Amendment.
2. Whether Congress acted within its Commerce Clause power in enacting § 922(g)(9).

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Thomas as to the Tenth Amendment holding | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for eight Justices; Tenth Amendment portion for Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Thomas | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the direct-regulation holding but rejects the Commerce Clause basis |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s application of *Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, decided March 13, 2000, to a local officer whose inability to carry a firearm follows from direct federal regulation rather than compelled state administration.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Tenth Amendment application to a local officer

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(g)(9) directly regulates individual firearm possession and does not commandeer state or local government merely because the regulated person is a police officer. It imposes no duty on the State or municipality to administer or enforce federal law. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Federal authorities enforce the disability through federal law. Indiana and Indianapolis remain responsible for structuring their police forces and determining the ordinary qualifications of officers, but they may not authorize conduct that valid federal law prohibits. The City’s requirement that every officer be trained and equipped to carry a firearm, combined with Gillespie’s federal disability, made him unable to satisfy the local position’s requirements. That practical consequence is not a federal command to investigate Gillespie, prosecute him, discharge him, or administer a federal program. *Fraternal Order of Police* therefore controls the institutional question. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/185/693/609166/))

##### Question 2: Commerce Clause authority

Holding and operative rule: Section 922(g)(9) is a valid fitted possession restriction within Congress’s regulation of the interstate firearms market when the statute’s offense-specific commerce connection is genuinely established. A nominal jurisdictional phrase or remote interstate history performing no present constitutional work is insufficient.

Reasoning: *Fraternal Order of Police* applied the simulated market-regulation rule of *United States v. Hale*: Congress may regulate possession when possession and transfer are functionally connected within a valid interstate product-market regime and exclusion of possession would permit retention or acquisition that defeats the market restriction. Section 922(g)(9) also requires an offense-specific commerce nexus. Gillespie’s facial and employment-based challenge does not present a criminal record in which the Government relied solely on a firearm’s remote historical movement. The statute therefore remains valid, while the sufficiency of commerce proof in a materially different prosecution remains open. The Seventh Circuit’s broader statement that one prior interstate journey is invariably enough is not adopted. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/185/693/609166/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas concurred that § 922(g)(9) directly regulates Gillespie rather than commandeering Indianapolis, but dissented from the Commerce Clause holding. He would not permit Congress to reach wholly intrastate official possession on the basis of the broader firearms-market relationship or a firearm’s remote movement across state lines.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Fraternal Order of Police v. United States*, decided March 13, 2000: followed; § 922(g)(9) directly regulates covered possessors and remains subject to a genuine offense-specific commerce connection.

*Printz v. United States*: distinguished; the statute assigns no federal administrative or enforcement obligation to state or local officers.

*United States v. Hale*: applied; possession may be included in a fitted interstate product-market regime when the anti-evasion relationship performs real work.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Gillespie obtains no reinstatement or other relief on the Tenth Amendment or Commerce Clause theories before the Court. The decision does not resolve an independent Second Amendment claim or hold that remote interstate movement alone is sufficient commerce proof in every prosecution.

### Fischer v. United States

Argued February 22, 2000 | Decided May 15, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit; judgment below: convictions under 18 U.S.C. § 666 sustained on the ground that the defrauded hospital received federal program benefits, 168 F.3d 1273 (11th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-667.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; statutory interpretation; Medicare

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Medicare payments are federal “benefits” received by a participating hospital within § 666(b).

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Stone announces no ground beyond the Court’s program-specific conclusion that continuing Medicare payments materially support participating hospital operations and advance an ongoing federal programmatic purpose.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Medicare payments as program benefits

Holding and operative rule: An organization receives benefits within § 666(b) when continuing federal payments materially support its operations and advance an ongoing federal programmatic purpose. The participating hospital received such benefits through Medicare.

Reasoning: The inquiry does not turn solely on whether federal money can be traced to the recipient or whether patients are the program’s primary beneficiaries. Medicare establishes a continuing regulated relationship with participating hospitals, imposes conditions directed to the quality and availability of care, and supplies substantial payments that support the institutions furnishing program services. Protecting hospitals from fraud and bribery advances the federal interest in maintaining their capacity to provide the care the program requires. That structure is materially different from ordinary procurement, a one-time purchase at market price, or a transaction lacking a continuing programmatic relationship. The Court therefore classifies the hospital as an organization receiving federal benefits without adopting the Government’s broader proposition that every federal payment is a benefit. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-529/pdf/USREPORTS-529-667.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Medicare’s benefits belong to eligible elderly and disabled patients, while hospitals receive reimbursement for services rendered through an ordinary compensatory transaction. They would not treat provider payments as organizational benefits under the criminal statute.

#### Doctrinal Effect

18 U.S.C. § 666(b): clarified; program benefits may extend to a participating organization when federal payments support its operations and serve an ongoing federal programmatic purpose.

Federal program-bribery coverage: limited; federal origin alone does not convert ordinary procurement, one-time market transactions, or payments lacking a programmatic relationship into benefits.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eleventh Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Fischer’s convictions remain intact as against the contention that the hospital received no federal program benefits. Independent count-specific, sentencing, or other preserved issues remain governed by the proceedings below.
