Source corrections: The supplied *Vermont Agency* comparator reports a 9-0 historical judgment, but the official report shows a 7-2 judgment, with Stevens and Souter dissenting. The supplied *Raleigh* posture states that the lower courts shifted the burden of proof to the State, but the official report shows that the Seventh Circuit ruled for the Department and kept the burden on the trustee. The official historical facts control; neither correction changes Stone’s approved legal position or disposition. `OT_1999CHUNK6.md` `OT_1999CHUNK6.md` ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/765/))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Morrison; Brzonkala v. Morrison

Argued January 11, 2000 | Decided May 15, 2000  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
the federal civil remedy for gender-motivated violence held unconstitutional, 169 F.3d 820 (4th Cir. 1999) (en banc).  
Area of Law: Federalism; Commerce Clause; Fourteenth Amendment

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress had Commerce Clause authority to create the civil remedy in 42 U.S.C. § 13981 for victims of gender-motivated violence.
2. Whether § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment authorized that private remedy.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas in full; Stevens and Ginsburg as to the Fourteenth Amendment § 5 holding | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for five Justices; § 5 holding for Stevens and Ginsburg |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Commerce Clause boundary |
| Dissent | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Would reverse | Commerce Clause issue |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens in full; Souter and Ginsburg as to the aggregation analysis | Would reverse | Commerce analysis and Breyer’s separate § 5 position |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: Court author.

Announced ground: The economic consequences documented by Congress do not establish direct interference with an interstate market, an anti-evasion component of a valid interstate regulatory system, or a material interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize. Section 5 also does not authorize a nationwide damages action against a private assailant untethered to unconstitutional state conduct.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Commerce Clause authority

Holding and operative rule: Congress may not create a general federal civil remedy for gender-motivated violence merely because the aggregate consequences of that violence affect employment, productivity, medical spending, consumption, and interstate travel. The regulated conduct must satisfy one of the commerce categories recognized in *United States v. Lopez*, decided April 26, 1995.

Reasoning: Congress assembled extensive evidence that gender-motivated violence produces grave personal and national economic harm. Legislative findings, however, do not remove the need for a constitutionally sufficient connection between the regulated conduct and interstate commerce. Section 13981 neither governs an interstate transaction nor protects a channel, instrumentality, person, or thing in interstate commerce. It is not an anti-evasion component of a larger interstate regulatory program, and the record does not establish an interstate externality that individual States cannot reasonably internalize. Its theory instead moves from private violent conduct through lost work, medical expense, reduced consumption, and other secondary economic consequences. Accepting that chain as sufficient would leave no principled limit on federal regulation of ordinary violent crime. The holding does not make noneconomic conduct categorically immune from commerce regulation when a direct market relationship, fitted anti-evasion function, or qualifying interstate externality is actually present. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/598/))

##### Question 2: Fourteenth Amendment enforcement authority

Holding and operative rule: Section 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment does not authorize Congress to impose damages liability on a private assailant for private violence when the operative remedy is not directed to a State, subdivision, or state actor responsible for the constitutional violation. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, and Ginsburg)

Reasoning: The Fourteenth Amendment’s substantive commands regulate state action. Congress may prevent and remedy unconstitutional discrimination by States and state officials, and it may adopt fitted measures addressing official obstruction or discriminatory administration. Section 13981 instead imposes liability on the private person who committed the alleged assault, without requiring state participation, discriminatory state action, or a relationship between the defendant and the governmental failure Congress documented. The extensive evidence of unequal state protection establishes the seriousness of the governmental problem but does not transform every private assailant into a constitutional violator. The remedy therefore fails the source requirement stated in *City of Boerne v. Flores*, decided June 25, 1997. Congress remains free to regulate commerce-linked offenses and to enact remedies directed at discriminatory States, subdivisions, and officials.

#### Concurrences

Thomas: The statute exceeds the commerce power even under the Court’s existing framework. He would reconsider the substantial-effects doctrine to the extent it permits federal regulation of activity that is neither commerce nor an indispensable component of regulating commerce.

#### Dissents

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Congress rationally found that gender-motivated violence has substantial aggregate economic consequences, and the Court should defer to that legislative judgment. They would sustain § 13981 under the Commerce Clause.

Breyer, joined by Stevens and in relevant part by Souter and Ginsburg: Economic and noneconomic activity cannot be separated through rigid categories when their aggregate consequences are documented. Breyer also would permit broader § 5 remedies addressing systemic failures of state protection.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Lopez*, decided April 26, 1995: applied and clarified; general downstream effects on productivity, employment, spending, and medical costs do not replace direct market interference, a fitted anti-evasion relationship, or a qualifying noninternalizable interstate externality.

*City of Boerne v. Flores*, decided June 25, 1997: applied; § 5 enforcement must operate against unconstitutional state conduct or a sufficiently connected governmental actor and may not impose an untethered constitutional damages remedy on a private assailant.

*Civil Rights Cases*, 109 U.S. 3 (1883), and *United States v. Harris*, 106 U.S. 629 (1883): followed; the Fourteenth Amendment’s substantive prohibitions remain directed to state action.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed, and the claims under 42 U.S.C. § 13981 shall remain dismissed.

Effect: Brzonkala may not maintain the federal civil-remedy claim against the private defendants under § 13981. State criminal and civil remedies, federal regulation of independently commerce-linked conduct, and federal remedies directed to unconstitutional state action remain unaffected.

### Valdespino v. Alamo Heights Independent School District

Argued March 21, 2000 | Decided May 16, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
judgment for the school district on the § 2 vote-dilution claim affirmed, 168 F.3d 848 (5th Cir. 1999). ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/valdespino-v-alamo-heights-891306310))  
Area of Law: Voting Rights Act; elections; vote dilution

Questions Presented:

1. Whether, when a structural § 2 vote-dilution claim depends on a proposed single-member district, the first *Gingles* precondition requires the protected group, or a coalition qualifying under the persuasive coalition rule in *Nixon v. Kent County*, 76 F.3d 1381 (6th Cir. 1996) (en banc), to constitute more than 50 percent of citizen voting-age population in a reasonably configured district, or whether reliable crossover support may substitute for that numerical majority.
2. Whether citizen voting-age population is the proper measure, and whether reliable current demographic evidence may displace decennial census figures when conditions have materially changed.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Thomas | Scalia | Concurs in the judgment | Broader statutory ground |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Souter | Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Citizen voting-age population and current-evidence holdings; rejection of the numerical-majority requirement |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins Kennedy’s plurality in full.

Announced ground: The ordinary first-*Gingles* route requires the protected group, or a qualifying coalition, to exceed 50 percent of citizen voting-age population in a reasonably configured district. Reliable current demographic evidence may displace stale census figures under the ordinary civil preponderance standard.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by Kennedy. No opinion of the Court.

##### Question 1: Numerical majority under the first Gingles precondition

Holding and operative rule: Controls under *Marks*: The ordinary first-*Gingles* route requires proof that the protected group, or a legally qualifying coalition, exceeds 50 percent of citizen voting-age population in a reasonably configured demonstration district. Reliable crossover support does not substitute for that threshold showing.

Reasoning: The first precondition asks whether the challenged electoral structure prevents a geographically compact minority community from exercising an electoral opportunity it could possess in a reasonably drawn single-member district. A numerical majority supplies the administrable boundary between an asserted ability-to-elect claim and an influence claim dependent on votes from outside the protected group. Crossover evidence remains relevant to later statutory analysis, including whether minority voters already possess meaningful electoral opportunity, but it cannot create the first-predicate majority. Kennedy, Stone, and O’Connor adopt that rule under existing § 2 precedent. Scalia and Thomas would reject districting-based vote-dilution liability more broadly and therefore necessarily agree that a claimant failing the numerical-majority rule cannot prevail. The plurality’s rule is the narrower ground supporting the judgment and controls under *Marks*. Crossing the threshold establishes neither liability nor a right to proportional representation or maximization.

##### Question 2: CVAP and current demographic evidence

Holding and operative rule: Citizen voting-age population is the proper denominator for the first *Gingles* inquiry when citizenship is material. Reliable current evidence may displace decennial census figures upon proof by a preponderance of the evidence; no clear-and-convincing standard applies. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The inquiry concerns the number of citizens legally eligible by age to vote, not total population or voting-age residents who cannot participate in the election. Decennial census information is ordinarily probative and useful, but it is not conclusive when credible evidence establishes material demographic change before trial. Alamo Heights conceded that the proposed district contained a Hispanic citizen voting-age majority under 1990 census data, then presented evidence that later development and population change had eliminated that majority by the 1997 trial. The district court credited that current evidence. Although any suggestion of a clear-and-convincing burden is rejected, the supported findings satisfy the ordinary preponderance standard and leave the proposed district below the required threshold. ([vLex](https://case-law.vlex.com/vid/valdespino-v-alamo-heights-891306310))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Section 2 should not authorize federal courts to restructure districting systems through vote-dilution theories of this kind. Because the plaintiffs fail even under the Court’s existing first-*Gingles* framework, they concur in the judgment.

Souter, joined by Stevens, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Citizen voting-age population and reliable current evidence are appropriate, but a cohesive minority population able to elect its preferred candidate with dependable crossover support should not be excluded categorically. They would reverse and remand for a complete functional inquiry.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Thornburg v. Gingles*: clarified under *Marks*; the ordinary first precondition requires a protected-group or qualifying-coalition majority exceeding 50 percent of citizen voting-age population in the demonstration district.

*Johnson v. De Grandy*, decided June 30, 1994: clarified; satisfaction of a numerical first precondition does not establish dilution, require proportional representation, or compel maximization.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The at-large Alamo Heights school-board system remains undisturbed by this § 2 claim. The decision does not foreclose a claim supported by a demonstration district exceeding 50 percent protected-group or qualifying-coalition CVAP, and it does not decide a distinct influence theory.

### Geier v. American Honda Motor Co.

Argued December 7, 1999 | Decided May 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below:  
the general no-airbag tort theory held preempted, 166 F.3d 1236 (D.C. Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/861/))  
Area of Law: Federal preemption; motor-vehicle safety; tort law

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a general no-airbag tort claim conflicts with FMVSS 208’s phased mix of passive restraints despite the Safety Act’s savings clause.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s source-specific conclusion that the broad no-airbag duty would defeat FMVSS 208’s deliberate mixed-restraint transition.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Conflict with the federal mixed-restraint objective

Holding and operative rule: The Safety Act’s savings clause preserves common-law liability generally and defeats categorical express preemption, but it does not preserve a tort duty that would obstruct a deliberate federal regulatory objective. The general claim that every covered 1987 automobile required a driver’s-side airbag conflicts with FMVSS 208’s phased mixed-restraint policy.

Reasoning: FMVSS 208 did more than establish a minimum safety floor while leaving manufacturers free to exceed it. During the relevant period, the standard deliberately required a gradual introduction of passive restraints while permitting manufacturers to employ a mix of airbags, automatic belts, and other approved technologies. The agency used that mix to obtain safety data, encourage technological development, manage public acceptance, and prevent premature commitment to a single design. A state-law duty treating every non-airbag vehicle as defective would replace that federal choice with an immediate all-airbag mandate. The savings clause preserves tort claims generally, but it does not require courts to enforce a duty that defeats an affirmative objective embedded in a valid federal standard. The holding reaches only the broad no-airbag theory. Noncompliance, defective installation, deficient performance, risk-specific design, and genuinely model-specific claims remain available when their duties do not obstruct the federal mixed-fleet objective. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/861/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter, Thomas, and Ginsburg: The savings clause preserves common-law liability, and FMVSS 208 permitted a range of restraint systems without granting manufacturers immunity for choosing one negligently. They would require clearer enacted evidence before treating a federal minimum standard as displacing state tort law.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Freightliner Corp. v. Myrick*, decided April 18, 1995: clarified; regulatory absence or agency preference alone does not preempt state law, but an affirmative federal objective embodied in an operative standard may create conflict preemption.

*United States v. Locke*, decided March 6, 2000: followed; federal displacement must be tied to enacted law, a valid regulation, or an affirmative federal determination.

Safety Act savings clause: clarified; preservation of common-law liability does not immunize a state duty that directly obstructs a specific federal regulatory objective.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed as to the general no-airbag tort theory.

Effect: Geier may not impose a categorical state-law duty requiring a driver’s-side airbag in every vehicle covered by the relevant phase of FMVSS 208. The decision does not create general compliance immunity or foreclose claims resting on duties compatible with the federal mixed-restraint objective.

### Jones v. United States

Argued March 21, 2000 | Decided May 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
federal arson conviction affirmed, 178 F.3d 479 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/848/))  
Area of Law: Federal criminal law; Commerce Clause; statutory interpretation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether an owner-occupied dwelling used only for ordinary family life is property used in interstate commerce or commerce-affecting activity under 18 U.S.C. § 844(i).

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | Thomas | Supports reversal and remand | Constitutional-avoidance ground |
| Concurrence | Thomas | Scalia | Supports reversal and remand | Commerce Clause boundary |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the statutory phrase used in commerce requires active commercial employment of the building.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Active commercial use under § 844(i)

Holding and operative rule: An owner-occupied dwelling used only for ordinary family life is not property used in interstate commerce or in an activity affecting interstate commerce within § 844(i). Passive, past, or ordinary incidents of home ownership do not satisfy the statute’s active-use requirement.

Reasoning: The word used directs attention to the function the building itself was serving when the arson occurred. Jones’s dwelling was occupied as a private family home and was not then rented, operated as a business, or employed in a materially mixed commercial activity. Its mortgage, insurance, receipt of utilities, out-of-state building materials, and former rental status connected the property to commercial transactions but did not make its present use commercial. Treating those ordinary incidents as sufficient would convert nearly every residence into federal commercial property and would read the statutory limiting language out of the Act. A currently rented building, business premises, or residence materially employed for commercial activity presents a different case. Because the statute does not reach this dwelling, the Court does not decide the outer constitutional extent of Congress’s arson power. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/848/))

#### Concurrences

Stevens, joined by Thomas: The narrow statutory construction also avoids the serious constitutional question that would arise if ordinary arson of every private home became a federal commerce offense.

Thomas, joined by Scalia: The Commerce Clause may impose a stricter boundary than the Court needs to decide. They would reserve whether Congress could constitutionally reach a private residence through a more explicit statute.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Russell v. United States*: distinguished; rental property is actively employed in a commercial market, while an owner-occupied family home is not.

*United States v. Lopez*, decided April 26, 1995: applied as constitutional-avoidance context; no constitutional holding is necessary because the statute does not reach the conduct.

*United States v. Bass*: followed; federal criminal statutes are not read to alter the federal-state balance through attenuated commercial connections absent clear enacted language.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate Jones’s conviction under 18 U.S.C. § 844(i) and conduct any remaining lawful proceedings.

Effect: The federal arson conviction cannot stand. The Court does not adjudicate other counts or prevent prosecution under applicable state law, and § 844(i) remains applicable to rental, business, and materially mixed-use properties.

### United States v. Playboy Entertainment Group, Inc.

Argued November 30, 1999 | Decided May 22, 2000  
On appeal from the United States District Court for the District of Delaware; judgment below:  
the full-scrambling or safe-harbor-hours requirement invalidated and enforcement enjoined, 30 F. Supp. 2d 702 (D. Del. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/803/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; cable television; content-based regulation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the full-scrambling or time-channeling requirement is the least restrictive means of protecting children from signal bleed.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, Stevens, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stevens | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Thomas | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | O’Connor, Scalia | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that government may not suppress protected adult programming for most of the day without proving that targeted household blocking is inadequate.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Least restrictive protection against signal bleed

Holding and operative rule: A content-based requirement that forces sexually explicit cable channels either to achieve complete scrambling or to cease transmission during most daytime and evening hours violates the First Amendment when the Government has not proved that targeted household blocking is an inadequate less restrictive alternative.

Reasoning: Protecting children from unwanted exposure to sexually explicit signal bleed is a compelling interest. Section 505 nevertheless suppresses protected programming for every household during the hours when many adults are most likely to view it. A separate provision permits a household to request individual blocking, allowing parents to control reception without preventing willing adults in other homes from receiving the channel. The Government bears the burden under strict scrutiny to prove that the broader restriction is necessary despite that targeted alternative. The record did not establish how many households experienced actionable signal bleed, whether notice and individual blocking could reach them effectively, or why improved scrambling combined with targeted requests would fail. Government may not suppress protected adult speech because a narrower system has not yet been used aggressively. Obscenity, targeted blocking, effective notice, improved scrambling, and a materially different evidence-supported measure remain outside the holding. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/803/))

#### Concurrences

Stevens: The First Amendment does not permit government to reduce the adult population to receiving only speech suitable for children. Available household control makes the broad suppression especially difficult to justify.

Thomas: The programmers’ protected speech interest is sufficient to decide the case regardless of uncertainty about the cable operator’s separate editorial rights.

#### Dissents

Scalia: The statute permissibly regulates the transmission of sexually explicit material into homes where children may encounter it. He would not require the Government to prove that an opt-in blocking system is equally effective.

Breyer, joined by O’Connor and Scalia: The record demonstrates a real signal-bleed problem, and the targeted alternative depends on parents learning of the intrusion and requesting relief after exposure has occurred. They would sustain the time-channeling requirement as a fitted protective measure.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Denver Area Educational Telecommunications Consortium, Inc. v. FCC*, decided June 28, 1996: applied; cable restrictions must be assessed with attention to the speech suppressed and the availability of targeted household control.

*Reno v. American Civil Liberties Union*, decided June 26, 1997: applied; protection of minors does not permit broad suppression of protected adult speech when a less restrictive effective alternative remains available.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the United States District Court for the District of Delaware is affirmed, and the injunction against enforcement of § 505 remains in force.

Effect: Cable operators may not be compelled by § 505 to confine the protected channels to safe-harbor hours merely because perfect scrambling is unavailable. Targeted household blocking, notice, improved scrambling, and regulation of obscenity remain permissible.

### Vermont Agency of Natural Resources v. United States ex rel. Stevens

Argued November 29, 1999 | Decided May 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
denial of the Vermont Agency’s motion to dismiss affirmed, 162 F.3d 195 (2d Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/765/))  
Area of Law: Article III; False Claims Act; state sovereign status

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a qui tam relator has Article III standing and whether a State is a person subject to private-relator liability under the False Claims Act.

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Souter.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Breyer | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Joins the Court and Ginsburg |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Ginsburg | Breyer | Concurs in the judgment | Statutory clear-statement ground and reservation of United States actions |
| Dissent | Stevens | Souter | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s assignment-based standing holding and its conclusion that the FCA contains no affirmative indication making a sovereign State a person subject to a private relator’s suit.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1A: Article III standing of a qui tam relator

Holding and operative rule: The FCA effects a partial assignment of the United States’ concrete damages claim to the relator, giving the relator Article III standing to litigate the assigned injury. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: The relator’s anticipated share of a recovery is not by itself an injury because that interest is generated by the lawsuit rather than by the defendant’s invasion of the relator’s own right. The United States, however, allegedly suffered a concrete proprietary injury from fraudulent claims. Congress authorized the relator to pursue part of that claim in the Government’s name and to retain a defined share of the proceeds. Traditional assignees may litigate the assignor’s injury, and the long historical practice of qui tam litigation confirms that this form of action falls within the cases and controversies traditionally resolved by courts. The relator therefore satisfies Article III without establishing a separate personal injury from the alleged fraud. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/765/))

##### Question 1B: State as a liable person under the FCA

Holding and operative rule: The FCA does not authorize a private relator to impose qui tam liability on a State or state agency. The statute contains no affirmative indication overcoming the presumption that the liable person does not include a sovereign State in this private-suit setting.

Reasoning: The FCA’s operative liability provision refers to any person but does not expressly include States. Statutory context, the original criminal and punitive features of the Act, related definitional provisions, and the federalism consequences of private treble-damages litigation provide no affirmative indication that Congress included sovereign States in the private-relator remedy. The Court therefore resolves the case as a matter of statutory interpretation and does not decide whether an otherwise authorized relator action against a State would violate sovereign immunity. The holding is confined to private-relator liability. It does not decide an FCA suit brought by the United States, municipal liability, retaliation claims, or legislation containing materially different language. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/765/))

#### Concurrences

Breyer: He joins the Court’s opinion in full and also joins Ginsburg’s explanation of the decision’s limited reach.

Ginsburg, joined by Breyer: The absence of a clear statement authorizing private qui tam suits against States is sufficient, and the Court properly avoids the constitutional immunity question. Whether a State is covered when the United States itself brings the action remains open.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Souter: The FCA’s text, history, and 1986 amendments demonstrate that States are persons capable of violating the Act. Because the relator litigates an assigned federal claim and the United States retains substantial control, they would affirm.

#### Doctrinal Effect

Article III assignment standing: established; a qui tam relator may rely on the United States’ injury through the FCA’s partial assignment of the federal damages claim.

False Claims Act person requirement: clarified; the private-relator remedy does not reach a State or state agency without an affirmative statutory indication.

State sovereign immunity: not reached; the statutory disposition leaves the constitutional question open.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is reversed, and the qui tam claim against the Vermont Agency of Natural Resources shall be dismissed.

Effect: Stevens may not pursue the FCA claim against the state agency as a private relator. The decision does not bar an action brought by the United States, determine municipal status, resolve a retaliation claim, or govern differently drafted legislation.

### Ohler v. United States

Argued March 20, 2000 | Decided May 22, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
conviction affirmed after defensive disclosure of a prior conviction was treated as forfeiting appellate review, 169 F.3d 1200 (9th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/529/753/))  
Area of Law: Criminal procedure; evidence; appellate preservation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a defendant forfeits appellate review by disclosing a prior conviction on direct examination after timely objection and a definitive adverse Rule 609 ruling.

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between a defensive disclosure made after a preserved definitive ruling and the voluntary introduction of evidence without objection.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Preservation after defensive disclosure

Holding and operative rule: A defendant who timely objects to a definitive adverse Rule 609 ruling does not forfeit appellate review merely by disclosing the conviction first on direct examination to reduce its prejudicial force.

Reasoning: The trial court definitively ruled that the Government could use the conviction to impeach Ohler if she testified, and she timely objected. Once that ruling fixed the evidentiary consequence of taking the stand, her decision to reveal the conviction before cross-examination was a defensive response to the ruling rather than consent to it. Treating that mitigation strategy as waiver would force a defendant to choose between preserving appellate review and presenting the evidence in the least damaging manner before the jury. The ordinary principle that a party cannot complain about evidence it voluntarily introduced does not control when the introduction follows a preserved and definitive ruling making disclosure by the opponent inevitable. The Court does not address silence, a tentative ruling, a defendant who never testifies, independent affirmative use of the conviction, consent, invited error, or another genuine waiver. The admissibility and harmlessness questions remain for the lower courts.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: A litigant who introduces evidence as part of her own case ordinarily may not challenge its admission on appeal. They would treat the tactical benefit of presenting the conviction first as incompatible with claiming reversible error.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Luce v. United States*: distinguished; a defendant must testify before challenging a conditional Rule 609 ruling, but testimony and defensive disclosure after a definitive preserved ruling do not erase the objection.

Federal Rules of Evidence 103 and 609: clarified; a timely objection to a definitive impeachment ruling preserves review notwithstanding anticipatory defensive disclosure.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for review of the Rule 609 ruling and, if error is found, harmless-error analysis.

Effect: Ohler receives appellate consideration of the preserved evidentiary claim, not an automatic new trial. Her conviction remains subject to the lower court’s determination of admissibility and prejudice.

### Doe v. Rosenberg

Argued March 27, 2000 | Decided May 23, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment for the private physicians and hospital on the § 1983 state-action claim affirmed, 166 F.3d 507 (2d Cir. 1999). ([OpenJurist](https://openjurist.org/166/f3d/507/okunieff-v-r-rosenberg))  
Area of Law: Section 1983; state action; involuntary commitment

Questions Presented:

1. Whether private physicians and hospitals act under color of state law when they invoke a state statute authorizing involuntary psychiatric commitment.
2. Whether statutory delegation of confinement authority, extensive regulation, and the State’s role in enforcing the confinement make the resulting deprivation fairly attributable to the State.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer. Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Kennedy | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that authorization, regulation, and judicial enforcement do not alone convert independently exercised private medical judgment into state action.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Private use of the commitment statute

Holding and operative rule: Private physicians and hospitals do not become state actors merely by invoking a generally available involuntary-commitment statute when the challenged commitment decision remains their independent medical judgment.

Reasoning: Section 1983 requires conduct fairly attributable to the State, not merely private conduct authorized by state law. The physicians evaluated Doe and made the challenged certification through professional judgments neither commanded nor jointly administered by a state official. The hospital’s compliance with statutory procedures and its ability to obtain legal enforcement supplied governmental authorization and a framework for review, but those features did not make the State responsible for the initial medical conclusion. The coercive consequence of commitment makes careful attribution essential, yet the severity of the deprivation cannot itself replace the attribution requirement. The judgment is confined to the developed record and does not govern a decision directed by officials, jointly made with the State, or selectively enforced through governmental participation.

##### Question 2: Delegation, regulation, and enforcement

Holding and operative rule: Extensive regulation, statutory authorization of confinement, and availability of judicial enforcement do not establish state action without governmental coercion, significant encouragement, joint administration of the challenged decision, legal compulsion, or delegation of an exclusively governmental adjudicative function.

Reasoning: New York regulates hospitals and defines the conditions under which private professionals may initiate temporary commitment. It did not dictate the medical findings, select Doe for confinement, or make the private certification conclusive without the review and procedures provided by law. Nor did the statute transfer to private physicians a function that, in its relevant form, has been exclusively reserved to government. The Court leaves open a materially different arrangement in which government commands the commitment, participates in the medical decision, selectively supplies enforcement, or makes a private decision legally final through delegated governmental adjudicative authority.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg: The State created the confinement power, prescribed its legal conditions, and supplied the coercive authority that made the physicians’ decision effective. They would hold that use of that delegated power to confine another person is fairly attributable to the State.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*American Manufacturers Mutual Insurance Co. v. Sullivan*, decided June 21, 1999: followed and extended; government authorization and regulation do not attribute independently exercised private judgment to the State merely because the decision has serious legal consequences.

*Blum v. Yaretsky*: followed; regulation and public authorization are insufficient without coercion, significant encouragement, or another attribution basis.

*Lugar v. Edmondson Oil Co.*: clarified; later access to judicial enforcement does not by itself transform the antecedent independent medical judgment into joint state action.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Second Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Doe’s § 1983 claim against the private physicians and hospital remains dismissed on the supplied record. The decision does not adjudicate state-law claims or a commitment commanded, jointly administered, selectively enforced, or made legally final through delegated governmental adjudication.

### Raleigh v. Illinois Department of Revenue

Argued April 17, 2000 | Decided May 30, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
the burden of proof on the Illinois tax claim held to remain on the bankruptcy trustee, 179 F.3d 546 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/15/))  
Area of Law: Bankruptcy; state taxation; burden of proof

Questions Presented:

1. Does bankruptcy alter the burden of proof assigned by state substantive law to a state tax claim?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that bankruptcy preserves the burden assigned by the substantive law creating the tax claim unless the Bankruptcy Code expressly changes it.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Burden governing a tax claim in bankruptcy

Holding and operative rule: Unless the Bankruptcy Code expressly provides otherwise, the burden of proof governing a tax claim in bankruptcy remains where the substantive law creating the claim places it. Illinois law places the burden on the responsible officer and therefore on the bankruptcy trustee representing the estate.

Reasoning: A creditor’s entitlement in bankruptcy ordinarily arises from the nonbankruptcy law creating the debtor’s obligation, subject to any contrary provision of the Bankruptcy Code. Burden of proof is a substantive component of the claim because it can determine the outcome and reflects the policies of the underlying legal regime. Illinois places the production and persuasion burdens on the responsible corporate officer once the State issues the prescribed notice. The Code contains express burden rules for several identified matters but contains no provision changing the burden on a state tax claim. Bankruptcy Rule 3001(f), which gives a properly filed proof of claim prima facie evidentiary effect, does not reassign the ultimate burden when the claim is disputed. The trustee therefore bears the same burden the debtor would have borne outside bankruptcy. Express Code rules, the filing and evidentiary consequences of a proof of claim, and the underlying merits of the tax liability remain unaffected. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/15/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Butner v. United States*: followed; nonbankruptcy law defines the substance and incidents of a claim unless the Bankruptcy Code supplies a contrary rule.

*Director, Office of Workers’ Compensation Programs v. Greenwich Collieries*: applied; the burden of proof is a substantive component of a claim.

Bankruptcy Code claim allowance: clarified; federal bankruptcy procedure does not silently reallocate a state-law burden governing the validity of a tax claim.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The trustee continues to bear the burden of disproving the Illinois penalty liability and obtains no relief from the judgment below. Remaining questions concerning the underlying tax liability and claim administration proceed under the existing bankruptcy judgment.

### Castillo v. United States

Argued April 24, 2000 | Decided June 5, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
the firearm types listed in former 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1) treated as sentencing factors and the enhanced sentences affirmed, 179 F.3d 321 (5th Cir. 1999). ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-supreme-court/530/120.html))  
Area of Law: Criminal law; statutory interpretation; sentencing

Questions Presented:

1. Did the firearm types listed in former 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1) define aggravated offenses or sentencing factors?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer. Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg in full; Scalia except point Fourth of Part II | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for eight Justices; opinion except the legislative-history point for Scalia |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the listed firearm types define aggravated offenses requiring indictment and jury determination under the former statute.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Firearm type as an aggravated-offense element

Holding and operative rule: Under the former version of 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1), machinegun, destructive device, silencer, short-barreled rifle, and short-barreled shotgun identify aggravated offenses rather than sentencing factors. The applicable firearm type must be charged and admitted as an element or proved to a jury beyond a reasonable doubt.

Reasoning: The statute’s first sentence defines the basic offense of using or carrying a firearm during and in relation to a crime of violence or drug-trafficking crime and places the listed firearm types in that same offense-defining sentence. Later sentences speak expressly in sentencing terms, making the structural contrast significant. Firearm type also concerns the conduct and instrumentality of the offense rather than a traditional sentencing consideration such as recidivism. Jury determination creates little practical difficulty because the prosecution ordinarily identifies and proves the weapon used, while judicial resolution could conflict with the jury’s underlying understanding of which firearm supported its verdict. The large increase in mandatory punishment reinforces the element reading. Statutory text, structure, tradition, and the interpretive principles reflected in *Jones v. United States*, decided March 24, 1999, therefore require aggravated-offense treatment. The holding concerns the former statutory text and does not classify materially amended provisions. ([FindLaw](https://caselaw.findlaw.com/court/us-supreme-court/530/120.html))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Jones v. United States*, decided March 24, 1999: applied and clarified; offense-related facts carrying substantially aggravated punishment are read as elements when statutory structure and tradition support that classification.

*Almendarez-Torres v. United States*, decided March 24, 1998: distinguished; the firearm type is part of the charged conduct, not a traditional recidivism consideration suitable for independent judicial determination.

Former 18 U.S.C. § 924(c)(1): clarified; the listed firearm types define aggravated crimes requiring indictment and jury determination.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to vacate the firearm-type sentences unsupported by an element verdict and conduct any lawful further proceedings.

Effect: The enhanced machinegun or other listed-firearm sentences may not rest solely on judicial factfinding under the former statute. The Government may pursue any remedy permitted by the indictment, verdict, double-jeopardy rules, and ordinary criminal procedure; the basic § 924(c)(1) convictions and other counts are affected only as required by those proceedings.

> **Structuring the cases**
>
> I’m organizing each decision by date, issue, historical baseline, altered vote, and resulting rationale. I’ll distinguish narrow holdings from broader rules, especially where the simulated record changes retroactivity or agency authority.

> **Refining Sims’ alignment**
>
> I’m separating the judgment from the reasoning: Stone and Thomas likely concur in the result, while the broader rationale remains fractured. I’m therefore treating the case as having no single fully controlling opinion, with separate opinions across the coalition.
>
> **Clarifying controlling reasoning**
>
> I’m treating the judgment as broader than any single rationale. The justices agree the statute cannot stand as applied, but their theories differ enough that no clean controlling rule emerges.
>
> **Assessing Hubbell’s outcome**
>
> I’m treating Stone’s replacement of the historical dissenter as producing a unanimous judgment. The principal opinion remains broadly joined, while a separate concurrence preserves an independent objection without changing the result.
>
> **Defining the mandate**
>
> I’m preserving the previously specified dismissal in Hubbell. For North Carolina RTL, I’m limiting the disposition to the two granted questions rather than silently deciding unrelated lower-court issues.
