## Decisions and Dispositions

### Sims v. Apfel

Argued March 28, 2000 | Decided June 5, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: issues omitted from the request for Appeals Council review treated as unavailable on judicial review, 200 F.3d 229 (5th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/103/))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; Social Security; judicial review

Questions Presented:

1. Does a claimant forfeit judicial review of an issue by omitting it from a request for discretionary Appeals Council review?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.  
Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg; Parts I and II-A for O’Connor |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Appeals Council notice and procedure |
| Dissent | Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the prescribed review route, absent a statute, regulation, or fair procedural notice requiring issue exhaustion, does not make omission from a discretionary Appeals Council request a forfeiture. `OT_1999CHUNK7.md`

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Issue exhaustion before the Appeals Council

Holding and operative rule: A Social Security claimant who completes the prescribed administrative-review route does not forfeit judicial review of an issue merely by omitting it from a request for discretionary Appeals Council review when no statute or regulation requires issue exhaustion and the governing procedure provides no fair notice of such a requirement.

Reasoning: Administrative issue exhaustion ordinarily arises from enacted law, valid agency regulations, or an adjudicative process in which the parties are charged with identifying and developing the controlling issues. No statute imposed issue exhaustion here, and the Social Security regulations required Sims to request Appeals Council review without directing her to list every legal issue she might later present. Appeals Council review is materially nonadversarial: the agency retains responsibility for examining the claim and may consider issues beyond those specifically identified by the claimant. Imposing a judicial forfeiture rule in that setting would convert a discretionary review request into an unannounced pleading trap. The decision concerns omission of issues at the Appeals Council stage after completion of the prescribed route. It does not decide preservation before the administrative law judge, deliberate withholding, presentation of materially new evidence, failure to request Council review, or a future valid regulation expressly requiring issue exhaustion. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supct/html/98-9537.ZO.html))

#### Concurrences

O’Connor: The Appeals Council forms and regulations affirmatively suggested that detailed issue specification was unnecessary. She would reserve whether courts may require issue exhaustion in another administrative process providing clear notice and assigning issue development to the parties.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Scalia and Kennedy: Ordinary administrative practice requires parties to give an agency the opportunity to address alleged errors before judicial review. They would treat Appeals Council review as sufficiently adjudicative to support that requirement despite the absence of an express regulation.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Thunder Basin Coal Co. v. Reich*: clarified; completion of a specialized administrative route does not itself create an issue-exhaustion requirement absent an enacted command, valid regulation, or procedure fairly assigning issue development to the claimant.

*Hormel v. Helvering*: applied; judicial issue-exhaustion rules depend on the character of the administrative proceeding and are weakest in a nonadversarial process lacking fair notice.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for consideration of Sims’s properly presented claims without treating omission from her Appeals Council request as forfeiture.

Effect: Sims may obtain judicial consideration of the omitted issues. The decision does not excuse failure to complete the administrative-review route or resolve preservation rules governing the administrative law judge stage.

### Troxel v. Granville

Argued January 12, 2000 | Decided June 5, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Washington; judgment below: the third-party-visitation statute and resulting visitation order invalidated, 137 Wash. 2d 1, 969 P.2d 21 (1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/57/))  
Area of Law: Due process; parental liberty; family law

Questions Presented:

1. Did Washington’s broad third-party-visitation statute, as applied over a fit parent’s objection, violate the parent’s liberty interest in directing her children’s care and upbringing?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Plurality | O’Connor | Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Stone | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Serious-harm or comparable-need predicate and narrow tailoring |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Souter | No other Justice | Concurs in the judgment | Facial breadth of the statute |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would vacate and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: authors a concurrence in the judgment, joined by Thomas.

Announced ground: A fit parent’s reasonable visitation decision receives a strong presumption, and a nonparent may obtain contrary compulsory visitation only upon a particularized showing of serious harm to the child or a comparably concrete legally recognized need, followed by relief narrowly fitted to that need.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by O’Connor. No opinion of the Court.

##### Question 1: Judicial displacement of a fit parent’s visitation decision

Holding and operative rule: No controlling rationale under *Marks* establishes a general third-party-visitation test. A majority agrees that the Due Process Clause protects a fit parent’s authority over the care, custody, and control of a child and that the visitation order entered here cannot stand, but no majority adopts a universal serious-harm requirement, strict scrutiny, the plurality’s special-weight formulation, or facial invalidation as the governing rule.

Reasoning: O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer concluded that Washington’s statute was extraordinarily broad, allowing any person to petition at any time and giving the trial judge no rule requiring meaningful deference to the fit parent’s decision. The trial court then substituted its own preferred visitation arrangement even though Granville had not denied all visitation and no finding of unfitness or special need had been made. Stone and Thomas agreed with affirmance on the more protective ground that serious harm or a comparably concrete legal need must be established before a court overrides a fit parent, and that any resulting order must be narrowly fitted. Souter would affirm because the state statute vested judges with facially unlimited authority to replace parental decisions. These grounds support the same judgment but do not form a logical subset yielding one controlling general test. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/57/))

#### Concurrences

Stone, joined by Thomas: A fit parent’s reasonable decision is presumptively controlling. Compulsory third-party visitation requires proof of serious harm or a comparably concrete legal need, and the court may order no more visitation than that showing justifies.

Souter: The statute’s authorization for any person to petition at any time, coupled with unrestricted judicial best-interests authority, is constitutionally defective on its face. He would not formulate a universal harm rule for narrower statutes.

#### Dissents

Stevens: A child has interests distinct from the parent’s, and state family law may account for those interests without first proving parental unfitness or serious harm. He would reverse the categorical invalidation and permit a more contextual inquiry.

Scalia: Although parental authority is deeply rooted, he would not derive an enforceable unenumerated visitation right from substantive due process. He would reverse the federal constitutional judgment.

Kennedy: Parental decisions warrant constitutional protection, but a harm prerequisite is too rigid and the validity of a best-interests standard depends on its particular legal and factual operation. He would vacate and remand for application of a more careful, case-specific standard. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/57/))

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Meyer v. Nebraska* and *Pierce v. Society of Sisters*: reaffirmed; the Due Process Clause protects a fit parent’s authority to direct a child’s care and upbringing.

*Marks v. United States*: applied; no single judgment-supporting rationale is a logical subset of the other grounds sufficient to establish a general visitation test.

*A. St. P. C. v. B. C.*: distinguished; no controlling coalition imports its termination-like no-contact proof rule into ordinary visitation proceedings.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Washington is affirmed.

Effect: The visitation order against Granville remains invalid, and the challenged Washington provision remains unenforceable under the state judgment. The decision does not establish an absolute parental veto or prevent emergency protection, findings of unfitness, or visitation relief supported by a constitutionally sufficient and properly fitted predicate.

### United States v. Hubbell

Argued February 22, 2000 | Decided June 5, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: the case remanded for application of a reasonable-particularity and independent-source inquiry to the immunized document production, 167 F.3d 552 (D.C. Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/27/))  
Area of Law: Fifth Amendment; self-incrimination; immunity

Questions Presented:

1. May the Government use the testimonial act and derivative fruits of a broad immunized document production to investigate and indict the producing witness?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Thomas | Scalia | Supports affirmance | Original understanding of the Self-Incrimination Clause |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s act-of-production and derivative-use analysis.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Derivative use of immunized document production

Holding and operative rule: When a broad subpoena compels an immunized witness to identify, locate, select, and assemble documents whose existence and possession the Government did not already know with reasonable particularity, the act of production is testimonial. The Government may not use that testimony or its direct or indirect fruits unless it proves that the evidence supporting prosecution came from sources wholly independent of the immunized production.

Reasoning: The voluntarily prepared contents of existing documents are ordinarily not compelled testimony, but complying with this subpoena communicated that responsive documents existed, were within Hubbell’s possession or control, and were the documents described. The subpoena did not identify known records with reasonable particularity. It required Hubbell to make extensive use of his own knowledge to locate and assemble more than 13,000 pages, thereby supplying the Government with the first essential link in a new investigation. Because the compelled act was protected, the immunity order had to be coextensive with the privilege under *Kastigar*. The prosecution therefore bore the burden of proving a wholly independent source for the evidence used to obtain the indictment. It conceded that it could not satisfy the governing reasonable-particularity and independent-source requirements, so the indictment must be dismissed. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/27/))

#### Concurrences

Thomas, joined by Scalia: Historical evidence may support a broader understanding of the Self-Incrimination Clause than existing doctrine recognizes. The present case is resolved under the Court’s settled act-of-production and immunity precedents.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Fisher v. United States*: distinguished; the foregone-conclusion rationale does not apply when the Government lacks prior knowledge of the documents’ existence, location, and possession with reasonable particularity.

*Kastigar v. United States*: applied; use and derivative-use immunity requires the prosecution to prove a source wholly independent of the testimonial act of production.

*United States v. Doe*: followed; broad compelled assembly of business records may itself communicate protected factual assertions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and the indictment shall be dismissed under the parties’ conditional agreement.

Effect: The Government may not prosecute Hubbell using the testimonial act of production or evidence derived from it. The decision does not protect voluntarily prepared document contents as such, alter collective-entity rules, or bar a prosecution supported wholly by genuinely independent evidence.

### North Carolina Right to Life, Inc. v. Bartlett

Argued April 3, 2000 | Decided June 6, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: full political-committee treatment and a broad expenditure definition invalidated or narrowed, while other campaign-finance provisions were sustained, 168 F.3d 705 (4th Cir. 1999). `OT_1999CHUNK7.md` ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/168/705/569255/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; campaign finance; vagueness

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a State may impose political-committee registration, reporting, and disclosure obligations on an issue-advocacy organization whose major purpose is not nominating or electing candidates.
2. Whether the State may define regulated campaign expenditures more broadly than communications containing express electoral advocacy without making the law unconstitutionally vague or overbroad.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion for Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, and Breyer; Question 1 for Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Express-advocacy boundary for Question 2 |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: Full political-committee status may not be imposed on an organization lacking a major electoral purpose merely because some communications discuss candidates, and a regulated-expenditure definition must contain a sufficiently definite electoral-advocacy boundary.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Political-committee status for an issue-advocacy organization

Holding and operative rule: A State may not impose the full, continuous political-committee regime on an organization whose major purpose is not nominating, electing, or defeating candidates merely because some of its communications discuss candidates or public issues.

Reasoning: Political-committee status carries continuing registration, organizational, accounting, reporting, and disclosure obligations materially different from transaction-specific disclosure attached to a particular expenditure. Under *Buckley* and *Massachusetts Citizens for Life*, those comprehensive burdens may be imposed on an organization formed or controlled for electoral advocacy, but not on an issue organization merely because candidate positions are relevant to its public discussion. North Carolina Right to Life was not associated with a candidate or party, and candidate election was not one of its major purposes. The challenged definition nevertheless reached an organization with an incidental purpose to influence an election, exposing ordinary issue discussion and voter guides to the full regulatory system. The State remains free to require appropriately fitted sponsor disclosure for particular election-related transactions and to regulate candidates, parties, coordinated spending, and organizations whose major purpose is electoral advocacy. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/168/705/569255/))

##### Question 2: Definition of regulated campaign expenditures

Holding and operative rule: A State may reach communications beyond a rigid list of electoral words only when the law supplies an objective and sufficiently definite boundary identifying communications that are unmistakably electoral in operation. North Carolina’s open-ended influence and political-purpose language, as applied to issue discussion and voter guides, is unconstitutionally vague and overbroad. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Campaign-finance regulation operates in an area where uncertain coverage predictably suppresses lawful speech before any enforcement action occurs. A definition depending on whether issue discussion has an incidental purpose to influence an election leaves speakers to guess whether mentioning a candidate, describing voting records, or distributing a voter guide transforms public-issue advocacy into regulated expenditure activity. The First Amendment does not require every statute to reproduce a fixed set of words, but it does require an administrable electoral boundary that prevents regulators from classifying speech according to subjective purpose or likely influence. North Carolina’s definition supplied no such boundary. Narrow sponsor identification and transaction-specific reporting remain constitutionally distinct from imposing political-committee status or liability under an indeterminate expenditure definition.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: Independent communications should fall within expenditure regulation only when they expressly advocate election or defeat through clear electoral language. They agreed that the challenged definition and resulting burdens were invalid but would not approve a broader functional-equivalence formulation.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Buckley v. Valeo*: clarified; full political-committee status remains confined to organizations whose major purpose is electoral advocacy.

*Federal Election Commission v. Massachusetts Citizens for Life*: followed; organizational form and issue advocacy do not alone justify comprehensive political-committee burdens.

*McIntyre v. Ohio Elections Commission* and *Buckley v. American Constitutional Law Foundation, Inc.*: applied; compelled identification and reporting requirements must be fitted to a definite electoral or enforcement interest and may not chill ordinary issue advocacy through uncertain coverage.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed insofar as it invalidates full political-committee treatment of North Carolina Right to Life and the challenged expenditure definition.

Effect: North Carolina may not subject the organization’s issue advocacy and voter guides to those provisions. Severable sponsor disclosure, candidate and party reporting, coordinated-spending rules, and political-committee regulation of organizations with a major electoral purpose remain available.

### National Mining Association v. United States Department of the Interior

Argued April 4, 2000 | Decided June 7, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit; judgment below: district-court summary judgment for the Department reversed on the relevant ownership, control, and retroactivity challenges, 177 F.3d 1 (D.C. Cir. 1999). `OT_1999CHUNK7.md` ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/177/1/475598/))  
Area of Law: Administrative law; statutory interpretation; retroactivity

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the Department of the Interior exceeded its statutory authority by using ownership-and-control rules to continue permit-blocking consequences after the relevant ownership or control relationship ended.
2. Whether the challenged rules were impermissibly retroactive or unlawfully reopened final agency determinations based on pre-rule conduct.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s interpretation of the statutory ownership-and-control language and its rejection of unauthorized retroactive permit consequences.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Permit blocking after control has ended

Holding and operative rule: The Surface Mining Control and Reclamation Act does not authorize Interior to continue an ownership-and-control permit block after the applicant’s legally relevant ownership or control has ended. Former control may support a permit consequence only through another enacted provision whose requirements are satisfied, including the provision governing a demonstrated pattern of willful violations.

Reasoning: Section 510(c) addresses an operation owned or controlled by the applicant that is currently in violation. The present-tense relationship between applicant and violating operation performs substantive work. Once the applicant no longer owns or controls the operation, the ordinary ownership-and-control ground no longer gives the applicant authority to abate the violation and no longer supplies the statutory basis for blocking a new permit. Congress separately addressed certain former relationships through the demonstrated-pattern provision, which requires willful violations of the specified nature, duration, and environmental consequence. Interior could not erase those additional conditions by interpreting the ordinary current-control provision to continue indefinitely after control ended. The ruling does not prevent regulation of indirect but current control or enforcement under an independently applicable statutory provision. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/177/1/475598/))

##### Question 2: Retroactive permit disabilities and reopening

Holding and operative rule: Interior may not attach a new permit-blocking disability to completed pre-rule transactions or reopen a final determination on that basis without enacted authority clearly supporting the retroactive consequence or reopening.

Reasoning: The challenged rule made permit eligibility turn on ownership, control, and violations occurring before the rule established that relationship as a disabling ground. That operation attached a new legal disability to completed conduct rather than merely regulating a present application through existing law. Administrative agencies possess no inherent authority to promulgate retroactive rules or reopen final matters. Such authority must be found in the statute Congress enacted, and SMCRA supplied no clear authorization for these applications. A current ongoing violation may remain legally relevant when the applicant currently owns or controls the operation, and a statute may expressly authorize reopening or a distinct willful-violation consequence. Those situations do not validate the new retrospective disability imposed here. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/177/1/475598/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Chevron U.S.A. Inc. v. Natural Resources Defense Council, Inc.*: applied; deference does not permit an agency to disregard the statute’s current-control limitation or claim authority the enacted text withholds.

*Bowen v. Georgetown University Hospital*: followed; retroactive administrative rulemaking requires express congressional authorization.

*Landgraf v. USI Film Products*: applied; a rule attaching a new permit disability to completed pre-rule conduct operates retroactively.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit is affirmed, and Interior shall conform the challenged ownership-and-control rules to the statutory limits identified in this opinion.

Effect: Interior may enforce permit consequences based on current ownership or control and may use independently valid willful-violation or reopening provisions. It may not continue the ordinary ownership-and-control block after the relationship ends or impose the challenged new disability on completed pre-rule transactions.

### Harris Trust and Savings Bank v. Salomon Smith Barney Inc.

Argued April 17, 2000 | Decided June 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: § 502(a)(3) held unavailable against a nonfiduciary party in interest, 184 F.3d 646 (7th Cir. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/99-579))  
Area of Law: ERISA; equitable remedies; prohibited transactions

Questions Presented:

1. Does § 502(a)(3) authorize appropriate equitable relief against a nonfiduciary party in interest that knowingly receives property through a prohibited transaction?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Thomas | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that historically equitable relief may run against a knowing nonfiduciary transferee without converting that person into a fiduciary or creating a general damages action.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Equitable relief against a nonfiduciary transferee

Holding and operative rule: ERISA § 502(a)(3) authorizes historically equitable relief against a nonfiduciary party in interest that receives plan property through a transaction prohibited by § 406(a), subject to proof of the prohibited transaction, the transferee’s actual or constructive knowledge, tracing, and traditional equitable defenses.

Reasoning: Section 502(a)(3) identifies the claimant and the relief available rather than limiting defendants to ERISA fiduciaries. Section 406(a) places the transactional duty on the fiduciary who causes the plan to enter the prohibited transaction, but the absence of a parallel fiduciary duty on the counterparty does not make transferred plan property immune from traditional equitable recovery. Trust law permitted beneficiaries and trustees to seek rescission, restitution, disgorgement, or recovery of specifically identifiable property from a transferee who had actual or constructive notice of the trust violation. A bona fide purchaser without notice ordinarily retained traditional protection. The lower court therefore erred in treating nonfiduciary status as a categorical defense. The Court does not determine whether the transaction was prohibited, what Salomon knew, whether the property or proceeds remain traceable, or which equitable remedy and defenses the evidence will support. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/99-579))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Mertens v. Hewitt Associates*: distinguished; rejection of compensatory damages against a nonfiduciary does not bar historically equitable recovery from a transferee of plan property.

*Varity Corp. v. Howe*: extended; § 502(a)(3) reaches appropriate individualized equitable relief against a proper defendant even when that defendant is not itself an ERISA fiduciary.

*John Hancock Mutual Life Insurance Co. v. Harris Trust & Savings Bank*: applied; plan-asset classification, fiduciary conduct, transferee status, and remedy remain distinct questions.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the alleged prohibited transaction, Salomon’s knowledge, tracing, equitable defenses, and any appropriate equitable relief.

Effect: Salomon’s nonfiduciary status does not categorically defeat the action. The decision authorizes no general legal-damages remedy and makes no finding that Salomon knowingly received property through a prohibited transaction.

### Pegram v. Herdrich

Argued February 23, 2000 | Decided June 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below: dismissal of the ERISA fiduciary count reversed and the claim allowed to proceed, 154 F.3d 362 (7th Cir. 1998). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/211/))  
Area of Law: ERISA; fiduciary duty; managed care

Questions Presented:

1. Does an HMO physician act in an ERISA fiduciary capacity when making an inseparably mixed treatment-and-eligibility decision influenced by the HMO’s incentive structure?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand with directions to dismiss the ERISA fiduciary claim.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s function-specific distinction between ERISA plan administration and an inseparably mixed medical treatment-and-benefit decision.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Fiduciary status for mixed medical decisions

Holding and operative rule: A treating physician does not act as an ERISA fiduciary when making an inseparably mixed decision about medically appropriate treatment and the benefit delivered through that treatment, even when the HMO’s compensation structure creates incentives to limit care.

Reasoning: ERISA fiduciary status is functional. A person acts as a fiduciary only to the extent the person exercises the plan-management, administration, asset-control, or discretionary authority identified by the statute. An HMO physician’s mixed decision determines both what treatment is medically appropriate and whether that treatment will be provided through the plan. Those elements cannot realistically be separated into a medical decision and a distinct fiduciary eligibility determination. Financial incentives to ration care are inherent in risk-bearing health plans, and classifying every mixed decision as fiduciary conduct would convert ordinary malpractice and managed-care disputes into federal trust litigation. Congress did not assign federal courts authority to decide which incentive structures create socially acceptable medical risks through ERISA’s fiduciary provisions. Pure plan administration, fiduciary communications, misuse of plan assets, and decisions separable from treatment remain governed by their own statutory functions. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/211/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Varity Corp. v. Howe*: clarified; fiduciary status follows the function actually performed and does not attach to all conduct affecting plan benefits.

*Lockheed Corp. v. Spink* and *Hughes Aircraft Co. v. Jacobson*: followed; ERISA distinguishes fiduciary administration from decisions made in another legal capacity.

ERISA fiduciary doctrine: limited; inseparably mixed treatment-and-benefit decisions by treating HMO physicians are not fiduciary acts.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to dismiss the ERISA fiduciary count.

Effect: Herdrich may not proceed on the theory that the mixed treatment decision itself was an ERISA fiduciary breach. Viable malpractice, disclosure, contract, regulatory, pure-administration, fiduciary-misrepresentation, or plan-asset claims remain governed by their independent requirements.

### Ramdass v. Angelone

Argued April 18, 2000 | Decided June 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below: federal habeas relief from the death sentence denied, 187 F.3d 396 (4th Cir. 1999). ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/99-7000))  
Area of Law: Capital sentencing; due process; federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. Did Due Process require parole-ineligibility information when the prosecution argued future dangerousness but the outstanding verdict remained subject to substantive postverdict alteration?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | No other Justice | Would affirm | AEDPA and state-law finality |
| Dissent | Kennedy | Scalia, Thomas | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand for a new penalty proceeding.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the State may not rely on an already returned guilty verdict to prove future dangerousness while withholding the practically certain parole consequence of that same verdict.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Parole information after an outstanding guilty verdict

Holding and operative rule: When the prosecution relies on an outstanding guilty verdict to prove future dangerousness, and that verdict will make the defendant parole-ineligible upon entry of judgment absent a genuine unresolved substantive defect, due process requires accurate parole information. A state court unreasonably applies *Simmons* by treating the ordinary interval before entry of judgment as dispositive while permitting the prosecution to use the verdict as substantively reliable.

Reasoning: *Simmons* prevents a capital jury from deciding between life and death on the materially false premise that a life sentence permits release when state law instead forecloses parole. *Allridge* reserves that protection for legally fixed consequences and does not require predictions about discretionary parole. Ramdass’s case does not involve a prediction about a parole board or an unresolved future prosecution. A jury had returned the Domino’s guilty verdict, the State relied on that adjudication to portray him as persistently dangerous, and entry of judgment would automatically produce parole ineligibility. The State identified no concrete substantive defect then awaiting resolution, only the ordinary legal possibility of postverdict action before formal judgment. Due process does not permit the prosecution to treat the verdict as sufficiently final to prove danger yet insufficiently final to disclose its certain legal consequence. The Virginia court’s formal-entry rule was therefore an unreasonable application of the governing capital-sentencing principle. ([Legal Information Institute](https://www.law.cornell.edu/supremecourt/text/99-7000))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor: Federal habeas review is limited to unreasonable applications of clearly established Supreme Court law. Because Virginia law did not yet classify Ramdass as parole-ineligible, she would hold that the state court remained within the range permitted by *Simmons*.

Kennedy, joined by Scalia and Thomas: *Simmons* supplies a workable rule tied to actual parole ineligibility under state law when the sentencing jury deliberates. They would not replace that legal boundary with an assessment of whether entry of judgment or rejection of postverdict objections appears practically certain.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Simmons v. South Carolina*: clarified and extended; formal entry of judgment is not indispensable when a returned verdict has no concrete unresolved substantive defect, will automatically create parole ineligibility, and is used by the prosecution to prove future dangerousness.

*Allridge v. Scott*: distinguished; *Allridge* involved legally available parole and predictions about discretionary future release, while Ramdass’s ineligibility followed automatically from an already returned verdict.

*O’Dell v. Netherland*: followed; the accuracy of parole information remains integral to the capital jury’s life-or-death choice when future dangerousness is argued.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to grant a conditional writ unless Virginia conducts a constitutionally adequate new penalty proceeding within a reasonable period or imposes a lawful noncapital sentence.

Effect: Ramdass’s murder conviction remains intact. His death sentence may not remain in force without a new penalty proceeding in which the sentencing decision is made on accurate information concerning parole eligibility.

### Reeves v. Sanderson Plumbing Products, Inc.

Argued March 21, 2000 | Decided June 12, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below: the age-discrimination verdict set aside for insufficient evidence, 197 F.3d 688 (5th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/133/))  
Area of Law: Employment discrimination; evidence; judgment as a matter of law

Questions Presented:

1. What standard governs judgment as a matter of law after an age-discrimination plaintiff establishes a prima facie case and supplies evidence that the employer’s explanation is false?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Ginsburg | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Practical operation of the pretext inference |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of the ordinary pretext inference and Rule 50’s verdict-protective standard.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Prima facie proof, pretext, and Rule 50

Holding and operative rule: A discrimination plaintiff’s prima facie case, combined with evidence permitting rejection of the employer’s stated reason, may support a finding of intentional discrimination. Rule 50 review must draw reasonable inferences for the verdict winner, avoid credibility determinations and reweighing, and disregard moving-party evidence the jury was not required to believe.

Reasoning: Once the employer produces a lawful explanation, the intermediate *McDonnell Douglas* presumption disappears, but the prima facie evidence remains part of the record. Proof that the explanation is false is circumstantial evidence of discriminatory intent and may permit the jury to infer that the employer is concealing an unlawful reason. That inference is not mandatory. Judgment as a matter of law remains proper when the record conclusively establishes another nondiscriminatory reason or when the evidence of pretext is so weak, and contrary evidence so conclusive, that no reasonable jury could find discrimination. The Fifth Circuit did not apply that standard. It discounted Reeves’s evidence, credited evidence favorable to Sanderson that the jury could reject, and reweighed the role of the manager who made age-related statements. Viewed as Rule 50 requires, the prima facie case, evidence undermining the attendance-record explanation, and evidence of age-based animus sufficiently supported the verdict. ([Justia Law](https://supreme.justia.com/cases/federal/us/530/133/))

#### Concurrences

Ginsburg: In most cases, a prima facie showing combined with substantial evidence of pretext will permit a reasonable discrimination finding because the employer ordinarily possesses the best evidence of its actual motive. The Court properly leaves room for unusual records in which the inference is nevertheless unreasonable.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*St. Mary’s Honor Center v. Hicks*: clarified; rejection of the employer’s explanation does not compel liability, but it may support the ultimate inference of intentional discrimination.

*McDonnell Douglas Corp. v. Green*: applied; the prima facie evidence remains probative after the employer satisfies its burden of production.

Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 50: clarified; courts must review the complete record in the verdict winner’s favor without weighing credibility or crediting evidence the jury was free to reject.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is reversed, and the case is remanded with instructions to reinstate the verdict and resulting judgment unless an independently preserved, verdict-compatible ground lawfully requires further proceedings.

Effect: Reeves’s verdict may not be displaced under the evidentiary standard used by the Fifth Circuit. The decision does not make proof of pretext automatic liability in every discrimination case.

### United States v. McIver

Argued April 10, 2000 | Decided June 13, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below: warrantless attachment of electronic tracking devices held neither a search nor a seizure, and public-road monitoring sustained, 186 F.3d 1119 (9th Cir. 1999). `OT_1999CHUNK7.md` ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/186/1119/569398/))  
Area of Law: Fourth Amendment; searches and seizures; electronic tracking

Questions Presented:

1. Whether officers’ warrantless physical attachment of electronic tracking devices to vehicles parked on an open residential driveway constitutes a search or seizure.
2. Whether warrantless monitoring of those devices to determine the vehicles’ movements on public roads violates the Fourth Amendment.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.  
Opposing: Breyer.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Physical attachment as a search | Reversed and remanded, 8-1 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Breyer | Determine warrant, exception, causation, suppression, and harmlessness |
| Physical attachment as a seizure | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | No seizure established on this record |
| Short-term public-road monitoring | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Monitoring is not independently a search under *Knotts* |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg in full; Breyer as to the no-seizure and public-road-monitoring holdings | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion for eight Justices; seizure and monitoring propositions for Breyer |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | No other Justice | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Concurs on seizure and public-road monitoring; dissents from the attachment-search holding |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse in part, affirm in part, and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction among physical search, possessory seizure, and observation of movements exposed on public roads.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Physical attachment to a vehicle

Holding and operative rule: Government conducts a search when it deliberately attaches an electronic tracking device to a person’s vehicle, an effect protected by the Fourth Amendment, for the purpose of obtaining information. Attachment constitutes a seizure only when it meaningfully interferes with possession, control, or use; no such interference was established here. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer as to the seizure proposition)

Reasoning: The Ninth Circuit treated the vehicle’s visible exterior and undercarriage as eliminating the Fourth Amendment issue. That reasoning confuses observation with physical occupation. Officers did not merely look at the vehicle from a lawful vantage. They placed two magnetized devices on it so that the Government could use the vehicle as the platform for an information-gathering system. The Amendment protects effects as well as places, and a purposeful physical intrusion upon an effect to obtain information is a search even when the contacted surface is publicly visible. The attachment did not, however, deprive McIver of dominion, alter the vehicle’s operation, damage it, or otherwise meaningfully interfere with possession. It therefore was not a seizure on the supplied record. Because the attachment was a search, the lower courts must determine whether a warrant or recognized exception authorized it and what evidence, if any, was obtained through that search. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/186/1119/569398/))

##### Question 2: Monitoring movements on public roads

Holding and operative rule: Short-term electronic monitoring that reveals a vehicle’s movements exposed on public roads is not independently a search under *United States v. Knotts* on this record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: A person traveling on public roads exposes the vehicle’s route, direction, stops, and progress to visual observation. The devices enabled officers to follow movements that could lawfully have been observed from public vantage points and did not reveal activity inside a home or another protected place. *Knotts* therefore governs the monitoring considered in isolation. That conclusion does not validate the antecedent physical attachment. Evidence discovered through monitoring may remain the fruit of the unlawful attachment unless the Government establishes a warrant, an exception, attenuation, independent source, inevitable discovery, or another lawful remedial ground. The Court does not decide prolonged aggregate monitoring capable of revealing a materially different body of information.

#### Concurrences

Breyer concurred that the attachment did not meaningfully interfere with possession and that short-term public-road monitoring was not independently a search. He dissented from treating the technical physical attachment to the publicly exposed vehicle exterior as a search without an additional invasion of a reasonable expectation of privacy.

#### Dissents

No separate dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Knotts*: followed; short-term monitoring of movements exposed on public roads is not independently a search.

*United States v. Karo*: distinguished; consensual installation before transfer and a device conveying no information through the transfer itself do not resolve unauthorized physical attachment to an owner’s existing vehicle for information gathering.

*Arizona v. Hicks*: extended; purposeful physical manipulation or occupation of an effect to obtain information may constitute a search even when visual inspection of the exposed surface would be lawful.

*Soldal v. Cook County*: reaffirmed; property-based and privacy-based Fourth Amendment protections remain analytically distinct.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is affirmed as to the absence of a seizure and the independent legality of short-term public-road monitoring, reversed as to the physical-attachment search, and remanded for warrant, exception, causation, suppression, and harmless-error proceedings.

Effect: McIver’s convictions are not automatically vacated. The Government must establish a lawful basis for the attachment or show that the challenged evidence and convictions survive ordinary Fourth Amendment remedial analysis.

> **Assessing doctrinal agreement**
>
> I’m separating agreement with the case-specific result from agreement with the broader procedural theory. Even judges rejecting a general pathway may still affirm because the claim was previously available and no later law made the conduct noncriminal.
>
> **Separating judgment and reasoning**
>
> I’m distinguishing unanimous agreement on the outcome from agreement on the rationale. The hypothetical court could affirm unanimously while dividing over whether the broader procedural pathway exists.
>
> **Applying doctrinal effects**
>
> I’m treating the narrow exception as controlling, while distinguishing related precedents that preserve limited routes for structural unavailability or later statutory innocence claims. The judgment remains an affirmance, leaving the petition dismissed.

> **Assessed doctrinal agreement, judgment, reasoning, and effects**
