The corrected tally affects historical comparison only and does not alter Stone’s approved position. `OT_1999CHUNK8.md` ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-604.pdf))

## Decisions and Dispositions

### United States v. Barrett

Argued April 11, 2000 | Decided June 14, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below:  
dismissal of the successive collateral petition and rejection of relief under § 2241 and the All Writs Act affirmed, 178 F.3d 34 (1st Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/178/34/601855/))  
Area of Law: Federal courts; habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. When is the remedy under 28 U.S.C. § 2255 inadequate or ineffective, permitting a federal prisoner to invoke 28 U.S.C. § 2241 through the savings clause?
2. Whether a prisoner may use that clause to present a claim of statutory innocence based on an intervening interpretation of the criminal statute when the claim is barred as second or successive under § 2255.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | Rejects the Court’s statutory-innocence exception but agrees that Barrett cannot proceed |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: Court author.

Announced ground: Section 2255 is not inadequate or ineffective merely because a prisoner omitted an available claim, lost an earlier application, or cannot satisfy AEDPA’s second-or-successive requirements. The savings clause nevertheless preserves a narrow route when an intervening substantive construction establishes that the prisoner’s conduct was not criminal and no earlier reasonable opportunity existed to present that claim.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: When the § 2255 remedy is inadequate or ineffective

Holding and operative rule: Section 2255 is not inadequate or ineffective merely because AEDPA bars a successive motion, an earlier motion failed, or the prisoner omitted a claim that could reasonably have been presented. A savings-clause route remains available when an intervening substantive interpretation establishes incarceration for conduct the criminal statute does not reach and the prisoner had no earlier reasonable opportunity to present that legal claim. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: The savings clause preserves a meaningful substitute when the § 2255 mechanism cannot test the legality of detention, but it does not turn § 2241 into an alternative appellate system for circumventing finality and successive-motion rules. AEDPA’s restrictions perform their ordinary work when the prisoner had one fair opportunity to challenge the conviction, even if the claim was omitted or mishandled. A materially different problem arises when controlling substantive law later establishes that the conduct of conviction was never criminal and the legal basis for that claim was unavailable during the earlier § 2255 proceeding. In that circumstance, applying the successive-motion bar without any substitute route would leave a prisoner confined for noncriminal conduct without a meaningful judicial channel. The exception concerns substantive statutory innocence, not ordinary evidentiary, procedural, or sentencing error.

##### Question 2: Barrett’s claimed route through § 2241

Holding and operative rule: Barrett may not use § 2241 or a residual writ to litigate his Jencks Act contention because the claim arose from the existing record, was legally available during his first § 2255 proceeding, and does not rest on an intervening substantive interpretation making his bank-robbery conduct noncriminal. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: Barrett knew of the interview transcript during his first collateral proceeding and attempted to refer to a Jencks Act theory in a reply memorandum. The inability to obtain merits review resulted from the timing and manner in which the theory was raised, not from a structural absence of any lawful § 2255 route. His claim also challenges the handling of evidence rather than the criminal reach of the bank-robbery statute. The savings clause therefore does not apply. Allowing § 2241 whenever a prisoner’s ordinary collateral claim is procedurally barred would make AEDPA’s gatekeeping provisions optional and erase the distinction between an unavailable remedy and an unsuccessfully used one.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas, concurred in the judgment. They would hold that § 2255 remains adequate when it afforded one lawful opportunity to test the conviction, even if a later statutory interpretation supplies a new argument, and would reserve any constitutional necessity exception for a case that actually presents it.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Felker v. Turpin*: clarified; AEDPA’s successive-application restrictions do not themselves make the ordinary collateral remedy inadequate, while the constitutional significance of eliminating every meaningful innocence channel remains reserved.

*Bousley v. United States*: extended; an intervening substantive interpretation may establish statutory innocence, but ordinary procedural default and actual-innocence requirements remain applicable when a lawful collateral route exists.

*McCleskey v. Zant*: followed; omission of an available claim does not create a new collateral-review entitlement.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Barrett may not proceed under § 2241, § 2255, or the All Writs Act on the Jencks Act claim before the Court. The judgment does not foreclose a qualifying future claimant whose conduct was made noncriminal by an intervening substantive construction and who lacked an earlier reasonable opportunity to present that claim.

### Arizona v. California

Argued April 25, 2000 | Decided June 19, 2000  
On exceptions to the report of the Special Master. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-392.pdf))  
Area of Law: Original jurisdiction; preclusion; Indian water rights

Questions Presented:

1. Do the earlier Colorado River proceedings or the Docket No. 320 consent judgment preclude the Fort Yuma boundary-water claim, and should the Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements be approved?

#### Judgment

Mixed original-jurisdiction disposition; component votes follow.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| State parties’ *Arizona I* exception | Overruled, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Thomas | The Fort Yuma boundary-water claim is not barred by *Arizona I* and returns for further original-jurisdiction proceedings |
| United States’ and Tribe’s Docket No. 320 exceptions | Sustained, 7-2 | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | O’Connor, Thomas | The consent judgment does not preclude litigation of the disputed reservation title and resulting water entitlement |
| Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements | Approved, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | The settlements shall be incorporated into the decree subject to the identified supplemental-decree procedure |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Ginsburg | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Breyer in full; O’Connor and Thomas as to settlement approval | Supports the mixed disposition | Complete opinion for seven Justices; settlement portions for O’Connor and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | O’Connor | Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Agrees with settlement approval but would sustain the preclusion defenses and bar the Fort Yuma claim |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to overrule the State parties’ *Arizona I* exception, sustain the United States’ and Tribe’s Docket No. 320 exceptions, approve both settlements, and remand the Fort Yuma matter.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s opinion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Preclusion of the Fort Yuma claim and approval of the settlements

Holding and operative rule: The State parties forfeited reliance on *Arizona I* by failing to raise the defense when they had ample opportunity and reason to do so. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer) The opaque Docket No. 320 consent judgment precludes only matters actually determined and does not establish that the disputed Fort Yuma title was adjudicated. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer) The Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements are approved on the Special Master’s record. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: The State parties participated throughout the earlier Colorado River proceedings but did not timely assert that *Arizona I* itself foreclosed the Fort Yuma boundary-water claim. Permitting that defense only after extensive additional proceedings would disregard ordinary preservation principles and the Court’s responsibility to manage a long-running original action fairly. Docket No. 320 supplies no independent bar. A consent judgment may settle claims broadly, but issue preclusion requires that the particular issue have been actually determined. The judgment’s language and record do not establish an adjudication of the disputed reservation boundary or title. The settlements concerning the Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservations, by contrast, were supported by the Special Master’s record and may be incorporated without deciding the remaining Fort Yuma title, quantity, riverbed, or private-right questions.

#### Concurrences

No separate concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Thomas, concurred in approving the settlements but dissented from the Fort Yuma disposition. They would treat the earlier proceedings and consent judgment as sufficient to bar renewed litigation over the disputed boundary lands.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Arizona v. California* (*Arizona I*): clarified; preclusion remains an affirmative defense that may be forfeited through unjustified failure to raise it during an available stage of the same original action.

*United States v. International Building Co.*: followed; an opaque consent judgment does not establish issue preclusion unless the record shows that the particular issue was actually determined.

Original-jurisdiction settlement authority: applied; the two submitted reservation settlements may be incorporated into the decree on the developed record without creating universal approval criteria.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The State parties’ *Arizona I* exception is overruled, the United States’ and Tribe’s Docket No. 320 exceptions are sustained, the Fort Mojave and Colorado River Reservation settlements are approved, and the Fort Yuma matter is remanded to the Special Master for the remaining original-jurisdiction proceedings.

Effect: The Fort Yuma claim may proceed to resolution of the disputed boundary, title, and water-quantity questions. No final determination is made concerning the boundary, riverbed title, private rights, or the amount of any additional Fort Yuma water entitlement.

### Santa Fe Independent School District v. Doe

Argued March 29, 2000 | Decided June 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
the football-game invocation policy held invalid under the Establishment Clause, 168 F.3d 806 (5th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-290.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Establishment Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Does the District’s policy permitting student-led, student-initiated prayer before football games violate the Establishment Clause?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that routing an institutionally structured school invocation through student elections does not convert it into private speech.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Student election and school-attributed prayer

Holding and operative rule: A public school may not use student elections to authorize and select an invocation that is institutionally structured, supervised, and delivered as part of a school event. The resulting religious exercise remains attributable to the school and violates the Establishment Clause.

Reasoning: The invocation occurs at a school-sponsored football game, over the school’s public-address system, under faculty supervision, and pursuant to a district policy governing whether the religious exercise will occur and who will conduct it. Those features distinguish the message from spontaneous private expression in a neutral forum. The student vote compounds rather than cures the constitutional problem because it permits a majority to decide whether an official school event will include a religious exercise and places dissenting students in the position of attending a school activity marked by officially structured worship. The Constitution does not forbid voluntary private prayer, student religious discussion, or genuinely neutral access. It prevents the District from assigning its own religious program to a student electoral mechanism.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would characterize the selected student’s remarks as private speech and would sustain the policy absent direct school composition of the prayer or compulsory attendance at the football game.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Lee v. Weisman*: applied and extended; school sponsorship and the pressure created by an institutionally structured religious exercise are not eliminated because the event is extracurricular or the speaker is a student.

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: distinguished; neutral access for independent private speakers does not govern a school policy designed to produce an invocation at the school’s own event.

*Board of Regents of the University of Wisconsin System v. Southworth*, decided March 22, 2000: applied; majority voting cannot substitute for the constitutional requirement of governmental neutrality.

*Capitol Square Review and Advisory Board v. Pinette*: distinguished; the invocation is embedded in a school event rather than independently sponsored expression in an open civic forum.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The District may not implement the challenged football-game invocation policy. Students remain free to pray privately, speak spontaneously, and participate in genuine neutral-access arrangements that do not involve school selection, control, financing, or attribution.

### Miller v. French; United States v. French

Argued April 18, 2000 | Decided June 19, 2000  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit; judgment below:  
the PLRA automatic-stay mechanism held categorically unconstitutional and its operation enjoined, 178 F.3d 437 (7th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-327.pdf))  
Area of Law: Separation of powers; prospective relief; PLRA

Questions Presented:

1. Does the PLRA’s automatic-stay provision impermissibly suspend existing prospective relief before a court adjudicates whether the new termination standards are satisfied?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.  
Opposing: Stevens, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | Stevens | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that Congress may change the law governing prospective relief and create an automatic stay, subject to an as-applied requirement that the court retain a meaningful opportunity to adjudicate the termination motion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Automatic suspension of prospective relief

Holding and operative rule: The PLRA’s automatic stay is not facially unconstitutional merely because it suspends prospective relief before final adjudication of a termination motion. Congress may change the substantive standards governing the continuing force of prospective relief and attach temporary statutory consequences to application of the new law. The statute may not, however, be applied through a timetable that deprives the court of a meaningful opportunity to decide whether the new termination standards are satisfied.

Reasoning: A continuing injunction remains subject to later changes in the law governing prospective relief. Congress did not command revision of completed factual findings or reopen a final damages judgment. It changed the legal conditions under which equitable relief remains enforceable and provided that a pending termination motion would temporarily stay relief after the statutory period. That structure does not facially prescribe the outcome of a particular case or transfer adjudication to Congress. The Seventh Circuit therefore erred in invalidating the mechanism categorically. A different constitutional issue may arise if the statutory deadlines, including any authorized extension, are so short in a particular complex case that the court cannot perform the judicial task of determining whether the injunction remains lawful under the new standard. The record does not establish that circumstance, so the question must be addressed on remand rather than presumed.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, would preserve judicial authority to suspend or modify the automatic stay when necessary to adjudicate the termination motion responsibly. They would affirm the injunction or construe the statute to avoid automatic displacement of a potentially valid prospective order.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Plaut v. Spendthrift Farm, Inc.*: clarified; Congress may alter the prospective operation of continuing equitable relief even though it may not reopen a final private judgment and require a different completed disposition.

*Agostini v. Felton*: distinguished; lower courts may not treat controlling precedent as silently displaced, but Congress may enact a new rule governing the future enforceability of prospective relief.

*United States v. Klein*: distinguished; the PLRA supplies a new generally applicable legal standard rather than prescribing factual findings or a result under unchanged law.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Seventh Circuit is reversed, and the consolidated cases are remanded for application of the PLRA’s automatic-stay and termination provisions and for any properly developed as-applied challenge concerning the court’s opportunity to adjudicate the motion.

Effect: The categorical injunction against the automatic stay cannot remain in force. Existing prospective relief is governed by the PLRA, but the lower court may consider whether the actual statutory timetable, including available extensions, affords a meaningful opportunity to determine whether the relief satisfies the new law.

### Crosby v. National Foreign Trade Council

Argued March 22, 2000 | Decided June 19, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the First Circuit; judgment below:  
Massachusetts’s Burma procurement restriction held preempted and permanently enjoined, 181 F.3d 38 (1st Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-363.pdf))  
Area of Law: Federal preemption; foreign affairs

Questions Presented:

1. Is Massachusetts’s Burma procurement restriction preempted by the federal sanctions statute?

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s source-specific finding that Massachusetts’s procurement sanction conflicts with the integrated federal sanctions regime.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Conflict with the federal Burma sanctions strategy

Holding and operative rule: A state procurement sanction is preempted when it broadens the class of conduct penalized by a federal foreign-policy statute, removes flexibility Congress assigned to the President, and obstructs the federal government’s multilateral strategy. Preemption rests on the concrete statutory conflict, not on a free-standing judicial foreign-affairs power.

Reasoning: Congress imposed specified sanctions on Burma while authorizing the President to calibrate, suspend, or terminate those measures according to statutory findings and diplomatic developments. Massachusetts adopted a broader purchasing prohibition with different coverage and fewer exceptions. The state law therefore reduced the President’s ability to use the leverage Congress supplied, penalized entities Congress left outside the federal restriction, and complicated the national government’s effort to obtain coordinated action by other countries. Those conflicts arise from the federal statute’s text, structure, and assigned mechanisms. The Court does not adopt a general rule displacing state action whenever foreign affairs are implicated. Neutral procurement standards, nonbinding governmental statements, and state action within genuine room left by federal law remain available.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Locke*, decided March 6, 2000: followed; federal displacement must be tied to enacted law, a valid regulation, or an affirmative federal determination rather than generalized agency preference.

*Geier v. American Honda Motor Co.*, decided May 22, 2000: applied; a state rule is preempted when its legal operation obstructs a concrete objective embedded in the governing federal regime.

*Hines v. Davidowitz*: followed; state law must yield when it materially interferes with the method Congress selected for executing federal policy.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the First Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The permanent injunction against enforcement of Massachusetts’s Burma procurement restriction remains in force. Massachusetts may continue using neutral purchasing rules and may act in areas genuinely left open by federal law, but it may not enforce a sanction that alters the coverage, flexibility, or diplomatic operation of the federal regime.

### Eisenberg v. Montgomery County Public Schools

Argued April 17, 2000 | Decided June 20, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
denial of preliminary relief reversed and injunctive relief directed against the race-determinative transfer policy, 197 F.3d 123 (4th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/197/123/546523/))  
Area of Law: Equal Protection; public education

Questions Presented:

1. Whether a public school system that is not remedying its own adjudicated constitutional violation may make an individual student’s race determinative in granting or denying a voluntary school transfer.
2. Whether racial diversity or avoidance of racial isolation in K-12 schools, standing apart from remediation of identified governmental discrimination, constitutes a constitutionally sufficient interest for an individual racial classification.
3. If such an interest can qualify, what requirements govern inclusionary integration measures, including race-neutral alternatives, individualized treatment, duration and review, and racial targets tied to systemwide percentages.
4. Whether maintaining racial balance for its own sake is constitutionally distinct from pursuing educational diversity.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.  
Opposing: Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion announcing the judgment; Opinion of the Court in issue-specific parts | Stone | O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to Question 1, the case-specific applications in Questions 3 and 4, and the judgment; Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer as to Question 2 and the general rule in Question 3; Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer as to the general distinction in Question 4 | Supports affirmance | Issue-specific Court portions as stated |
| Concurrence in part and in the judgment | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Supports affirmance | Would apply strict scrutiny to every governmental racial classification and rejects the intermediate-scrutiny category |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Breyer | Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg | Would reverse | Joins the integration-interest and structural-fit holdings but concludes that the challenged policy should survive |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.

Public role: Court author.

Announced ground: A race-determinative decision allocating an identified school seat receives strict scrutiny. Stone separately concludes, with a different majority, that genuinely inclusionary structural K-12 measures may pursue meaningful integration under demanding intermediate scrutiny, while racial balance for its own sake remains insufficient.

#### Decision of the Court

Judgment announced by Stone. No single opinion of the Court commands a majority across all four questions.

##### Question 1: Race-determinative transfer decision

Holding and operative rule: A school system not remedying its own adjudicated constitutional violation may not make an individual student’s race determinative of access to a specific school or program unless the classification satisfies strict scrutiny. Montgomery County’s denial of Eisenberg’s transfer because he is white does not satisfy that standard.

Reasoning: The challenged rule decides whether an identified child receives an identified school seat by comparing his race with the school’s and county’s racial percentages. That is a discrete racial allocation, not merely a structural policy affecting attendance patterns generally. Montgomery County has never been subject to a desegregation order and identifies no adjudicated violation supplying a remedial predicate. Nor does the record establish that denying this transfer was necessary, that workable race-neutral measures were inadequate, or that the classification was bounded by individualized narrow tailoring. The Fourth Circuit therefore correctly prohibited use of the percentage rule against Eisenberg.

##### Question 2: Integration and avoidance of serious racial isolation

Holding and operative rule: Meaningful K-12 integration and prevention of serious racial isolation may constitute an important governmental objective for a genuinely inclusionary structural measure, even apart from remediation of an adjudicated constitutional violation. Such a measure receives intermediate scrutiny only when its purpose and operation concern integration of the institution rather than allocation of an individual benefit by race. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer)

Reasoning: A public school system may have a substantial educational interest in ensuring that students participate meaningfully across racial lines and that its institutions do not become seriously isolated. A bounded structural measure serving that objective does not necessarily operate like an exclusion, quota, or individual racial entitlement. The constitutional treatment therefore turns on legal operation rather than a benign label. Intermediate scrutiny requires an important integration objective and a substantial relationship between the objective and the actual mechanism. This is not a general exception for preferred racial classifications and does not permit government to administer schools indefinitely by proportional racial targets.

##### Question 3: Fit and boundaries of an inclusionary measure

Holding and operative rule: A qualifying inclusionary measure must expand or preserve meaningful interracial educational participation, avoid racial entitlement to a school or resource, avoid stigma and stereotype, reject racial percentages as ends in themselves, consider workable race-neutral means, and contain meaningful limits on scope, duration, review, and termination. (joined by Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer) Montgomery County’s policy determines an identified transfer by race and therefore lies outside that structural category. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas)

Reasoning: Intermediate scrutiny remains demanding. Demographic information may inform program design, but percentages cannot become permanent decisional commands. Structural tools such as attendance-zone design, transportation, program location, recruitment, and resource distribution may be evaluated according to how they affect institutional integration without assigning a racial entitlement to a particular child. Montgomery County instead used systemwide racial percentages to decide Eisenberg’s individual application. The judgment coalition therefore treats the policy as a discrete race-determinative allocation governed by Question 1, while the separate five-Justice majority establishes the rule for genuinely structural measures.

##### Question 4: Educational integration and racial balance

Holding and operative rule: Educational integration and prevention of serious racial isolation are constitutionally distinct from racial balance for its own sake; proportional racial numbers cannot serve as an independent governmental end. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer) The challenged transfer rule uses proportionality as the decisional mechanism for an individual benefit and is invalid. (joined by Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas)

Reasoning: Integration concerns the institution’s educational operation and the opportunity for meaningful participation across racial lines. Racial balance treats correspondence with a demographic percentage as the objective itself. The latter cannot justify assigning or withholding a specific governmental benefit according to race. Montgomery County’s policy compares each school’s racial composition with countywide percentages and then grants or denies an individual transfer according to the applicant’s race. That mechanism does not merely collect demographic information or structure a generally applicable program. It turns proportionality into the rule of decision and therefore cannot be sustained.

#### Concurrences

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas, concurred in part and in the judgment. They would apply strict scrutiny to every governmental racial classification and would not recognize a distinct intermediate-scrutiny category for nonremedial K-12 integration measures.

#### Dissents

Breyer, joined by Stevens, Souter, and Ginsburg, agreed that integration is distinct from racial balance and joined the Court’s rules governing structural inclusionary measures. They would characterize Montgomery County’s policy as a fitted integration measure rather than an unconstitutional allocation and would reverse the Fourth Circuit.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Adarand Constructors, Inc. v. Peña*: extended; strict scrutiny continues to govern race-determinative allocation of a discrete governmental benefit, while the nonexclusive-access principle extends to genuinely inclusionary structural K-12 measures under demanding intermediate scrutiny.

*Missouri v. Jenkins*: clarified; racial balance and demographic reversal remain insufficient independent objectives, although a bounded nonremedial integration measure may rest on the distinct educational interest recognized here.

*Freeman v. Pitts*: extended; scope, duration, periodic review, and termination remain material limits on continuing race-conscious educational administration.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The injunction against Montgomery County’s race-determinative transfer rule remains in force, and Eisenberg may not be denied the requested transfer under that mechanism. The school system may employ race-neutral measures and may pursue genuinely inclusionary structural integration under the rule announced by the separate five-Justice majority, but it may not use proportional racial targets to determine an identified student’s access to a particular seat.

### Dickerson v. United States

Argued April 19, 2000 | Decided June 26, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
suppression reversed under 18 U.S.C. § 3501’s voluntariness rule, 166 F.3d 667 (4th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-428.pdf))  
Area of Law: Fifth Amendment; Miranda; criminal procedure

Questions Presented:

1. May Congress supersede *Miranda* with § 3501’s voluntariness-centered admissibility regime?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.

Public role: Court author.

Announced ground: *Miranda* is a constitutional admissibility framework that Congress may supplement or satisfy through genuinely equivalent safeguards but may not replace with voluntariness alone.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Congressional replacement of Miranda

Holding and operative rule: *Miranda* and its constitutional progeny govern the admissibility of statements obtained through custodial interrogation in state and federal courts. Congress may supplement that framework or provide genuinely equivalent safeguards, but § 3501 may not replace it with voluntariness as the sole admissibility rule.

Reasoning: *Miranda* implements the Fifth Amendment’s protection against compelled self-incrimination in the distinctive setting of custodial interrogation. Its application to the States, its operation as a rule of admissibility, and the Court’s repeated development of custody, interrogation, waiver, and exception principles establish that it is not merely a supervisory direction to federal courts. Congress retains authority to regulate evidence and to provide safeguards at least equivalent to the constitutional framework. Section 3501 does not do that. It omits the warning and waiver requirements and makes traditional voluntariness the controlling test, thereby reinstating the regime *Miranda* found insufficient. The decision does not make the exact original warning formula indispensable or eliminate recognized limits and exceptions. It holds only that voluntariness alone cannot supersede the constitutional safeguard.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas, would treat *Miranda* as a judicially created prophylactic rule rather than a constitutional command and would permit Congress to restore voluntariness as the statutory test for federal prosecutions.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Miranda v. Arizona*: reaffirmed and clarified; the warnings-and-waiver framework is constitutional in status, although genuinely equivalent safeguards remain possible.

*Oregon v. Elstad* and *Harris v. New York*: followed; recognized limits on exclusion and uses outside the prosecution’s case in chief remain intact.

18 U.S.C. § 3501: limited; it is inoperative insofar as it makes voluntariness alone sufficient to admit a statement obtained in violation of the governing constitutional framework.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is reversed, and the District Court’s suppression order is restored.

Effect: The Government may not use Dickerson’s unwarned custodial statement in its case in chief on the present record. The prosecution may continue without that use and may invoke any properly preserved exception recognized by the governing doctrine.

### Apprendi v. New Jersey

Argued March 28, 2000 | Decided June 26, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of New Jersey; judgment below:  
the twelve-year enhanced sentence sustained, 159 N.J. 7, 731 A.2d 485 (1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-466.pdf))  
Area of Law: Sixth Amendment; due process; sentencing

Questions Presented:

1. Must a nonrecidivist fact authorizing punishment above the verdict- or plea-supported maximum be submitted to a jury and proved beyond a reasonable doubt?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Kennedy, Breyer.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Scalia, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Scalia | No other Justice | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Thomas | Scalia as to the historical and offense-element analysis | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion except as stated |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Kennedy, Breyer | Would affirm | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Breyer | No other Justice | Would affirm | Sentencing-administration ground |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of the existing simulated rule that a fact increasing the punishment authorized by the charged offense is an element requiring a charge, admission, or jury finding beyond a reasonable doubt.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Maximum-increasing nonrecidivist facts

Holding and operative rule: A nonrecidivist fact legally necessary to authorize punishment above the maximum supported by the indictment and verdict or plea is an offense element. It must be charged and either admitted as an element or proved to a jury beyond a reasonable doubt. New Jersey could not authorize Apprendi’s twelve-year sentence through a judicial preponderance finding when his admitted offense carried a ten-year maximum.

Reasoning: The constitutional inquiry turns on the legal work the disputed fact performs, not the legislature’s sentencing-factor label. Apprendi admitted an offense carrying a maximum sentence of ten years. The judge’s later finding that he acted with a racially biased purpose supplied the sole legal authority for an additional two years of imprisonment. That finding therefore defined an aggravated offense. Simulated *United States v. Gaudin* requires the jury to determine every offense element, and simulated *Almendarez-Torres v. United States* and *Jones v. United States* establish that maximum-increasing effect makes a fact an element. A sentencing court may continue finding facts used to select a sentence within the range already authorized by the conviction. It may not create authority for a higher maximum through its own preponderance finding.

#### Concurrences

Scalia emphasized that the jury guarantee concerns who determines facts authorizing punishment, not whether judicial sentencing might be more efficient or consistent.

Thomas, joined by Scalia as to the historical and offense-element analysis, concluded that the element rule follows from the traditional distinction between greater and lesser crimes. He would not recognize an uncharged maximum-increasing recidivism exception, a question outside the granted issue.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Kennedy and Breyer, would treat biased purpose as a sentencing consideration and would overrule or confine the simulated maximum-increasing-element precedents. They warned that the Court’s rule restricts legislative authority to structure sentencing ranges.

Breyer separately emphasized the administrative and comparative-sentencing difficulties created by requiring jury adjudication of facts previously used by judges to select punishment.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Gaudin*: applied; the jury must determine every fact constituting the aggravated offense.

*Almendarez-Torres v. United States*, decided March 24, 1998: followed; a fact increasing the conviction-authorized maximum is an element rather than an ordinary sentencing factor.

*Jones v. United States*, decided March 24, 1999: applied; statutory structure and maximum-increasing effect require aggravated-offense treatment.

*United States v. Watts; United States v. Putra*: distinguished; judicial factfinding remains permissible only within the punishment already authorized by the conviction.

*Castillo v. United States*, decided June 5, 2000: applied; offense-related facts carrying aggravated statutory punishment require element treatment when the governing law makes them necessary to the higher sentence.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of New Jersey is reversed, Apprendi’s enhanced sentence is vacated, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with the offense-element rule.

Effect: Apprendi’s guilty plea and conviction for the admitted offense remain intact. New Jersey may impose a sentence within the authorized ten-year range and may undertake any further proceeding permitted by state law and the governing notice, plea, waiver, sufficiency, finality, and double-jeopardy rules.

### California Democratic Party v. Jones

Argued April 24, 2000 | Decided June 26, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit; judgment below:  
California’s blanket-primary system sustained, 169 F.3d 646 (9th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-567.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; political association; elections

Questions Presented:

1. May California require political parties to select their nominees through a blanket primary open to every voter regardless of affiliation?

#### Judgment

Reversed, 7-2.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stevens, Ginsburg.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Scalia | Stone, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Breyer | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Kennedy | No other Justice | Supports reversal | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | Ginsburg as to the principal associational analysis | Would affirm | Complete opinion except as stated |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that compelled participation by nonmembers in selecting a party’s official nominee severely burdens political association.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Compelled outsider participation in nominee selection

Holding and operative rule: A State may not compel a political party to select its official nominee through a blanket primary in which voters unaffiliated with that party, including adherents of competing parties, participate on equal terms. Because the burden falls on the party’s central associational decision, the requirement receives the most demanding review and cannot be sustained by California’s asserted interests.

Reasoning: The choice of a standard-bearer determines which candidate speaks for the party in the general election and is among the party’s central associational acts. California’s system does not merely regulate ballot form, timing, access, or neutral election administration. It transfers participation in that decision to every voter, including persons who reject the party’s principles or support a rival organization. Interests in participation, moderation, voter choice, and privacy may support less intrusive election designs, but they do not justify compelled outsider control of a party nomination. California may employ nonpartisan or top-two elections, permit parties to open their own primaries, and regulate ordinary mechanics. It may not confer the party label through a process the party cannot limit to its own chosen electorate.

#### Concurrences

Kennedy emphasized that compelling parties to accept nominees shaped by outsiders risks altering the content of party advocacy itself and cannot be justified as ordinary regulation of a state-created ballot.

#### Dissents

Stevens, joined by Ginsburg as to the principal associational analysis, would treat the primary as part of California’s public election machinery and would sustain the burden in light of the State’s interests in broad participation, moderation, and voter choice.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Eu v. San Francisco County Democratic Central Committee*: followed; the State may regulate elections but may not control the substance of internal party decisionmaking without constitutionally sufficient justification.

*Tashjian v. Republican Party of Connecticut*: followed; the party ordinarily retains authority to define participation in selecting its nominee.

*Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston*: applied; compelled inclusion is constitutionally severe when it alters an association’s selection of the person who will carry its message.

*Reform Party of Allegheny County v. Allegheny County Department of Elections*, decided May 8, 2000: clarified by contrast; neutral ballot administration and equal access differ from transferring the party’s standard-bearer choice to nonmembers.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Ninth Circuit is reversed, and California may not enforce the blanket-primary requirement against the petitioner political parties.

Effect: The parties regain control over participation in selecting their official nominees. California remains free to adopt nonpartisan or top-two elections, party-authorized open primaries, and neutral rules governing ballot access, timing, and administration.

### Mobil Oil Exploration & Producing Southeast, Inc. v. United States; Marathon Oil Co. v. United States

Argued March 22, 2000 | Decided June 26, 2000  
On writs of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit; judgment below:  
the Government held not liable for breach of the offshore leases, 177 F.3d 1331 (Fed. Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-604.pdf))  
Area of Law: Government contracts; restitution

Questions Presented:

1. Did the Government materially breach offshore leases by refusing to process exploration plans under the incorporated legal regime after Congress imposed new conditions?

#### Judgment

Reversed and remanded, 8-1.  
Supporting: Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Stevens.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg | Supports reversal and remand | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Stevens | No other Justice | Would affirm | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to reverse and remand.

Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.

Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the Government materially repudiated the leases by withholding the contracted approval process because of a later statutory condition outside the lessees’ assumed risk.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Repudiation of the contracted approval process

Holding and operative rule: When the Government contracts to provide a specified approval process under an incorporated legal regime, its refusal to provide that process because of a later statutory condition outside the counterparty’s assumed risk constitutes material repudiation. The injured party may elect restitution of the consideration paid without proving that full performance would ultimately have produced a profit or final regulatory approval.

Reasoning: The offshore leases did not guarantee that the companies would receive every permit or obtain commercially successful production. They did promise access to a defined statutory and regulatory process in exchange for substantial up-front payments. The later statute prevented Interior from acting under that promised process until additional conditions, not incorporated into the leases, were met. The Government remained free to enact and enforce the new law, but the change did not transfer the financial consequence of nonperformance to the lessees. Their later participation in administrative steps did not waive the repudiation. Because they seek restitution rather than lost profits, they need not prove that the original process would have produced final approval or economic gain.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Stevens would hold that the leases made development contingent on continuing regulatory approval and assigned the companies the risk that the governing process might change before exploration was authorized. He would affirm the Federal Circuit’s judgment for the Government.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*United States v. Winstar Corp.*: followed; lawful later legislation remains fully effective, but an authorized government contract may allocate the financial consequences when the change prevents promised performance.

Government-contract restitution: established; a party may recover transferred consideration after material governmental repudiation without proving the expectancy value of successful final performance.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit is reversed, and the consolidated cases are remanded for entry and administration of restitution consistent with this opinion.

Effect: The lessees are entitled to return of the proved up-front payments, subject to lawful credits and offsets. Any award of interest requires an independent statutory or contractual basis. The judgment neither guarantees exploration approval nor awards expected profits from offshore production.
