## Decisions and Dispositions

### Mixon v. Ohio

Argued April 24, 2000 | Decided June 27, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit; judgment below:  
state-law and direct federal Equal Protection claims against Ohio dismissed, and judgment for defendants otherwise affirmed, 193 F.3d 389 (6th Cir. 1999). ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/193/389/477494/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))  
Area of Law: Voting Rights Act; Equal Protection; one-person-one-vote

Questions Presented:

1. Whether § 2 of the Voting Rights Act reaches a State’s replacement of an elected local governing body with an appointed body, thereby eliminating the electorate’s opportunity to vote for that office.
2. If § 2 reaches such a restructuring, what benchmark governs when the asserted injury is elimination of the election itself rather than dilution within an existing electoral system.
3. Whether the Equal Protection Clause permits a State to replace an elected governing body with an appointed body when the restructuring was adopted for a racially discriminatory purpose, even though the Constitution does not independently require that office to be elective.
4. Whether Equal Protection or one-person-one-vote principles are violated when a governing board serving residents of several municipalities is appointed by a mayor elected only by residents of one of those municipalities.
5. Whether the absence of an independent constitutional right to elect the particular office defeats a discriminatory-purpose claim, or merely means that a neutral decision to use appointment rather than election would ordinarily be constitutional.

#### Judgment

Affirmed in part, reversed in part, and remanded, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

| Judgment component | Disposition and vote | Supporting | Opposing | Relief or remand |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Direct state-law and federal Equal Protection claims against Ohio | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Ohio’s sovereign-immunity dismissal remains in force |
| Voting Rights Act § 2 claim | Reversed and remanded, 6-3 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Apply the immediately preceding electoral system as the benchmark and determine unequal political opportunity under the statutory totality |
| Intentional-discrimination claim against Cleveland’s mayor or another proper nonimmune defendant | Reversed and remanded, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Apply the governing purpose, causation, and same-decision rules without presuming discriminatory purpose |
| Standalone one-person-one-vote claim | Affirmed, 9-0 | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | No Justice | Rejection of that claim remains in force |

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stone | Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer in full; Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas as to the sovereign-immunity, discriminatory-purpose, one-person-one-vote, and no-independent-right holdings | Supports affirmance in part, reversal in part, and remand | Complete opinion for six Justices; identified issue propositions for Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas |
| Concurrence in part and dissent in part | Scalia | Kennedy, Thomas | Concurs in part and dissents in part | Joins the constitutional and immunity holdings but rejects § 2 coverage of the election-to-appointment change |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm in part, reverse in part, and remand.  
Public role: Court author.  
Announced ground: Section 2 reaches a direct restructuring that removes an existing electoral opportunity when a concrete lawful benchmark is available, but liability still requires unequal political opportunity. Separately, governmental discretion to make an office appointive does not include authority to restructure it for a racially discriminatory purpose.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Section 2 coverage of an election-to-appointment change

Holding and operative rule: A State’s replacement of an existing lawful elected governing body with an appointed body may be tested under § 2 because the change removes an established electoral opportunity. Cognizability does not itself establish vote dilution; protected voters must still prove that the restructuring leaves them with less political opportunity than other citizens.

Reasoning: Section 2 reaches standards, practices, and procedures governing the opportunity to participate in the political process. The challenged enactment did not concern an office that had always been appointive. It altered the machinery through which district residents had previously selected the school board. That concrete transition is cognizable without creating a perpetual statutory right to elect every school board. *Holder v. Hall* requires an objective comparator and separates comparator sufficiency from ultimate liability. The plaintiffs therefore must establish, under the full statutory totality, that the new structure produces racial inequality in political opportunity, not merely that every resident lost a direct vote. The Sixth Circuit’s categorical exclusion of the restructuring from § 2 cannot stand. `OT_1999CHUNK9.md` ([law.justia.com](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/193/389/477494/?utm_source=chatgpt.com))

##### Question 2: Benchmark for elimination of an existing election

Holding and operative rule: The lawful electoral system operating immediately before the challenged restructuring supplies the objective benchmark. That comparison establishes the baseline for analysis, not the existence of a violation.

Reasoning: Unlike a challenge requiring a court to invent the ideal size or form of a governing body, this case supplies an administrable comparator chosen and operated by the State itself. The former elected board identifies the electoral opportunity removed by the later enactment and permits a concrete comparison of participation, representation, and practical political access. The benchmark remains subject to the ordinary requirement that the prior arrangement was lawful. It also does not relieve the plaintiffs of proving racial inequality under § 2’s totality. The universal disappearance of a direct election, standing alone, remains insufficient.

##### Question 3: Racially discriminatory restructuring

Holding and operative rule: A proper nonimmune defendant may not implement or maintain replacement of an elected body with an appointed body when racial discrimination materially motivated the restructuring, even though a racially neutral appointment system would be constitutional. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Equal protection regulates the use of governmental power as well as the substantive arrangements government ordinarily may select. A State may generally choose appointment rather than election for a local administrative body, but that authority does not include choosing or maintaining the structure to disadvantage citizens because of race. The direct constitutional claims against Ohio remain barred by sovereign immunity. The claim against Cleveland’s mayor or another proper nonimmune defendant presents a different remedial vehicle. Because the lower courts rejected the theory without applying the governing discriminatory-purpose and causation framework, remand is required. The plaintiffs retain the burden to prove that race materially influenced the restructuring; disparate impact, awareness of racial consequences, or political disagreement alone is insufficient. A defendant may establish that independently sufficient lawful reasons would have produced the identical structure.

##### Question 4: One-person-one-vote and appointment power

Holding and operative rule: A genuinely appointive multi-jurisdictional governing body does not violate one-person-one-vote merely because the appointing mayor is elected only by residents of one participating municipality. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: One-person-one-vote principally governs the election of officials who exercise governmental authority. Once the school board is genuinely appointive, residents of every area served by it do not acquire a constitutional right to vote for each official who participates in the appointment process. Cleveland voters elected the mayor, but that election did not become a districtwide election of the school board. The arrangement may remain subject to a proven discriminatory-purpose challenge, and a nominal appointment system functioning as an indirect electoral device would present a different question. The narrower electorate of the appointing officer does not, by itself, establish malapportionment.

##### Question 5: Effect of no independent right to elect the board

Holding and operative rule: The absence of a freestanding right to elect the school board means that a neutral appointment structure is ordinarily constitutional. It does not immunize a restructuring materially motivated by racial discrimination. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Governmental discretion and constitutional neutrality are separate inquiries. The Constitution does not require Ohio to preserve an elected school board merely because one existed before. It does require the State and proper nonimmune officials to exercise structural authority without purposeful racial discrimination. The statutory § 2 claim, the constitutional purpose claim, and the one-person-one-vote claim therefore remain distinct, with separate elements, defenses, and remedies.

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Kennedy and Thomas: They agree that racial purpose remains independently actionable and that the appointive arrangement does not violate one-person-one-vote. They would hold that § 2 does not govern a decision to eliminate the election universally, because an appointive office supplies no continuing voting process in which one racial group has less opportunity than another.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Holder v. Hall*: extended and clarified; an immediately preceding lawful electoral system may provide the objective benchmark for a direct election-to-appointment restructuring, but benchmark sufficiency does not establish § 2 liability.

*Chisom v. Roemer*: extended; when a State has chosen and operated an election, § 2 may examine a later practice eliminating that electoral opportunity, subject to proof of racial inequality.

*Village of Arlington Heights v. Metropolitan Housing Development Corp.*: applied; governmental authority to adopt a neutral structure does not protect the same structure when racial discrimination materially motivated its adoption or maintenance.

*Sailors v. Board of Education*: followed; one-person-one-vote does not govern a genuinely appointive board merely because the appointing official’s electorate is narrower than the board’s service area.

*Hadley v. Junior College District*: distinguished; its apportionment rule governs officials selected through election, not a genuinely appointive body.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Sixth Circuit is affirmed as to the direct claims against Ohio and the standalone one-person-one-vote claim, reversed as to the Voting Rights Act and intentional-discrimination claims, and the case is remanded for proceedings consistent with this opinion.

Effect: Ohio remains immune from the direct state-law and federal Equal Protection claims. The plaintiffs may attempt to prove a § 2 violation against the proper defendants under the prior electoral benchmark and may pursue the discriminatory-purpose claim against a proper nonimmune official. No violation, restoration of an elected board, or final remedy is established at this stage.

### Boy Scouts of America v. Dale

Argued April 26, 2000 | Decided June 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of New Jersey; judgment below:  
application of the public-accommodations law sustained, 160 N.J. 562, 734 A.2d 1196 (1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-640.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; expressive association; public accommodations

Questions Presented:

1. Whether applying New Jersey’s public-accommodations law to require the Boy Scouts to retain an otherwise qualified openly gay adult leader violates the organization’s First Amendment right of expressive association.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s requirement of a record-supported nexus among the organization’s defined message, the particular role at issue, and the expressive alteration allegedly caused by inclusion.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Expressive-association nexus

Holding and operative rule: A generally applicable public-accommodations law may require an open civic organization to retain an otherwise qualified participant unless the organization proves that the particular role carries a defined organizational message and that inclusion would materially alter expression attributable to the group.

Reasoning: The Boy Scouts engages in protected expressive activity, but recognition of expressive association does not make every membership or leadership decision constitutionally immune from neutral access law. The organization must identify the expression at issue, connect the particular role to communication of that expression, and show a material alteration caused by inclusion. *Hurley* involved a parade contingent formed to communicate an identified message within the organizers’ expressive event. The record here does not establish that an assistant scoutmaster speaks for the organization on sexual-orientation policy, that the organization required leaders to communicate the asserted position, or that Dale sought to use the role to alter the Scouts’ program. Deference to an organization’s sincere description of its views does not require conclusive acceptance of a litigation label disconnected from the function of the disputed role. New Jersey may therefore enforce its neutral public-accommodations law on this record. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-640.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: The Boy Scouts is an expressive association, and its adult leaders help embody and transmit its moral program. They would defer to the organization’s sincere account of that program and hold that compelled retention of Dale materially interferes with the Scouts’ selection of those who convey its values.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Hurley v. Irish-American Gay, Lesbian and Bisexual Group of Boston*: clarified; compelled-inclusion protection requires a record-supported relationship between a defined message, the particular role, and the asserted expressive alteration.

*Roberts v. United States Jaycees*: followed; neutral public-accommodations regulation remains valid when inclusion does not materially alter protected expression.

*California Democratic Party v. Jones*, decided June 26, 2000: distinguished; an official party nominee is the association’s chosen standard-bearer, while this record does not establish that an assistant scoutmaster carries a defined organizational message concerning the disputed subject.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of New Jersey is affirmed.

Effect: New Jersey may apply its public-accommodations law to require Dale’s reinstatement and administer the relief authorized by state law. The Boy Scouts remains free to advocate its views, select genuine spokespersons, define message-bearing roles, and discipline role-related conduct through neutral standards. The decision does not govern intimate associations or roles shown to carry an attributed organizational message.

### Hill v. Colorado

Argued January 19, 2000 | Decided June 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the Supreme Court of Colorado; judgment below:  
facial First Amendment challenge rejected and the statute sustained, 973 P.2d 1246 (Colo. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-703.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; time, place, and manner regulation

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Colorado may prohibit a person within 100 feet of a health-care entrance from knowingly approaching within eight feet of another, without consent, for leafletting, sign display, protest, education, or counseling.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Stevens | Stone, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Souter | O’Connor, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Content-neutrality and tailoring |
| Dissent | Scalia | Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that the knowledge, consent, and eight-foot limits preserve realistic face-to-face advocacy while narrowly regulating speaker-initiated approaches to unwilling listeners.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Knowing nonconsensual approaches near health-care entrances

Holding and operative rule: A content-neutral prohibition on knowingly approaching within eight feet of another person, without consent, within 100 feet of a health-care entrance is a valid time, place, and manner restriction when stationary advocacy, consensual conversation, signs, audible communication, and communication with willing listeners remain available.

Reasoning: The statute regulates the manner of a speaker-initiated approach rather than the viewpoint expressed. Its broad categories of protest, education, counseling, leafletting, and sign display do not favor one side of a public dispute, and the State’s interests in safe access, avoidance of unwanted close pursuit, and administrable enforcement are unrelated to disagreement with any message. The eight-foot distance permits ordinary conversation and visible signs, while the consent limitation leaves willing exchanges untouched. A speaker may remain stationary as another person passes within eight feet and may offer a leaflet that the recipient can accept. The knowledge requirement protects against accidental violations. These features distinguish the law from the uncertain 15-foot floating zones invalidated in *Schenck*. The statute creates neither a general speech-free zone nor a right to silence stationary speakers. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-703.pdf))

#### Concurrences

Souter, joined by O’Connor, Ginsburg, and Breyer: Statutory reference to communicative purposes does not make the restriction content based when coverage turns on the act of initiating a close approach and applies without regard to viewpoint. The knowledge and consent requirements materially confine both enforcement discretion and the burden on speech.

#### Dissents

Scalia, joined by Thomas: The law singles out approaches made for expressive purposes and operates in practice against abortion opponents engaged in sidewalk counseling. They would treat it as content based and facially invalid.

Kennedy: A consent requirement imposed before a speaker may approach within ordinary conversational distance gives each listener substantial control over speech on a public sidewalk. He would hold that the State’s interests can be addressed through direct rules against obstruction, threats, touching, and persistent following.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Schenck v. Pro-Choice Network of Western New York*: clarified; a knowing eight-foot approach restriction preserving stationary and consensual communication differs constitutionally from an uncertain 15-foot floating zone and automatic retreat requirement.

*Madsen v. Women’s Health Center, Inc.*: distinguished; the challenged measure is a generally applicable statute rather than a party-specific injunction.

*Ward v. Rock Against Racism*: applied; the law is content neutral, narrowly tailored to significant interests, and leaves ample realistic channels of communication.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Supreme Court of Colorado is affirmed.

Effect: Colorado may enforce the knowing eight-foot approach restriction within the designated 100-foot areas. The decision does not authorize a broad speech-free zone, compel stationary speakers to retreat, restrict consensual communication, punish conduct without knowledge, or foreclose a discriminatory-enforcement or materially different as-applied challenge.

### Mitchell v. Helms

Argued December 1, 1999 | Decided June 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit; judgment below:  
Chapter 2 invalidated as administered in Jefferson Parish, 151 F.3d 347 (5th Cir. 1998). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-793.pdf))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; Establishment Clause; educational aid

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Chapter 2 violates the Establishment Clause by placing publicly purchased instructional materials and equipment under participating religious schools’ control.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 5-4.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Dissent | O’Connor | Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas | Would reverse | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s distinction between neutral eligibility and direct provision of fungible instructional resources placed under a religious institution’s practical control.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: School-controlled instructional resources

Holding and operative rule: Neutral allocation does not validate direct provision of fungible instructional resources when a religious school controls their classroom use and the aid can substitute for institutional expense. Genuinely student-directed benefits and publicly controlled services remain constitutionally distinct.

Reasoning: Chapter 2 supplies computers, software, library materials, and comparable instructional resources according to enrollment-based formulas. Neutral eligibility is relevant, but it does not answer whether public resources remain publicly controlled or instead become institutional support for the recipient school’s own instructional program. As administered in Jefferson Parish, religious schools exercised day-to-day classroom control over fungible items capable of replacing expenditures the institutions otherwise would have borne. The allocation was made to schools through a governmental formula, not through an independent student or parental decision transferring unrestricted private value. Public retention of title and general restrictions on religious use do not overcome practical institutional control and substitution on this record. *Southworth* protects viewpoint-neutral support for a genuine private-speech program, while *Strout v. Albanese*, decided April 3, 2000, confirms that neutral routing through private choice does not automatically privatize restricted public value used for integrated religious instruction. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-793.pdf))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

O’Connor, joined by Scalia, Kennedy, and Thomas: Neutral allocation, public ownership, monitoring, and restrictions on religious use are sufficient absent proof of actual diversion. They would not presume that instructional materials advance religion merely because a religious school controls their ordinary secular classroom use.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Agostini v. Felton*: distinguished; its Rule 60(b)(5) holding preserved controlling precedent but did not decide the fresh Establishment Clause merits of school-controlled instructional equipment.

*Rosenberger v. Rector and Visitors of the University of Virginia*: applied and clarified; neutral eligibility does not convert direct public resources into private funds when an institution controls their religious instructional use.

*Strout v. Albanese*, decided April 3, 2000: followed; neutral allocation and private enrollment do not convert restricted public value into unrestricted private expenditure.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fifth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Chapter 2 may not operate in Jefferson Parish through the school-controlled transfer of fungible instructional resources established on this record. Publicly staffed services, tightly controlled short-term loans, genuinely student-directed secular benefits, and programs structured to prevent institutional substitution remain available.

### Stenberg v. Carhart

Argued April 25, 2000 | Decided June 28, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below:  
Nebraska’s abortion-method prohibition held unconstitutional and permanently enjoined, 192 F.3d 1142 (8th Cir. 1999). ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-914.pdf))  
Area of Law: Due process; abortion regulation; penal clarity

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Nebraska’s prohibition is unconstitutional because it lacks a health exception and reaches standard dilation and evacuation as well as the targeted intact procedure.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 6-3.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: Scalia, Kennedy, Thomas.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Breyer | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Souter, Ginsburg | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | O’Connor | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Narrow statutory and health-exception boundaries |
| Dissent | Kennedy | No other Justice | Would reverse | Narrowing-construction and medical-necessity issues |
| Dissent | Thomas | Scalia | Would reverse | Rejects the governing constitutional framework |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s application of operational undue-burden, serious-health, and penal-clarity requirements to the statutory definition and the established medical record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Breadth of the procedure ban and absence of a health exception

Holding and operative rule: A penal abortion-method prohibition is unconstitutional when its practical definition reaches the predominant previability dilation-and-evacuation method or when it lacks a usable exception for circumstances in which compelled substitution creates a material risk of death, serious physical injury, substantial irreversible impairment, or comparable grave health harm.

Reasoning: Nebraska’s definition turns on partial vaginal delivery of a living fetus or a substantial portion of one before the act causing death. In ordinary dilation and evacuation, fetal tissue may pass through the cervix and into the vaginal canal before the procedure is completed. The statutory terms and penal consequences therefore expose physicians performing standard D&E to prosecution and license revocation, notwithstanding the State’s asserted focus on a narrower intact procedure. Because D&E is the predominant second-trimester method, that practical reach creates a substantial obstacle for the patients to whom the prohibition applies. The statute independently fails because its exception is confined to preservation of life. The record establishes circumstances in which the prohibited method may materially reduce serious health risks compared with compelled alternatives. A penal law cannot require physicians to await deterioration or incur grave avoidable harm. No broader reworking of substantive due process is necessary. ([GovInfo](https://www.govinfo.gov/content/pkg/USREPORTS-530/pdf/USREPORTS-530-914.pdf))

#### Concurrences

O’Connor: The judgment rests on the statute’s practical inclusion of standard D&E and its failure to provide usable protection against serious health harm. A future statute precisely limited to a genuinely distinct procedure, accompanied by appropriate culpability and health safeguards, would require separate review.

#### Dissents

Kennedy: Nebraska’s courts should be permitted to adopt the State’s narrower construction limiting the prohibition to the intact procedure. He would also hold that the record does not establish a constitutionally necessary health exception once safe alternative methods remain available.

Thomas, joined by Scalia: The Constitution does not authorize federal courts to invalidate the State’s moral and medical judgment through the governing abortion framework. They would reverse and permit enforcement of the law.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Fargo Women’s Health Organization v. Schafer*: applied and clarified; operational undue-burden review and the practically usable serious-health exception govern penal abortion-method prohibitions.

*Planned Parenthood of Southeastern Pennsylvania v. Casey*: applied; a law reaching the predominant previability method creates a substantial obstacle for the relevant class.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the permanent injunction remains in force.

Effect: Nebraska may not enforce the challenged prohibition. The decision does not prevent enactment of a precisely defined method regulation that excludes standard D&E, includes appropriate culpability, preserves practical protection against death and serious health harm, and does not create a substantial obstacle.

### Bradley v. Arkansas Department of Education

Argued April 25, 2000 | Decided June 29, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below:  
denial of Arkansas’s sovereign-immunity defense to the IDEA claims affirmed, 189 F.3d 745 (8th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/745/526628/))  
Area of Law: Education law; state sovereign immunity; Spending Clause

Questions Presented:

1. Whether Congress unmistakably expressed an intent in the IDEA to subject States and state agencies to private suit in federal court.
2. Whether, as applied to the IDEA claims and remedies before the Court, Congress validly exercised § 5 of the Fourteenth Amendment by enforcing constitutional protections against disability discrimination and unconstitutional deprivation of educational rights.
3. In conducting that § 5 inquiry, what constitutional baseline governs and whether the IDEA’s FAPE, procedural, and remedial requirements are sufficiently fitted to preventing or remedying the identified constitutional violations rather than creating new constitutional rights.
4. If Congress did not validly abrogate immunity through § 5, whether a State knowingly and voluntarily waives sovereign immunity by accepting IDEA funds after Congress unmistakably conditions participation on amenability to private federal suit.
5. Whether that funding condition is a valid Spending Clause condition, including whether it gives sufficiently clear notice and preserves a genuinely voluntary choice.
6. If direct suit against the State remains barred, whether responsible state officials may nevertheless be sued prospectively to end an ongoing IDEA violation.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence | Stone | No other Justice | Supports affirmance | Claim-specific § 5 analysis and prospective officer enforcement |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full and authors a concurrence.  
Announced ground: Stone joins the Court’s clear-statement, funding-conditioned consent, and Spending Clause holdings. He separately concludes that § 5 permits claim-specific IDEA abrogation for conduct independently violating the Fourteenth Amendment and that properly confined prospective officer relief remains available when direct State liability is unavailable.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Clear congressional statement

Holding and operative rule: The IDEA unmistakably states that participating States and state agencies are amenable to private suit in federal court for violations of the Act.

Reasoning: Section 1403 expressly identifies States and removes immunity from federal suit, while § 1415 provides an enforceable federal judicial route for persons aggrieved by the prescribed IDEA process. Those provisions satisfy the requirement that Congress speak unmistakably before exposing States to private federal litigation. Clear intent remains distinct from constitutional authority. The text establishes the condition and identifies the contemplated forum, but does not by itself prove that § 5 abrogation is valid, that a State has consented, or that the plaintiff has established an IDEA violation. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/745/526628/))

##### Question 2: Claim-specific § 5 abrogation

Not reached; Arkansas’s valid funding-conditioned consent independently sustains the denial of sovereign immunity.

##### Question 3: Constitutional baseline and statutory fit

Not reached; resolution of the § 5 issue is unnecessary once the Court upholds the funding-conditioned consent.

##### Question 4: Funding-conditioned waiver

Holding and operative rule: A State that knowingly accepts IDEA funds after Congress unmistakably conditions participation on amenability to private federal suit consents to that program-specific federal enforcement.

Reasoning: Acceptance of federal program funds is an affirmative legal act, unlike ordinary participation in a federally regulated market. Congress placed Arkansas on clear notice that IDEA participation carried federal judicial enforcement of IDEA obligations. Arkansas then chose to accept the funds and administer the program. That conduct constitutes genuine consent rather than constructive waiver inferred from activity Congress independently regulates. Consent extends only to the clearly stated program condition and remedies authorized by the IDEA; it does not waive Arkansas’s immunity from unrelated claims. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/189/745/526628/))

##### Question 5: Spending Clause validity

Holding and operative rule: Conditioning IDEA funds on program-specific private federal enforcement is a valid Spending Clause condition on this record.

Reasoning: The condition is prospective, directly related to the federal education program, and stated before acceptance. Federal adjudication of IDEA duties provides a method of enforcing the same obligations for which the funds are offered. The record identifies no retroactive surprise, threat to unrelated existing programs, or pressure eliminating Arkansas’s genuine legal choice whether to participate. The importance and practical value of IDEA funding do not alone establish unconstitutional coercion. The Court reserves a materially different condition whose financial structure leaves a State no genuine choice.

##### Question 6: Prospective officer enforcement

Not reached; direct suit against Arkansas may proceed on the funding-conditioned consent ground.

#### Concurrences

Stone: Section 5 supports IDEA abrogation as applied to conduct that independently violates Equal Protection or Due Process, but ordinary FAPE, IEP, or procedural noncompliance does not automatically become a constitutional violation. If direct State liability is unavailable in another application, a responsible officer with a genuine enforcement connection remains subject to properly confined prospective relief for an ongoing violation.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Atascadero State Hospital v. Scanlon*: clarified; IDEA §§ 1403 and 1415 provide unmistakable notice of private federal enforcement against participating States.

*College Savings Bank v. Florida Prepaid Postsecondary Education Expense Board*: followed; acceptance of a separately valid and clearly stated funding condition is genuine consent, not constructive waiver from regulated activity.

*South Dakota v. Dole*: applied; the federal-suit condition is prospective, program related, clearly stated, and not coercive on this record.

*Pennhurst State School and Hospital v. Halderman*: applied; a State’s acceptance binds it only to conditions stated with sufficient clarity before receipt of the funds.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed, and the case is remanded for adjudication of the IDEA claims consistent with Arkansas’s program-specific consent to federal suit.

Effect: Arkansas may not obtain dismissal of the Bradley IDEA action on sovereign-immunity grounds. The Court makes no finding concerning FAPE, the IEP, the adequacy of the hearing, liability, damages, or other underlying IDEA merits. The Court does not decide the validity of categorical § 5 abrogation or the prospective officer question.

### Hawkins v. Freeman

Argued July 10, 2000 | Decided August 14, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit; judgment below:  
federal habeas relief denied, 195 F.3d 732 (4th Cir. 1999) (en banc). ([Fourth Circuit Court](https://www.ca4.uscourts.gov/opinions/967539A.P.pdf))  
Area of Law: Substantive due process; federal habeas corpus

Questions Presented:

1. Whether the State violates substantive due process by reincarcerating a person after its own final but mistaken release allowed him to live at liberty for a substantial period.
2. Whether that claim is governed by a fundamental-liberty inquiry, a reliance or fundamental-fairness rule, or the shocks-the-conscience standard applicable to executive action.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | Souter | Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |
| Concurrence in the judgment | Scalia | Thomas | Concurs in the judgment | History-and-tradition ground |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s conclusion that correction of a good-faith mistaken release, to enforce a still-valid and uncompleted sentence, does not shock the conscience on the established record.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Reincarceration after mistaken release

Holding and operative rule: Reincarceration to complete a still-valid sentence after a good-faith mistaken release does not violate substantive due process merely because the person lived successfully at liberty for a substantial period, absent bad-faith inducement, deliberate manipulation, an effort to evade a legally completed sentence, or comparably egregious circumstances. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: Hawkins possessed a serious practical liberty interest while living lawfully in the community, working, and complying with release conditions. That interest does not extinguish the concededly valid sentence or transform an unauthorized release into a permanent constitutional commutation. The Parole Commission discovered and corrected an administrative mistake after approximately 20 months. The record shows negligence and earlier misinformation, but no deliberate effort to induce irreversible reliance, no manipulation through alternating release and confinement, and no attempt to retake Hawkins after the sentence legally ended. Reincarceration undoubtedly imposed a grave disruption, yet substantive due process does not constitutionalize every harsh consequence of governmental error. State-law credit, estoppel, compensation, and procedural objections remain analytically separate. ([Fourth Circuit Court](https://www.ca4.uscourts.gov/opinions/967539A.P.pdf))

##### Question 2: Governing substantive-due-process standard

Holding and operative rule: A challenge to an executive decision correcting mistaken release is governed by the shocks-the-conscience standard, informed by history, tradition, the actor’s culpability, the existence of a valid sentence, and the practical opportunity for deliberation. No fundamental right exists to retain liberty obtained through an administrative error while a lawful sentence remains uncompleted. (joined by Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Kennedy, Souter, Ginsburg, and Breyer)

Reasoning: *County of Sacramento v. Lewis* distinguishes executive action from legislation and requires a threshold assessment of egregious arbitrariness. Because officials had time to investigate and deliberate, intentional abuse, bad faith, or comparable misuse of authority would carry substantial weight. *Washington v. Glucksberg* separately requires a careful description of any asserted fundamental right. History does not establish a deeply rooted right to avoid completion of a valid sentence solely because officials mistakenly released the prisoner for a period. Successful adjustment and reasonable reliance remain relevant to whether correction of the error is conscience shocking, but they do not create an automatic entitlement or a fixed limitations period. The approximately 20-month good-faith correction here does not cross the constitutional threshold.

#### Concurrences

Scalia, joined by Thomas: History and tradition recognize no fundamental right to retain freedom obtained through an unauthorized release while a valid sentence remains outstanding. They would affirm on that ground without employing an independent shocks-the-conscience inquiry.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*County of Sacramento v. Lewis*: applied and clarified; correction of an executive release error is assessed for conscience-shocking arbitrariness in light of deliberative opportunity, culpability, and traditional practice.

*Washington v. Glucksberg*: applied; there is no deeply rooted fundamental right to retain liberty obtained through administrative mistake while a lawful sentence remains uncompleted.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Fourth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: Hawkins remains subject to the uncompleted sentence, and federal habeas relief is denied on the substantive-due-process theory before the Court. The decision does not resolve state-law sentence credit, estoppel, compensation, defects in the retaking procedure, a much longer state-caused delay, deliberate manipulation, or custody sought after a sentence has legally ended.

### Henerey v. City of St. Charles, School District

Argued September 5, 2000 | Decided September 25, 2000  
On writ of certiorari to the United States Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit; judgment below:  
summary judgment for the school district and officials affirmed, 200 F.3d 1128 (8th Cir. 1999). ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/200/1128/521594/))  
Area of Law: First Amendment; student speech; public education

Questions Presented:

1. Whether speech in a school-sponsored student-government election is governed by the substantial-disruption rule applicable to independent student speech or the pedagogical-control rule applicable to school-sponsored speech.
2. Whether a public school may disqualify a student candidate for violating viewpoint-neutral campaign rules reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical concerns.

#### Judgment

Affirmed, 9-0.  
Supporting: Stone, Stevens, O’Connor, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer.  
Opposing: No Justice.

#### Opinions

| Writing | Author | Joined by | Relationship to judgment | Scope joined |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Opinion of the Court | O’Connor | Stone, Stevens, Scalia, Kennedy, Souter, Thomas, Ginsburg, Breyer | Supports affirmance | Complete opinion |

#### Justice Stone's Position

Vote: to affirm.  
Public role: joins the Opinion of the Court in full.  
Announced ground: he announces no additional ground beyond the Court’s classification of the student-government campaign as school-sponsored expression and its enforcement of clear, viewpoint-neutral rules reasonably related to legitimate pedagogical objectives.

#### Decision of the Court

##### Question 1: Standard governing school-sponsored election speech

Holding and operative rule: Speech within a school-created student-government election, conducted under school rules and presented as part of the school’s civic program, is governed primarily by *Hazelwood* rather than *Tinker*. Independent student advocacy outside the school-sponsored campaign remains governed by ordinary student-speech doctrine.

Reasoning: The school created the student council, defined eligibility, scheduled and administered the election, restricted participation to enrolled students, required candidates to accept campaign conditions, and retained prior-approval authority over campaign materials distributed through the school. It did not open the election as a public forum for indiscriminate expression. Campaign speech in that setting could reasonably be understood as part of a school-supervised civic exercise and therefore bore the school’s institutional imprimatur to a degree independent hallway advocacy would not. *Tinker* continues to protect personal expression that merely occurs at school, subject to its disruption rule. It does not displace the school’s authority over expression integrated into a sponsored curricular or extracurricular program. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/200/1128/521594/))

##### Question 2: Enforcement of viewpoint-neutral campaign rules

Holding and operative rule: A public school may enforce clear, viewpoint-neutral campaign rules reasonably related to fair procedure, equal campaign access, responsible election administration, and the educational purposes of student government, including disqualification for a material violation.

Reasoning: Henerey received notice that campaign materials required advance approval and agreed to comply with school rules. He distributed unapproved condoms bearing his campaign slogan in school hallways on election day and was disqualified for bypassing the approval requirement. The rule applied to campaign materials regardless of viewpoint, and the record does not establish that officials permitted comparable unapproved materials from favored candidates or acted because of Henerey’s political position. Teaching candidates to follow announced procedures and preserving equal campaign conditions are legitimate pedagogical objectives. Disqualification was a permissible response to a material election-rule violation. The decision does not validate suppression based on mere offense, audience hostility, or disagreement with a candidate’s views. ([Justia Law](https://law.justia.com/cases/federal/appellate-courts/F3/200/1128/521594/))

#### Concurrences

No concurrences filed.

#### Dissents

No dissents filed.

#### Doctrinal Effect

*Hazelwood School District v. Kuhlmeier*: extended and clarified; its pedagogical-control rule governs a school-created and school-administered student-government election that has not been opened as a public forum.

*Tinker v. Des Moines Independent Community School District*: distinguished; its substantial-disruption rule continues to govern independent student expression outside the sponsored campaign rather than campaign activity operating under the school’s institutional auspices.

#### Ruling Beyond the Court

Mandate: The judgment of the Court of Appeals for the Eighth Circuit is affirmed.

Effect: The summary judgment for the school district and officials remains in force, and Henerey’s disqualification survives the First Amendment challenge. Schools may administer sponsored student elections through clear, viewpoint-neutral pedagogical rules, but may not use those rules as a pretext for viewpoint discrimination, punish independent off-program advocacy under the sponsored-speech standard, or rely on audience hostility alone.
